Learning to Reason with Curriculum II: Compositional Generalization Thanks: Microsoft Research, New York, NY. schapire@microsoft.com
Nived Rajaraman, Audrey Huang, Miroslav Dudík, Dylan Foster, Akshay Krishnamurthy

Abstract
Compositional generalization—the ability to solve complex problems by combining solutions to simpler sub-problems—is a fundamental capability of both natural and artificial intelligence, and a key mechanism underlying chain-of-thought reasoning. However, the theoretical underpinnings of compositional generalization remain poorly understood: when and why does decomposing a problem into parts yield more efficient learning than solving it directly? We study this question through the canonical problem of learning to simulate semiautomata (predicting the outcome of T T steps of sequential computation), a model that captures state tracking, regular language recognition, and modular arithmetic. We show that an autocurriculum-based approach building on Part I of this series—recursively decomposing longer sequences into shorter sub-problems, learning to solve them, and composing the solutions—achieves dramatically better statistical complexity than direct methods. (i) For a setting inspired by supervised fine-tuning (SFT) where the learner receives interactive feedback on intermediate states of the computation, curriculum facilitates learning from only 2 𝒪 ~ ​ ( log ⁡ T ) 2^{\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\sqrt{\log T})} tokens of supervision; i.e., subpolynomial in the sequence length T T , overcoming the Ω ⁡ ( T ) \Omega(T) token barrier required by direct simulation. (ii) For a setting inspired by reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR), where the learner improves a pre-trained reference model using an outcome verifier, we show that curriculum reduces the requirement on the reference model from coverage at the full sequence length T T to coverage at a shorter block length B ≪ T B\ll T , an exponentially weaker condition.



Contents

1
Introduction

Chain-of-thought reasoning, where models expend additional computation by producing intermediate reasoning tokens prior to a final answer, has driven significant advances in the capabilities of language models. A key mechanism underlying these advances is
compositional generalization
—the ability to combine solutions to simpler sub-problems in order to solve harder ones—with a growing body of evidence that it is central to how reasoning models scale to difficult tasks
(
32
;
42
;
39
;
13
;
53
)
.

Many works emphasize the importance of
curriculum-based training

(
7
)
for enabling compositional generalization in reasoning models
(
51
;
36
;
47
)
. Here, problems of increasing difficulty teach the learner increasingly complex compositions of low-level skills.
Recent work has further demonstrated that curricula can be induced by the learner itself, avoiding the need for hand-engineered notions of problem difficulty
(
10
;
45
;
50
;
52
)
.

While these
autocurriculum
approaches are intuitively appealing, it remains poorly understood as to
when
,
why
, and
to what extent
autocurricula can reduce the cost of learning, and what principles should guide their design. To this end, in Part I of this series
(
40
)
, we showed that a form of autocurriculum inspired by boosting—specifically, using the model’s own performance to
select which problems to focus on
from a large corpus—provably improves learning efficiency for supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning. In this work, we ask a more ambitious question:

How should a learner
design its own problems
to facilitate solving tasks far beyond its base capabilities, and how can compositional structure guide this process?

s_{T}

w_{2}
\cdots

s_{t}=\phi(A(w_{t})s_{t-1}+B(w_{t})w_{t})

s_{t}=\phi_{\text{loop}}(s_{t-1})

:
Model overview.
Left:
semiautomata dynamics under an unknown transition function
\pi^{\star}
.

1.1
Contributions

We study these questions through the task of
semiautomaton simulation
—learning to predict the outcome of
T
steps of sequential computation—a canonical model that captures state tracking, regular language recognition, and modular arithmetic. A semiautomaton is defined by an unknown transition function
\pi^{\star}:S\times\Sigma\to S
that updates a state via
s_{t}\leftarrow\pi^{\star}(s_{t-1},w_{t})
, where
\bm{w}=\bm{w}_{1:T}
is an input word. The intermediate states
s_{1:T}
constitute a chain-of-thought, and the goal is to predict the final state
s_{T}
after
T
steps. In the language modeling context, one can think of states as tokens, but they can be more general (say, a block of
k
tokens, corresponding to a fixed-size attention window). The Markovian structure of semiautomata means that models solving shorter instances can be
composed
to solve longer ones, making this a natural testbed for understanding how curriculum can enable compositional generalization.
1
1

1

This model can be viewed as a Markovian restriction to the chain-of-thought learning framework of
29
;
40
, which allows for arbitrary dependence on previous states.

Our main results—building on Part I
(
40
)
—show that a self-generated compositional curriculum, where the learner adaptively decomposes long problems into shorter sub-problems, yields superpolynomial reductions in supervision and computational cost relative to non-curriculum baselines, in both supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR).

Section

3
: Fine-tuning with interactive CoT supervision (iSFT).

Our first set of results considers a setting in which the learner has interactive access to the semiautomata
\pi^{\star}
of interest and aims to select a set of state transitions on which to fine-tune a model with next-token prediction (concretely, given any word
\bm{w}
and step
t
, the learner can ask for the state
s_{t}
, at one unit of cost). The standard approach would be to generate full reasoning traces and train on every intermediate state, but can we do better by deliberately choosing
which
states to supervise? We show that the answer is yes. We give an autocurriculum-based algorithm,
AutoLearn
(
Algorithm

2
), which interactively selects which intermediate states to label by constructing a curriculum of shorter sub-problems and composing them. The resulting query complexity—the number of state labels requested—is
t^{2^{2}_{2}}

2^{\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\sqrt{\log T})}
, which is
subpolynomial
in the sequence length
T
. This overcomes the
\Omega(T)
barrier inherent to direct simulation, where every intermediate state must be labeled.

Section

4
: Fine-tuning a reference model (RLVR).

Our second set of results considers a setting inspired by reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR)
(
33
)
. Here, the learner has access to a pre-trained
reference model

\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
that can correctly solve short instances of length
B\ll T
with constant probability, as measured by a coverage coefficient
(
55
;
46
)
, along with an
outcome verifier
that checks correctness of guesses for the terminal state. The reference model’s coverage at the full sequence length
T
may be exponentially worse than at length
B
, so directly applying prior RL methods
(
40
)
at the length-
T
scale is prohibitively expensive. We give an algorithm,
\texttt{AutoLearn}.\texttt{RL}
, which uses autocurriculum to compose short-range predictions into full-length solutions, reducing the requirement on
\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
from coverage over full sequences of length
T
to coverage at the shorter block scale
B
—an exponentially weaker condition.
This is enabled by a
coverage expansion
phenomenon: curriculum enables the reference model to incrementally build coverage over harder problems, starting from the short length-
B
instances it can already solve.

1.2
Organization

Section

2
formally introduces semiautomata and our interactive learning protocols for fine-tuning and RL, then provides a high-level overview of our main results.
Section

3
and
Section

4
then give a detailed presentation of our main algorithms and results for iSFT and reinforcement learning, respectively, and
Section

5
gives an overview of the key technical challenges and analysis ideas behind the recursion used in both algorithms. We conclude with open questions and future directions in
Section

6
. All proofs and supporting results are deferred to the appendix.

2
Problem Setting and Overview of Results

This section formalizes the two learning settings we study—supervised fine-tuning with interactive feedback (iSFT) and reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR)—and presents informal statements of our main results. We begin with preliminaries on semiautomata (
Section

2.1
), then introduce the iSFT setting and results (
Sections

2.2
and
2.3
), and finally introduce the RLVR setting and results (
Sections

2.4
and
2.5
).

2.1
Preliminaries: Semiautomata and Chain-of-Thought Learning

We focus on learning to generate high-quality chains of thought, through direct supervision or reinforcement learning with verifier feedback. To understand how curriculum and composition can reduce the cost of learning, we adopt
semiautomata
as a stylized yet rich model of chain-of-thought reasoning.

Definition 2.1

(Semiautomaton simulation)
.

A semiautomaton is defined by a transition function
\pi:S\times\Sigma\to S
over a state space
S
and input alphabet
\Sigma
. An instance
{\bm{x}}=(s_{0},\bm{w}_{1:t})
is composed of an initial state
s_{0}\in S
and a word
\bm{w}_{1:t}\in\Sigma^{t}
for some
t\in\mathbb{N}
, and
t
is the length of the instance
2
2

2

For a vector
\bm{w}
,
\bm{w}_{1:t}
indicates the vector obtained by slicing to length
t
, while
w_{t}
(non-bold) indicates the
t^{\text{th}}
coordinate.
. Words correspond to the input strings that we want the model to process, while the initial state
s_{0}
represents the starting point of the computation. The overall instance
{\bm{x}}
can be interpreted as a prompt in the language modeling context. The semiautomaton simulator
\pi_{t}({\bm{x}})
returns the terminal state reached after applying
\pi
iteratively for
t
steps on
{\bm{x}}
.
3
3

3

We consider the
parameter-sharing regime
where transition functions are time invariant.
Namely
\pi_{t}({\bm{x}})=s_{t}
, where
\forall 1\leqslant t^{\prime}\leqslant t,\ s_{t^{\prime}}\leftarrow\pi(s_{t^{\prime}-1},w_{t^{\prime}})
. We will use
\pi_{1:t}({\bm{x}})
to denote the full sequence of states
(s_{1},\cdots,s_{t})\in S^{t}
generated by this process, which is referred to as the chain-of-thought (CoT).
4
4

4

The notations
\pi_{t^{\prime}}({\bm{x}})
and
\pi_{1:t^{\prime}}({\bm{x}})
extend to
t^{\prime}<t
by truncating
{\bm{x}}=(s_{0},\bm{w}_{1:t})
to
{\bm{x}}^{\prime}=(s_{0},\bm{w}_{1:t^{\prime}})
and returning
\pi_{t^{\prime}}({\bm{x}}^{\prime})
or
\pi_{1:t^{\prime}}({\bm{x}}^{\prime})
.

We use the term ‘‘simulation’’ to reflect that the objective is to predict the final state (outcome) of the semiautomaton, not specifically to learn the transition function or full CoT (except as a means to predict the outcome). In the language modeling context, the state itself can be a single token, but our setting is more general and allows for more complex state representations (e.g., blocks of tokens).
5
5

5

Going forward, we will often use the term “state” and “token” interchangeably when it is clear from context.

The key structural property of semiautomata is that the transition function
\pi^{\star}
is Markovian and stationary. That is, it is applied identically at every step, and the state
s_{t}
is a sufficient summary of the computation history up to time
t
. This means that any contiguous chunk of a length-
T
computation can be interpreted as a self-contained computation of shorter length
t\ll T
, and models trained to solve shorter instances can be
composed
to solve longer ones. This structure makes semiautomata a natural testbed studying how curriculum, composition, and self-generated sub-problems interact. This refines the setup from Part I
(
40
)
, which considered general, potentially non-Markovian transition functions. Note that the Markovian structure is precisely why we allow a state to encode more than a single token: at the granularity of individual tokens, the Markovian assumption would be severe (language models attend to their entire history) whereas letting
s_{t}
summarize a block of tokens (such as a fixed attention window) keeps the Markovian condition milder and more realistic.

2.1.1
Examples of semiautomata

Beyond serving as a theoretical testbed, semiautomata capture a broad range of sequential computation tasks, including settings where the state space
S
and input alphabet
\Sigma
can be very large, necessitating function approximation. We discuss a few examples below.

1.

Modular arithmetic and group products.
Let
(G,\cdot)
be a finite group with
|G|=k
. The corresponding semiautomaton has state space
S=G
, input alphabet
\Sigma=G
, and transition function
\pi(s,w)=s\cdot w
. For an input word
\bm{w}
, simulating the semiautomaton for
T
steps computes the group product
s_{0}\cdot w_{1}\cdot w_{2}\cdots w_{T}
. For the case where
G=\mathbb{Z}/k\mathbb{Z}
under addition, this reduces to computing modular sums. As a more interesting example, when
G=S_{5}
(the symmetric group on
5
elements), iterated group multiplication can simulate any
\textsf{NC}^{1}
circuit by Barrington’s theorem
(
5
)
. The class
\Pi
of all valid group multiplication tables on
[k]
has Natarajan dimension at most
\mathrm{Ndim}(\Pi)\leqslant\mathcal{O}(k^{2}\log k)
.

2.

Linear recurrences over finite fields.
Consider the semiautomaton with state space
S=\mathbb{F}_{q}^{d}
, input alphabet
\Sigma=\mathbb{F}_{q}^{d}
, and transition function
\pi(s,w)=As+Bw
for unknown matrices
A\in\mathbb{F}_{q}^{d\times d}
and
B\in\mathbb{F}_{q}^{d\times d}
. Simulating for
T
steps computes the linear recurrence
s_{T}=A^{T}s_{0}+\sum_{t=1}^{T}A^{T-t}Bw_{t}
. The class
\Pi
of all such linear transitions is parameterized by the pair
(A,B)
, giving
|\Pi|=q^{2d^{2}}
, and Natarajan dimension
\mathrm{Ndim}(\Pi)\leqslant\mathcal{O}(d^{2}\log q)
which is exponentially smaller than the number of states,
|S|=q^{d}
.

3.

Regular languages.
A regular language over an alphabet
\Sigma
is the set of strings accepted by some Deterministic Finite Automaton (DFA), where input words are arbitrary strings in
\Sigma^{*}
. These correspond to strings that match a regular expression built from alphabet symbols, concatenation, union (
|
), and Kleene star (
∗
). The
alphabetic length

k
—specifically, a regular expression has length
k
if it is built from
k
alphabet symbols, counted with multiplicity
6
6

6

Other related notions of length, such as the ordinary length and the reverse polish length, are upper bounded by a constant factor times the alphabetic length
(
23
)
.
—is a natural complexity measure for regular languages. In particular, by Glushkov’s construction
(
22
)
and the powerset constructions, a regular language with alphabetic length
k
can be decided by a DFA with at most
2^{k+1}
states. In
Section

A.1
, we give a concrete example of a regular expression and describe the induced semiautomaton.

2.1.2
Modeling and function approximation

To allow for generalization when the state and input alphabet are large, we assume access to a
model class

\Pi\subseteq\{S\times\Sigma\to S\}
of candidate transition functions (equivalently, “next-state/token predictors”) containing the unknown transition function
\pi^{\star}
. This class can represent any parameterized model family (e.g., transformers or state-space models). As a computational primitive, our algorithms make use of
next-state/token prediction
(ERM on the next-state/token prediction loss), but otherwise access the model class only implicitly.

Definition 2.2

(Next-token prediction oracle for
\Pi
)
.

Given any realizable dataset
D=\{(s_{i},w_{i})\mapsto\pi^{\star}(s_{i},w_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
of input-next-state pairs, a next-state/token prediction oracle for
\Pi
,
\texttt{NTP}(D)
, returns
\widehat{\pi}\in\Pi
that minimizes the next-state/token prediction loss:
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{I}(\widehat{\pi}(s_{i},w_{i})\neq\pi^{\star}(s_{i},w_{i}))=0
.
7
7

7

Since semiautomata are deterministic, we work with the
0
-
1
loss. For general (stochastic) language modeling settings, standard next-token prediction corresponds to ERM under the logarithmic loss.

Many of our results make use of the Natarajan dimension of
\Pi
, a complexity measure that characterizes the distribution-free sample complexity of learning
\Pi
from i.i.d. examples (up to logarithmic factors).

Definition 2.3

(Natarajan dimension of
\Pi

(
37
)
)
.

The Natarajan dimension of a class of models
\Pi
is the largest
d\in\mathbb{N}
such that there exist instances
\{(s_{i},w_{i})\}_{i=1}^{d}\subseteq S\times\Sigma
and pairs of labels
\{y_{i},y_{i}^{\prime}\}_{i=1}^{d}\subseteq S\times S
satisfying
y_{i}\neq y_{i}^{\prime}
for all
i\in[d]
, with the following property: for every subset
I\subseteq[d]
, there exists a model
\pi_{I}\in\Pi
such that
\forall i\in I,\ \pi_{I}(s_{i},w_{i})=y_{i}
and
\forall i\not\in I,\ \pi_{I}(s_{i},w_{i})=y_{i}^{\prime}
.

Remark 2.4

(Simplifications in the semiautomaton framework)
.

While our results are motivated by language model reasoning, our problem setting makes two fairly strong simplifications: (1) We restrict the learner to Markovian models, which rules out classes like Transformers that can attend to the full history. (2) We consider a deterministic setting, which rules out stochastic models. Regarding the first point, we note that while this may seem like a strong simplification when we interpret states
s_{i}
as tokens, our guarantees allow for general, potentially large state spaces, which makes the framework quite rich nonetheless. For example,
s_{i}
may represent a block of tokens of length
k
, in which case our model allows for fixed-size attention windows of size
k
. Relaxing both limitations is an important direction for future work, but we believe our framework is still a powerful setting to explore the role of composition.

2.2
Setting I: Learning from Interactive Chain-of-Thought Supervision (iSFT)

Recall from
Section

2.1
that the learner’s goal is to predict the terminal state
\pi^{\star}_{T}({\bm{x}})
reached by a ground-truth semiautomaton
\pi^{\star}
on length-
T
instances
{\bm{x}}\sim\rho
. Standard supervised approaches fix in advance
what
supervision to collect
(
29
)
: either label the full chain-of-thought
\pi^{\star}_{1:T}({\bm{x}})
on each instance and fit a model to its next-state pairs via next-token prediction, or learn
end-to-end
from the terminal state
\pi^{\star}_{T}({\bm{x}})
alone. Our first setting considers a more flexible model—
learning from interactive chain-of-thought supervision
, or
iSFT
—in which the learner instead chooses
which
states of the chain-of-thought to supervise, querying the state reached at any step
t
at one unit of cost. We formalize this interactive access through the oracle below.

Definition 2.5

(Interactive chain-of-thought oracle)
.

Let
\pi^{\star}
denote the ground-truth semiautomaton. An interactive chain-of-thought oracle,
\texttt{iCoT}(\cdot)
, is a function which takes as input an instance
{\bm{x}}=(s_{0},\bm{w}_{1:t})
of any length
t\in\mathbb{N}
and returns the chain-of-thought state
\pi^{\star}_{t}({\bm{x}})
at step
t
.

Two interpretations of this oracle will be useful. First, when applied to a length-
t
instance,
\texttt{iCoT}({\bm{x}})
returns that instance’s
terminal state
: the outcome of running
\pi^{\star}
for
t
steps. Second, when applied to a length-
t

prefix
of a longer length-
T
instance, the same query returns the
intermediate state

\pi^{\star}_{t}({\bm{x}})
that the computation passes through at step
t
. Thus, by querying prefixes, the learner can recover any state along the chain-of-thought of a long instance.

Problem 2.6

(Learning from Interactive Chain-of-Thought Supervision: iSFT)
.

The learner is given a class
\Pi\subseteq\{S\times\Sigma\to S\}
of next-state/token predictors containing the true semiautomaton
\pi^{\star}
, along with sampling access to a target distribution
\rho\in\Delta_{S\times\Sigma^{T}}
of length-
T
instances. The objective is to learn a model
\widehat{\pi}:S\times\Sigma^{T}\to S
such that with probability at least
1-\delta
, the terminal state prediction error satisfies
\mathrm{Pr}_{{\bm{x}}\sim\rho}\big[\widehat{\pi}({\bm{x}})\neq\pi^{\star}_{T}({\bm{x}})\big]\leqslant\varepsilon
. To do so, the learner may query the interactive chain-of-thought oracle
\texttt{iCoT}(\cdot)
of
Definition

2.5
on instances of any length. The learner is evaluated according to the following desiderata:

1.

Query complexity: The number of calls
n_{\texttt{query}}
made to the interactive chain-of-thought oracle
\texttt{iCoT}(\cdot)
.

2.

Sample complexity: The number of instances
n_{\texttt{sample}}
drawn from
\rho
.

Note that in the iSFT setting, the learner can recover both standard forms of supervision (since
\texttt{iCoT}(\cdot)
accepts instances of any length):
T
prefix queries suffice to reconstruct an instance’s full chain-of-thought (for next-token prediction
(
15
;
29
)
), and a single query suffices to obtain the end-to-end outcome. The limitation of both approaches is that they incur
\Omega(T)
query complexity
(
29
)
. The real leverage of the iSFT framework comes from the Markovian structure of
\pi^{\star}
(
Section

2.1
), which lets the learner
construct its own sub-problems
: splitting a length-
T
instance at chosen boundaries and labeling the boundary states with
\texttt{iCoT}(\cdot)
decomposes it into shorter instances, the terminal state of each becoming the start state of the next. In what follows, we will show that this form of self-generated supervision can be used to break the
\Omega(T)
query complexity barrier faced by the approaches above.

Motivation for the iSFT interaction model.

We recognize that at first glance, the iSFT setting may seem somewhat contrived or artificial. One might expect that in general, labeling an intermediate state
\pi^{\star}_{t}({\bm{x}})
should be no easier than labeling the entire intermediate CoT
\pi^{\star}_{1:t}({\bm{x}})
, yet our framework counts the former as a single unit of cost and counts the latter as
t
units of cost. There are two reasons why we believe the model is useful:

•

First, for many natural semiautomata, labeling a single state
is
cheaper than producing an entire chain-of-thought. We observe that for semiautomata including modular arithmetic, Dyck languages, and linear recurrences (
Appendix

A
), the state at step
t
can be computed in parallel in
\mathcal{O}(\log t)
time by divide-and-conquer over matrix or group products, whereas simulating
\pi^{\star}
step by step to obtain the full length-
t
chain-of-thought takes
\Omega(t)
time. Thus, a single state query is a natural unit of cost here.

•

Second, the iSFT model underpins our reinforcement learning results in the sequel (
Section

4
). There, we show that one can use a weak reference model and outcome verifier to “simulate”
iCoT
, allowing the curriculum and composition machinery we develop here to carry over directly.

2.3
Overview of Results for iSFT Setting

Our main result shows that, by querying the interactive chain-of-thought oracle to assemble a self-generated curriculum of shorter instances, semiautomata can be learned in the iSFT setting with sample and query complexity scaling
subpolynomially
in the sequence length
T
. Our algorithm
AutoLearn
(
Algorithm

2
) thereby breaks the
\Omega(T)
barrier faced by non-curriculum approaches.

Theorem 2.7

(Main result for iSFT setting (informal; see
Theorem

3.4
))
.

AutoLearn
(
Algorithm

2
) learns a model with
\mathrm{Pr}_{{\bm{x}}\sim\rho}\big[\widehat{\pi}({\bm{x}})\neq\pi^{\star}_{T}({\bm{x}})\big]\leqslant\varepsilon
with probability at least
1-\delta
in the iSFT setting, and is computationally efficient in terms of a next-token prediction oracle for
\Pi
(
Definition

2.2
). The sample and query complexity used by the algorithm are upper bounded by:

n_{\texttt{sample}},n_{\texttt{query}}\leqslant 2^{\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\big(\sqrt{\log(T)}\big)}\cdot\left[\frac{d\log(|S|)\log^{2}(\frac{1}{\varepsilon})\log(\frac{1}{\delta})}{\varepsilon}\right].

Here,
d\leqslant\log_{2}(|\Pi|)
is the Natarajan dimension of
\Pi
(
Definition

2.3
).

AutoLearn
(
Algorithm

2
) builds this curriculum over the course of training by exploiting
composition
: since
\pi^{\star}
is Markovian, a model that simulates
\pi^{\star}
accurately for
\tau
steps (under a suitable mixture over intermediate states) can be composed with itself to simulate
\pi^{\star}
for
T\gg\tau
steps. The catch is that errors accumulate under composition, so the short-range accuracy must be driven high enough to tolerate them.
AutoLearn
resolves this with a
multiscale
variant of boosting
(
43
)
that recursively interleaves two operations:
(a)
boosting, which aggregates several short-range models to drive their error down, and
(b)
composition, which stitches the boosted models into longer-range ones with degraded guarantees. The central difficulty, treated at length in
Sections

3
and
5
, is to turn samples from the target distribution
\rho
into samples from the distribution over short sub-problems required by boosting, without inflating the sample complexity. The only learning primitive
AutoLearn
invokes is next-token prediction (
Definition

2.2
) on i.i.d. next-state data; in fact, this guarantee is a
reduction
—any weak learner with low next-token-prediction error, not only ERM, can be substituted (
Section

3
).

Comparison with non-curriculum baselines.

The subpolynomial rate in
Theorem

2.7
stands in sharp contrast with guarantees achievable by natural non-curriculum approaches.

•

Learning from full chain-of-thought:
A full chain-of-thought learner labels every intermediate state of
\pi^{\star}
on each training instance and fits a model to the resulting next-state pairs by next-token prediction. This is computationally efficient—it reduces to next-token prediction over
\Pi
—but labels all
T
states per instance, so its query complexity grows linearly in
T
. We show this is unavoidable: any such learner needs
\Omega(dT)
queries to reach constant error, regardless of which instances are queried (
Proposition

3.7
).

•

Learning from end-to-end feedback:

An end-to-end learner queries only the terminal state
\pi^{\star}_{T}({\bm{x}})
on length-
T
instances.
29
show its statistical complexity scales as
n_{\texttt{sample}}=n_{\texttt{query}}=\Omega(dT)
, as the generalization error can be
\Omega(T)
times larger than that of the base class
\Pi
(
Proposition

3.9
). This approach is also computationally intractable under standard cryptographic assumptions
(
30
)
, as semiautomata can encode branching programs
(
5
)
.

We give the formal lower bounds and a detailed comparison in
Section

3.4
.

2.4
Setting II: Improving a Weak Reference Model from Verifier Feedback (RLVR)

Our second setting is inspired by reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR), and models the post-training of a pretrained model. The learner is given a
reference model

\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
(a weak generator of candidate solutions) and an
outcome verifier
that checks whether a guessed terminal state is correct, and aims to improve the accuracy of
\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
for the task of simulating the final semiautomaton state output
\pi^{\star}_{T}
. When
\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
already has nontrivial
coverage
of correct length-
T
solutions, this is well understood, and standard RLVR fine-tuning succeeds
(
16
;
40
)
. We instead study a harder regime in which
\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
is competent only at a much shorter block length
B\ll T
—that is, it solves length-
B
instances with constant probability, but is exponentially unlikely to solve length-
T
instances directly—and ask how to
compose
this short-horizon competence into full-length solutions.

We begin by formalizing the verifier and learning problem, then formally state our assumption on
\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
.

Definition 2.8

(Outcome verifier)
.

An outcome verifier
\mathcal{V}
takes in an instance
{\bm{x}}\in S\times\Sigma^{t}
of length
t\leqslant T
, along with a guess
s\in S
for the terminal state
\pi^{\star}_{t}({\bm{x}})
, and returns a reward
\mathbb{I}\big(s=\pi^{\star}_{t}({\bm{x}})\big)
.

The verifier is much weaker than the interactive chain-of-thought oracle of
Definition

2.5
. Instead of returning the correct state outright, it gives only binary feedback on a guess, and is uninformative when the guess is wrong (especially when
|S|
is large). We will use two equivalent interpretations for the verifier: (1) When applied to a length-
t
instance and a guess
s
,
\mathcal{V}({\bm{x}},s)
tests whether
s
is that instance’s
terminal state
: the outcome of running
\pi^{\star}
for
t
steps; (2) When applied to a length-
t

prefix
of a longer length-
T
instance (interpreted as a new length-
t
instance), the same query tests whether
s
is the
intermediate state

\pi^{\star}_{t}({\bm{x}})
that the computation passes through at step
t
. With this in hand, we state the learning problem.

Problem 2.9

(RL setting)
.

The learner is given a class
\Pi\subseteq\{S\times\Sigma\to S\}
of next-state/token predictors containing the true semiautomaton
\pi^{\star}
, along with sampling access to a target distribution
\rho
over length-
T
instances. The objective is to learn a model
\widehat{\pi}:S\times\Sigma^{T}\to S
such that with probability at least
1-\delta
, the terminal state prediction error satisfies
\mathrm{Pr}_{{\bm{x}}\sim\rho}\big[\widehat{\pi}({\bm{x}})\neq\pi^{\star}_{T}({\bm{x}})\big]\leqslant\varepsilon
. To do so, the learner has access to a reference model
\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}:S\times\Sigma\to\Delta_{S}
and an outcome verifier
\mathcal{V}
(
Definition

2.8
). The learner is evaluated according to the following desiderata:

1.

Sample complexity: The number of instances
n_{\texttt{sample}}
drawn from
\rho
.

2.

Query complexity: The number of calls
n_{\texttt{query}}
made to the verifier
\mathcal{V}
.

3.

Computational cost: The number of state transitions
n_{\texttt{comp}}
generated from
\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
or another model in
\Pi
over the course of learning.

Remark 2.10
.

The measure of computational cost we consider focuses on generation costs over the course of training, and ignores other sources of cost for the sake of simplicity. This is inspired by RLVR in practice where the dominant cost of training is autoregressive generation; model updates constitute a lower order cost
(
26
)
.

Reference model and coverage.

We formalize the competence of the reference model
\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
through its
coverage
—the probability it assigns to a correct short solution—over blocks of size
B\ll T
.

Definition 2.11

(Block-level coverage)
.

For a block length
B\in\mathbb{N}
and constants
C_{\texttt{seq}}^{(B)}\geqslant C_{\texttt{ans}}^{(B)}\geqslant 1
, the reference model
\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
satisfies the following guarantees:

\inf_{{\bm{x}}\in S\times\Sigma^{B}}\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}_{1:B}(\bm{y}^{\star}|{\bm{x}})\geqslant\frac{1}{C_{\texttt{seq}}^{(B)}},\text{ and }\inf_{{\bm{x}}\in S\times\Sigma^{B}}\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}_{B}(y^{\star}_{B}|{\bm{x}})\geqslant\frac{1}{C_{\texttt{ans}}^{(B)}}.

(1)

where
\bm{y}^{\star}:=\pi^{\star}_{1:B}({\bm{x}})
. We refer to
C_{\texttt{ans}}^{(B)}
as the outcome coverage coefficient at length
B
, and
C_{\texttt{seq}}^{(B)}
as the sequence-level coverage coefficient at length
B
.

The two coefficients capture different notions of block-level success:
C_{\texttt{ans}}^{(B)}
is the probability that
\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
produces the correct terminal state of a length-
B
instance (potentially through an incorrect CoT), and
C_{\texttt{seq}}^{(B)}
is the probability that it produces the
entire
length-
B
CoT correctly. We always have
C_{\texttt{ans}}^{(B)}\leqslant C_{\texttt{seq}}^{(B)}
, but using both coefficients will allow us to state more refined guarantees

Coverage assumptions of this kind are standard in the analysis of reinforcement learning and inference-time methods
(
49
;
46
;
28
;
27
;
16
;
9
)
, formalizing the idea that pretraining endows
\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
with a weak but nonzero probability of producing correct reasoning traces. While typically RLVR algorithms scale with coverage
\smash{C_{\texttt{seq}}^{(T)}}
at the full sequence level
T
, our definition assumes competence at length
B\ll T
only. Rolling
\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
out across all
T/B
blocks of a length-
T
instance succeeds with probability exponentially small in
T/B
, so naive RLVR at the target length is hopeless (indeed, extrapolating from short to long reasoning is a notoriously difficult form of out-of-distribution generalization
(
4
;
54
)
). This raises the question: can we exploit composition to
expand

\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
’s coverage from length
B
to length
T
, avoiding exponential blowup in cost?

2.5
Overview of Results for RLVR Setting

We show that self-generated curriculum makes
coverage expansion
possible. Our main algorithm,
\texttt{AutoLearn}.\texttt{RL}
(
Algorithm

6
), bootstraps
\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
from block-level competence to high accuracy at the full length
T
, with all costs governed by
\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
’s coverage at the short block scale
B
.
\texttt{AutoLearn}.\texttt{RL}
is a reinforcement learning counterpart to
AutoLearn
, following the same composition/boosting template but replacing the chain-of-thought oracle with verifier feedback.

Theorem 2.12

(Main result for RLVR setting (informal; see
Theorem

4.2
))
.

\texttt{AutoLearn}.\texttt{RL}
(
Algorithm

6
) learns a model with
\mathrm{Pr}_{{\bm{x}}\sim\rho}\big[\widehat{\pi}({\bm{x}})\neq\pi^{\star}_{T}({\bm{x}})\big]\leqslant\varepsilon
with probability at least
1-\delta
in the RLVR setting, and is computationally efficient in terms of a next-token prediction oracle for
\Pi
(
Definition

2.2
). The sample complexity to achieve this guarantee is bounded by
n_{\texttt{sample}}\leqslant\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon}\right)
, and the query complexity (number of calls to the outcome verifier) is bounded by:

\displaystyle n_{\texttt{query}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon}\right)+\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{T}{B}\cdot dC_{\texttt{ans}}^{(B)}+dC_{\texttt{seq}}^{(B)}\right).

The computational cost (number of individual state generations from
\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
or any model in
\Pi
over the course of training) is bounded by:

\displaystyle n_{\texttt{comp}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{dT}{\varepsilon}\right)+\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(dTC_{\texttt{ans}}^{(B)}+dBC_{\texttt{seq}}^{(B)}\right).

Above,
d
denotes the Natarajan dimension for
\Pi
, and
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\cdot)
hides logarithmic factors in
|S|,C_{\texttt{seq}}^{(B)},\delta^{-1}
,
\varepsilon^{-1}
, and
T/B
.

We interpret
Theorem

2.12
as realizing a coverage expansion phenomenon concretely. Each bound splits into:

1.

A leading term,
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(d/\varepsilon)
for the sample and query complexity, and
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(dT/\varepsilon)
for the computational cost, capturing the irreducible cost of learning to accuracy
1-\varepsilon
.

2.

Burn-in
terms that carry all dependence on the coverage for
\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
, yet are nearly-independent of
\varepsilon
. Crucially, the burn-in depends only on coverage at the
block
scale
B
: over training,
\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
’s effective coverage at length
T
expands from exponentially small to a constant, after which improvement proceeds as if
\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
had had constant length-
T
coverage all along.

In contrast, standard RLVR fine-tuning (GRPO-style methods) applied directly at length
T
must pay for
\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
’s coverage at that length,
C_{\texttt{seq}}^{(T)}
.

\texttt{AutoLearn}.\texttt{RL}
closely follows the
AutoLearn
template, decomposing each length-
T
instance into a curriculum of length-
B
sub-problems, which can then be solved through off-the-shelf RLVR fine-tuning; as in our iSFT results, this leverages the fact that blocks of long instances can be interpreted as short instances in the semiautomaton setting. The same obstacles arise as in iSFT: short-range models must be accurate enough to survive composition, and the right distribution over sub-problems must be sampled.

3
iSFT: Learning from Interactive Chain-of-Thought Supervision

This section presents our algorithms and main guarantees for learning semiautomata in the iSFT setting (
2.6
). First, in
Section

3.1
, we motivate our main algorithm,
AutoLearn
, by highlighting the compositional structure of our semiautomaton learning setup. We then formally describe
AutoLearn
and present our main guarantee in
Section

3.3
. Finally, in
Section

3.4
we compare with guarantees achieved by natural non-curriculum baselines.

3.1
Algorithm Template: Composition and Boosting

Our main algorithm,
AutoLearn
(
Algorithm

2
), exploits the compositional structure of semiautomata to achieve sublinear-in-
T
query complexity for learning models with high accuracy on length-
T
instances. The core idea behind composition is that a model that predicts the state at time
\tau
with high accuracy for
\tau=\frac{T}{L}
can be composed
L
times to obtain a model for the terminal state on length-
T
instances with constant accuracy. We formalize this idea through the notion of a
composition operator
.

Definition 3.1

(Composition operator)
.

Fix some
t\in\mathbb{N}
, and consider any model
\widehat{\pi}:S\times\Sigma^{t}\to S
for the terminal state on instances of length
t
. Then for some
L\in\mathbb{N}
, the composed model
\widehat{\pi}^{\circ L}:S\times\Sigma^{tL}\to S
operates on instances of length
tL
.
\widehat{\pi}^{\circ L}
chunks the instance into
L
segments of length
t
, applying
\widehat{\pi}
to predict the terminal state of each chunk, using this as the start state for the next chunk, and repeating this process until termination. Formally, for a length-
tL
instance
{\bm{x}}=(s_{0},\bm{w}_{1:tL})
,
\widehat{\pi}^{\circ L}
outputs the state
s_{L}
, defined recursively by the process
s_{\ell}\leftarrow\widehat{\pi}({\bm{x}}^{\ell})
for
{\bm{x}}^{\ell}=(s_{\ell-1},\bm{w}_{t(\ell-1)+1:t\ell})
for
1\leqslant\ell\leqslant L
.

Composition allows us to extend models solving shorter instances to longer ones, and is equivalent to wrapping the short-length model within a recurrent loop. While this suggests a natural algorithm design approach (learn a model for short instances, then compose it), one expects that generically, inaccuracies in the learned model
\widehat{\pi}
will compound the more times we compose, degrading performance. This relationship between composition and accuracy is captured in the following observation.

Observation 3.2

(Compositional generalization)
.

Recall that
\rho
denotes the distribution over length-
T
instances given by nature. Let
\overline{\rho}
denote the uniform mixture distribution over instances of length
\tau=\frac{T}{L}
obtained by chunking longer length-
T
instances (defined formally in
Eq.

6
) and let
\varepsilon_{0}=\frac{1}{4L}
. Let
\widehat{\pi}
be any model satisfying a high-accuracy correctness guarantee on length-
\tau
test instances drawn from
\overline{\rho}
; namely,
\mathrm{Pr}_{{\bm{x}}\sim\overline{\rho}}\big(\widehat{\pi}({\bm{x}})\neq\pi^{\star}_{\tau}({\bm{x}})\big)\leqslant\varepsilon_{0}
. Then, the
L
-fold composition model
\widehat{\pi}^{\circ L}
(defined formally in
Definition

3.1
) satisfies
\mathrm{Pr}_{{\bm{x}}\sim\rho}(\widehat{\pi}^{\circ L}({\bm{x}})\neq\pi^{\star}_{T}({\bm{x}}))\leqslant\varepsilon_{0}L=1/4
.

3.2
follows by a simple union bound, and provides a mechanism by which length (i.e., hardness) can be traded off for accuracy. However, this length-versus-accuracy tradeoff is not sufficient on its own to achieve sublinear-in-
T
query complexity, as the increased accuracy requirement (i.e.,
\varepsilon_{0}
in
3.2
) wipes out potential gains from solving instances of much shorter length. For instance, if we naively train a model
\widehat{\pi}
using next-token prediction to very high accuracy on
1
-step instances, the target error on
1
-step problems would need to be
\varepsilon_{0}=\frac{1}{4T}
, which requires sample complexity scaling as
\Theta\big(d/\varepsilon_{0}\big)=\Theta(dT)
.

\begin{subarray}{c}\big(T,\frac{1}{4}\big)\\
\widehat{\pi}^{\circ L_{H}}\end{subarray}
\begin{subarray}{c}\big(\frac{T}{L_{H}},\frac{1}{4L_{H}}\big)\\
\widehat{\pi}_{H}\leftarrow\texttt{Plu}\big(\widehat{\pi}^{0},\cdots,\widehat{\pi}^{k_{H}-1}\big)\end{subarray}
\begin{subarray}{c}\big(\frac{T}{L_{H}},\frac{1}{4}\big)\\
\widehat{\pi}^{k_{H}-1}\end{subarray}
\begin{subarray}{c}\big(\frac{T}{L_{H}},\frac{1}{4}\big)\\
\widehat{\pi}^{0}\end{subarray}
\cdots
\vdots
\begin{subarray}{c}\textbf{Target:}\\
\text{Learner}\\
\text{at length-}T\end{subarray}
\begin{subarray}{c}\textbf{Composition:}\\
\text{trading-off length}\\
\text{for target error}\end{subarray}
\begin{subarray}{c}\textbf{Boosting:}\\
\text{combine weak}\\
\text{learners to achieve}\\
\text{low error}\end{subarray}

(a)

Tree structure of models.
(t,\varepsilon)
indicates the length of instances and the target error for the model at that node. The
k_{H}
weak length-
T/L_{H}
models
\widehat{\pi}_{H}^{0},\ldots,\widehat{\pi}_{H}^{k_{H}-1}
are combined by plurality vote into
\widehat{\pi}_{H}
and composed
L_{H}
-fold. The recursion continues for
H
levels; models are trained at the bottom-most level to solve
1
-step instances, say via next-token prediction.

\begin{subarray}{c}\text{Length-}T\\
\text{instance}\end{subarray}
\begin{subarray}{c}L_{H}\text{ sub-instances}\\
\text{of length-}T/L_{H}\end{subarray}
\begin{subarray}{c}\text{one sub-instance}\\
\text{of length-}T/L_{H}\end{subarray}
\cdots
\cdots
\vdots
\begin{subarray}{c}\textbf{Source:}\\[1.5pt]
\text{instances}\\[-1.0pt]
\text{drawn from }\rho\end{subarray}
\begin{subarray}{c}\textbf{Split:}\\
\text{Decompose length-}T\\
\text{instances into length-}\\
T/L_{H}\text{ sub-instances}\end{subarray}
\begin{subarray}{c}\textbf{Inverted sampling:}\\
\text{Adaptively select}\\
\text{``hard'' length-}T/L_{H}\\
\text{sub-instances}\end{subarray}

(b)

Data flow on the same tree. Length-
T
instances
{\bm{x}}\sim\rho
are decomposed by
Split
into
L_{H}
contiguous length-
T/L_{H}
sub-instances.
InvSampling
selects at most one among these to pass on a given one of the
k_{H}
boosting learners in (a). For the
j^{\text{th}}
boosting learner,
InvSampling
selects sub-instances based on the previous
j-1
models’ performance, to adaptively focus on “hard” sub-instances.

Figure 2
:
Two views of the recursion within
AutoLearn
(
Algorithm

1
): the
(a)
tree-like structure of induced models and
(b)
flow of data on the same tree.

Building on Part I
(
40
)
, we improve this length-versus-accuracy tradeoff by combining composition with
boosting

(
19
)
. Boosting lifts weak (i.e., constant-accuracy) models into high-accuracy models by training them on reweighted versions of the target distribution over instances and aggregating them. When combined with composition, the algorithm takes the following form:

1.

Train models solving short length-
\tau
instances to high accuracy via boosting.

2.

Compose the resulting models to solve length-
T
instances.

With some care, this approach can be shown to achieve query complexity
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(d\tau\log(1/\varepsilon_0)+d/\varepsilon_{0})
where
\varepsilon_{0}=\tau/T
when the target error under
\rho
is a constant. Choosing
\tau
optimally gives us a guarantee scaling as
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(d\sqrt{T})
, which is sublinear in
T
, and improves over standard baselines.

From sublinear to subpolynomial query complexity.

To achieve the stronger
subpolynomial

2^{\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\sqrt{\log T})}
in our main theorem (
Theorem

3.4
), our main approach,
AutoLearn
(
Algorithm

2
) applies the boosting + composition template above recursively. To train models solving length-
\tau
instances, we decompose instances into even shorter length-
\tau^{\prime}\ll\tau
ones, decompose these instances into even shorter length-
\tau^{\prime\prime}\ll\tau^{\prime}
instances, and so on, until all target instances have length-
1
. At this “bottom level” of the recursion, all training examples consist of next-state transitions of the form
(s,w)\mapsto\pi^{\star}(s,w)
, and weak learners can be trained via next-token prediction. Bottom-level models trained this way within
AutoLearn
are then recursively
aggregated
through boosting and
composed
to solve longer length instances. The final length-
T
model trained through this process recursively aggregates models across multiple scales; we refer to this overarching approach as
multiscale boosting
.

Multiscale boosting realizes a
length-based curriculum
: as the algorithm proceeds to deeper levels, longer (i.e., harder) instances are progressively decomposed into shorter ones which are easier to train to solve. By composition, models which solve shorter instances are combined to realize ones which can solve harder problems. Moreover, the curriculum itself is
self-generated
: at each scale,
AutoLearn
adaptively selects which sub-instances to train on based on the failures of models trained at finer scales (via
InvSampling
,
Algorithm

3
), rather than following a fixed schedule.

3.2
Overview of
AutoLearn

We now formally introduce
AutoLearn
(
Algorithm

2
), which instantiates the multiscale boosting template above with
2H+1
levels where
L
is a branching factor and
H=\log_{L}(T)
. The algorithm is visualized in
Fig.

2(a)
. We present the recursion in terms of half-integer levels
h=H+\frac{1}{2},H,H-\frac{1}{2},\ldots,0
, where the base level
h=0
corresponds to length-
1
instances, and larger
h
corresponds to longer instances.
AutoLearn
is parameterized by two schedules:

•

Decomposition schedule

(L_{h})
: determines how many sub-instances to break instances (of a certain length) into at each level of recursion; the corresponding model trained in each level is composed
L_{h}
-fold to induce a model solving longer instances.

•

Branching schedule

(k_{h})
: determines how many weak models to aggregate at each level of recursion.

The half-step levels delineate two types of recursion steps. At integer levels
h
,
\texttt{AutoLearn}_{h}
implements both composition and boosting, setting
L_{h}=2^{\Theta(\sqrt{\log(T)})}
and
k_{h}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(1)
. At half-integer depths
h
,
\texttt{AutoLearn}_{h}
implements only boosting, not composition, setting
L_{h}=1
and
k_{h}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(1)
.

Splitting and inverted sampling.

At integer depths
h\in\mathbb{N}
where composition occurs, length-
\tau_{h}
instances are broken down into length-
\tau_{h-1}
sub-instances via subroutines
Split
and
InvSampling
(
Algorithms

4
and
3
). A key structural property is that each length-
\tau_{h}
instance contributes at most one length-
\tau_{h-1}
sub-instance, preserving independence; this is visualized in
Fig.

2(b)
, and ensures that length-
1
instances (the base case in the recursion), can be solved using off-the-shelf methods for classification/next-token prediction. The subroutine
InvSampling
addresses a central technical issue we have not yet discussed—
distribution shift
—which complicates the process of generating examples from the appropriate distribution for boosting; we defer a dedicated discussion of the motivation and design of this component to
Section

5
.

Base learner and next-token prediction.

Crucially, all of the model training in
AutoLearn
takes place at the bottom-level of the recursion, where
h=0
. By this level, instances are reduced to a collection of next-state examples
(s,w)\mapsto\pi^{\star}(s,w)
. To fit a model
\widehat{\pi}
in
\Pi
,
AutoLearn
makes use of a generic “base learner”
Base
, representing next-token prediction or another off-the-shelf algorithm. We can interpret
\texttt{AutoLearn}_{h}
for
h\geqslant\frac{1}{2}
as adaptively constructing a curriculum of shorter instances on which to fit models using
Base
, concentrating on instances where the current model fails and decomposing them into sub-instances where necessary.

Our main guarantee for
AutoLearn
is stated in terms of the sample complexity required to fit a next-token predictor model to constant accuracy using
Base
, under an arbitrary but fixed distribution over instances.

Definition 3.3

(Weak learning
\Pi
from i.i.d.​ next-state examples)
.

Let
D=\{(s_{i},w_{i})\mapsto\pi^{\star}(s_{i},w_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
where
(s_{i},w_{i})\overset{\text{i.i.d.}}{\sim}\sigma\in\Delta_{S\times\Sigma}
denotes a dataset of i.i.d.​ next-state examples. A weak learner for
\Pi
is any algorithm
\texttt{Base}(D\,\|\,\delta^{\prime})
parameterized by a failure probability
\delta^{\prime}\in(0,1)
, which learns a model for
\pi^{\star}
to constant accuracy from
D
with sample complexity
n_{\texttt{weak}}(\delta^{\prime})
. Namely, for any
\sigma
and
\pi^{\star}\in\Pi
, as long as
n\geqslant n_{\texttt{weak}}(\delta^{\prime})
the model
\widehat{\pi}:S\times\Sigma\to S
returned by
Base
satisfies w.p. at least
1-\delta^{\prime}
,

\mathrm{Pr}_{(s,w)\sim\sigma}\big[\widehat{\pi}(s,w)\neq\pi^{\star}(s,w)\big]\leqslant\frac{1}{4}.

Furthermore, we say that
Base
admits a generic weak-learning guarantee if
n_{\texttt{weak}}(\delta^{\prime})\leqslant\texttt{comp}(\Pi)\log(1/\delta')
, where
\texttt{comp}(\Pi)
is some notion of complexity of the class
\Pi
.

Definition

3.3
posits that the class
\Pi
is learnable from
i.i.d.
​ next-state examples to constant accuracy. In the definition above, we demarcate the “generic” weak-learning guarantee to capture typical scaling behavior of the sample complexity with respect to the failure probability
\delta^{\prime}
. For instance, when
Base
is instantiated as empirical risk minimization (ERM) under the
0
-
1
loss, we obtain a generic weak-learning guarantee with
\texttt{comp}(\Pi)=d\log(|S|)
where
d
is the Natarajan dimension of
\Pi
(
Definition

2.3
)
(
37
)
.

3.3
Main Result: Compositional Curriculum Achieves Sublinear Query Complexity

Our main guarantee for
AutoLearn
shows that it achieves subpolynomial-in-
T
sample and query complexity when invoked with off-the-shelf base learners such as next-token prediction.

Algorithm 1

\texttt{AutoLearn}_{h}\big(D_{\mathrm{input}}\ \|\ \tau_{h},\delta\big)

1:

# Learning semiautomata via multiscale boosting

2:

Input:
Class of semiautomaton transitions
\Pi
over state space
S
and alphabet
\Sigma
,

3:

Current depth
h\in\{0,\frac{1}{2},1,\frac{3}{2},\cdots\}
,

4:

Current length of instances
\tau_{h}\in\mathbb{N}
,

5:

Target failure probability
\delta
,

6:

Dataset of instances
D_{\mathrm{input}}=\{{\bm{x}}^{i}=(s^{i}_{0},\bm{w}^{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
.

7:

Learner alg.
\texttt{Base}(\,\cdot\,\|\,\delta^{\prime})
for
\Pi
from i.i.d. next-state data (
Definition

3.3
).

8:

\blacktriangleright

\texttt{Base}(\,\cdot\,\|\,\delta^{\prime})
has sample complexity
n_{\texttt{weak}}(\delta^{\prime})
parameterized by failure probability.

9:

Theorem 3.4

(Main guarantee for
AutoLearn
)
.

For any
\delta^{\prime}\in(0,1)
, let
\texttt{Base}(\cdot\,\|\,\delta^{\prime})
be any weak learner for
\Pi
from i.i.d. next-state data, as defined in
Definition

3.3
. Suppose
Base
admits a generic weak-learning guarantee satisfying
n_{\texttt{weak}}(\delta^{\prime})\leqslant\texttt{comp}(\Pi)\log(1/\delta')
.

Let
\varepsilon,\delta\in(0,1)
and suppose
H=\sqrt{\log_{2}(T)}\in\mathbb{N}
. Suppose
\texttt{AutoLearn}(\cdot\,\|\,T,\varepsilon,\delta)
(
Algorithm

2
) is invoked with a base learner
\texttt{Base}(\cdot\,\|\,\delta^{\prime})
with weak-learning sample complexity
n_{\texttt{weak}}(\delta^{\prime})\leqslant\texttt{comp}(\Pi)\log(1/\delta')
. Then
AutoLearn
learns a model
\widehat{\pi}:S\times\Sigma^{T}\to S
with
\mathrm{Pr}_{{\bm{x}}\sim\rho}\big[\widehat{\pi}({\bm{x}})\neq\pi^{\star}_{T}({\bm{x}})\big]\leqslant\varepsilon
with probability at least
1-\delta
, using sample and query complexity upper bounded by:

n_{\texttt{sample}},n_{\texttt{query}}\leqslant 2^{\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\big(\sqrt{\log(T)}\big)}\cdot\left[\frac{\texttt{comp}(\Pi)\log^{2}(1/\varepsilon)\log(1/\delta)}{\varepsilon}\right].

The main proof is provided in
Section

C.3
. Relevant lemmas are sketched in
Appendix

C
.
Theorem

2.7
follows from this result by instantiating
Base
as empirical risk minimization (ERM) on the next-token prediction objective (
Definition

2.2
), and noting that
\texttt{comp}(\Pi)
can be chosen as
d\log(|S|)
, where
d
is the Natarajan dimension. With more sophisticated choices for
Base
, such as the recently proposed algorithm of
38
, we can achieve improved sample complexity guarantees such as
\texttt{comp}(\Pi)=d_{\texttt{DS}}
, where
d_{\texttt{DS}}
is the DS dimension
(
11
)
. Our most general guarantees for
AutoLearn
(given in
Theorem

C.1
) are directly stated in terms of
n_{\texttt{weak}}(\cdot)
(and do not assume a generic weak learning guarantee for
Base
).

In
Appendix

B
we analyze a simpler variant of
AutoLearn
which considers a single round of boosting + aggregation to understand the core mechanism.

Remark 3.5

(On the technical condition on length
T
)
.

Algorithm

1
requires the condition
\sqrt{\log_{2}(T)}\in\mathbb{N}
. The guarantees can be extended to any value of
T\in\mathbb{N}
by replacing
T
by
T^{\prime}
, where
T^{\prime}
is the smallest value larger than
T
such that there exists
H\in\mathbb{N}
satisfying
T^{\prime}=(L^{\prime})^{H}
, where
L^{\prime}=2^{\sqrt{\log_{2}(T^{\prime})}}
,
8
8

8

It suffices to choose
T^{\prime}=2^{m^{2}}
for some
m\in\mathbb{N}
. Such a
T^{\prime}
always exists in
[T,2T^{2}]
.
and changing the class
\Pi
to
\Pi^{\prime}
as follows: the state space
S
is changed to
S^{\prime}=S\times[T]
enabling the “timestep” to be tracked, and, each
\pi\in\Pi
is changed to
\pi^{\prime}\in\Pi^{\prime}
as,

\text{For }s^{\prime}=(s,t)\in S\times[T],\quad\pi^{\prime}(s^{\prime},w)=\begin{cases}(\pi(s,w),t+1)&\text{if }t<T,\\
(s,t)&\text{if }t=T,\\
\end{cases}

Changing
\Pi
to
\Pi^{\prime}
results in the state space growing by a factor of
T
in size. However, this transformation itself does not affect the learnability of
\Pi
, since an algorithm for learning an unknown
\pi^{\star}\in\Pi
from i.i.d. next-state data also induces a model for learning the corresponding
(\pi^{\prime})^{\star}\in\Pi^{\prime}
from i.i.d. next-state data achieving the same error guarantee, and vice versa.
