3.4
Comparison with Non-Curriculum Baselines

To interpret
Theorem

3.4
, we contrast the dependence on the sequence length-
T
achieved by
AutoLearn
with that of two natural non-curriculum baselines: SFT on full sequences and end-to-end learning from final-state supervision
(
29
)
.
9
9

9

We remark that these baselines are more general than
AutoLearn
, in the sense that they can be applied to general autoregressive models, not just semiautomata.

3.4.1
Vanilla SFT: Learning from Full Chains-of-Thought

Arguably the most natural approach to learning semiautomata is to use supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on full chains-of-thought. Concretely, in our framework, this corresponds to querying the
\texttt{iCoT}(\cdot)
oracle
T
times on each instance in the training dataset to label the full state sequence
\pi^{\star}_{1:T}({\bm{x}}^{i})
. Such a labeled dataset is composed of many next-state examples of the form
(s,w)\mapsto\pi^{\star}(s,w)
, and a model
\widehat{\pi}
can be fit to this dataset via empirical risk minimization on the next-token prediction objective:

\displaystyle\widehat{\pi}\in\argmin_{\pi\in\Pi}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{I}\big(\pi(s^{i}_{t-1},w^{i}_{t})\neq\pi^{\star}(s^{i}_{t-1},w^{i}_{t})\big).

where
{\bm{x}}^{i}=(s_{0}^{i},\bm{w}^{i}_{1:T})
and
s^{i}_{t}=\pi^{\star}_{t}({\bm{x}}^{i})
for all
i\in[n]
and
t\in[T]
, given an input dataset of
n
instances
\{{\bm{x}}^{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
. This is an instance of a more general class of
full-CoT
learners we define as follows.

Definition 3.6

(Full CoT learner)
.

Given a dataset of instances
D=\{{\bm{x}}^{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}\overset{\text{i.i.d.}}{\sim}\rho
, a full CoT learning algorithm
\texttt{Alg}(D)
chooses a subset of instances indexed
S\subseteq[n]
and queries
\texttt{iCoT}(\cdot)

T
times on each instance
{\bm{x}}^{i}
for
i\in S
to observe the full state sequence
\pi^{\star}_{1:T}({\bm{x}}^{i})
. The final model returned is trained on the CoT labeled dataset—for example, by using next-token prediction.

As full CoT learners commit to querying full state sequences on every instance (even when potentially unnecessary), we show that any learner in this class requires linear-in-
T
query complexity; the proof of this result is given in
Section

F.1
.

Proposition 3.7
.

Consider any full CoT learner
Alg
(cf.
Definition

3.6
). For any
\varepsilon,\delta\in(0,\tfrac{1}{2})
, there exists a class of next-state/token predictors
\Pi
with Natarajan dimension
d
and a target distribution
\rho
such that for
Alg
to return a model
\widehat{\pi}
such that
\mathrm{Pr}_{{\bm{x}}\sim\rho}\big[\widehat{\pi}({\bm{x}})\neq\pi^{\star}_{T}({\bm{x}})\big]\leqslant\frac{1}{4}
with probability at least
\frac{1}{2}
, its sample complexity must satisfy
n_{\texttt{sample}}\geqslant cd
for a universal constant
c>0
. Furthermore, this implies that for any such approach, the query complexity must satisfy
n_{\texttt{query}}\geqslant cdT
to achieve the same guarantee.

In Part I
(
40
)
, we showed that for the goal of achieving high accuracy (
1-\varepsilon
), it is possible to improve over vanilla SFT by adaptively choosing which instances to gather full CoTs for. This leads to query complexity
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(dT/\varepsilon)
to
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\big(d/\varepsilon+dT\big)
, pushing the dependency on
T
into a burn-in term. Ultimately though, each CoT requires making
\Omega(T)
queries to the
\texttt{iCoT}(\cdot)
oracle, so the query complexity is still linear in
T
.

3.4.2
End-to-End Feedback

Since the final objective in our setting is to accurately predict the final state
\pi^{\star}_{T}({\bm{x}}^{i})
, another natural learning approach is to ignore the intermediate CoT altogether, and label each instance in the training dataset only with the final state
\pi^{\star}_{T}({\bm{x}}^{i})
. One can then train a model which directly classifies the final state based on the instance. We formally define this class of
end-to-end
learners as follows.

Definition 3.8

(End-to-end learner)
.

Given a dataset of instances
D=\{{\bm{x}}^{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}\overset{\text{i.i.d.}}{\sim}\rho
, an end-to-end learning algorithm
\texttt{Alg}(D)
queries
\texttt{iCoT}(\cdot)
once per instance to label the terminal state
\pi^{\star}_{T}({\bm{x}}^{i})
. A model is then trained on this dataset. For example, by finding a model
\widehat{\pi}
consistent with all terminal labels—that is,
\widehat{\pi}_{T}({\bm{x}}^{i})=\pi^{\star}_{T}({\bm{x}}^{i})
for all
i\in[n]
—and predicting the terminal state on new instances using
\widehat{\pi}_{T}
. This example corresponds to learning a model by empirical risk minimizer over the induced “end-to-end” class
\Pi_{T}
defined as
\Pi_{T}=\{\pi_{T}:\pi\in\Pi\}

(
29
)
.

While this approach seems potentially appealing since it requires only one
\texttt{iCoT}(\cdot)
query per instance, the absence of intermediate CoT grounding makes learning from such feedback more challenging statistically:
29
show that any end-to-end learner requires a number of examples linear in
T
.

Proposition 3.9

(Corollary of Theorem E.1 of
29
)
.

Consider any end-to-end learning algorithm
Alg
(cf.
Definition

3.8
). For any
\varepsilon,\delta\in(0,1/2)
, there exists a class of next-token predictors
\Pi
with Natarajan dimension
d
, and a target distribution
\rho
over instances, such that for
Alg
to return a model
\widehat{\pi}
such that
\mathrm{Pr}_{{\bm{x}}\sim\rho}\big[\widehat{\pi}({\bm{x}})\neq\pi^{\star}_{T}({\bm{x}})\big]\leqslant\varepsilon
with probability at least
1-\delta
,
Alg
must incur sample complexity
n_{\texttt{sample}}\geqslant\Omega\big(\big(dT+\log(1/\delta)\big)\cdot\varepsilon^{-1}\big)
.

Ignoring statistical considerations, for simple models such as iterated linear classifiers, learning from end-to-end feedback is computationally hard under standard complexity theoretic assumptions, even when next-token prediction is computationally efficient
(
29
, Theorem 4.4)
.

3.5
Applications of
Theorem

3.4

In this section we instantiate
Theorem

3.4
for the specific classes of semiautomata previously discussed in
Section

2
: Regular languages and linear recurrences over finite fields.

Regular languages.

As discussed earlier, the class of regular languages induced by regular expressions with alphabetic length at most
k
induces a natural class of semiautomata. Specifically, the induced semiautomaton is always initialized at the state
s_{0}=0
, and input words are strings in
\Sigma^{T}
. The terminal state of the semiautomaton can be used to decide whether the input word is a member of the regular language (i.e., whether it is generated by the corresponding regular expression).

In more detail, let
\texttt{RE}_{k}(\Sigma)
denote the set of regular expressions over alphabet
\Sigma
with alphabetic length at most
k
. The number of syntactically distinct languages realized by
\texttt{RE}_{k}(\Sigma)
is at most
|\Sigma|^{\Theta(k)}

(
23
, Table 8)
. The DFA for each regular language can be obtained by determinizing an NFA on
k+1
states (Glushkov’s construction) via the powerset construction. Thus,
\texttt{RE}_{k}(\Sigma)
corresponds to a class of semiautomata
\Pi_{\texttt{RE}_{k}}
on
|S|=2^{\Theta(k)}
states, and the size of the class itself is
|\Pi_{\texttt{RE}_{k}}|=|\Sigma|^{\Theta(k)}
. See
Section

A.1
for a detailed example.

In this setting, the
\texttt{iCoT}(\cdot)
oracle identifies the set of states which are reachable at time
t
. By divide-and-conquer, it can be implemented in parallel time
\mathrm{poly}(k,\log(T))
(and with
T\cdot\mathrm{poly}(k)
work). Furthermore, when
Theorem

3.4
is specialized to this setting, the following guarantees on sample complexity and query complexity are established for
AutoLearn
(
Algorithm

2
),

\displaystyle n_{\texttt{sample}},n_{\texttt{query}}\leqslant 2^{\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\sqrt{\log(T)})}\cdot\frac{k\log(|\Sigma|)\log^{2}(1/\varepsilon)\log(1/\delta)}{\varepsilon},

where the target failure probability is
\delta
, error rate is
\varepsilon
. Notably, this guarantee only scales linearly in the bound on the alphabetic length,
k
, even though the underlying (semi-)automata are on
2^{\Theta(k)}
states.

Linear recurrences over finite fields.

Linear recurrences over finite fields correspond to semiautomata with state space
S=\mathbb{F}_{q}^{d}
, input alphabet
\Sigma=\mathbb{F}_{q}^{d}
, and transition function
\pi(s,w)=As+Bw
for unknown matrices
A\in\mathbb{F}_{q}^{d\times d}
and
B\in\mathbb{F}_{q}^{d\times d}
. Simulating for
T
steps computes the linear recurrence
s_{T}=A^{T}s_{0}+\sum_{t=1}^{T}A^{T-t}Bw_{t}
. The class
\Pi
of all such linear transitions is parameterized by the pair
(A,B)
, giving
|\Pi|=q^{2d^{2}}
, and Natarajan dimension
\mathrm{Ndim}(\Pi)\leqslant\mathcal{O}(d^{2}\log q)
. Specializing
Theorem

3.4
to this setting results in the following sample complexity and query complexity upper bounds for
AutoLearn
:

\displaystyle n_{\texttt{sample}},n_{\texttt{query}}\leqslant 2^{\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\sqrt{\log(T)})}\cdot\frac{d^{2}\log(q)\log^{2}(1/\varepsilon)\log(1/\delta)}{\varepsilon}.

Here, the target failure probability is
\delta
, error rate is
\varepsilon
. This guarantee only scales polynomially in the dimension
d
, even though the underlying semiautomaton has
|S|=q^{d}
states. In this setting, the
\texttt{iCoT}(\cdot)
oracle again has a natural interpretation as before, computing the state after
t
steps of recursion, which by divide-and-conquer can be computed in parallel in time
\mathrm{poly}(d,\log(T))
with
T\cdot\mathrm{poly}(d)
work.

4
RLVR: Fine-Tuning a Weak Reference Model

This section adapts the compositional curriculum from
Section

3
to the RLVR setting, where the learner aims to improve a weak reference model
\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
using an outcome verifier (
Definition

2.8
). We show that the compositional curriculum reduces the coverage requirement on
\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
from the full sequence length
T
to the shorter block scale
B
, an exponentially weaker condition. We begin by motivating the need for a compositional curriculum through a discussion of naive RLVR approaches (
Section

4.1
), then describe the algorithm
\texttt{AutoLearn}.\texttt{RL}
(
Section

4.2
), and finally present the main guarantee (
Section

4.3
).

4.1
Motivation: Naive RLVR Methods and the Need for Coverage

Given a pre-trained model
\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
and an outcome verifier
\mathcal{V}
, the basic principle underlying standard RLVR fine-tuning methods (e.g., GRPO
(
44
)
) is to draw rollouts from
\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
, filter by outcome correctness, and train on the survivors.
RLFineTune
(
Algorithm

5
; also used in Part I
(
40
)
) provides a simple, theoretically tractable instantiation of this principle. Given a distribution over problem instances, it repeatedly generates candidate CoTs from
\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
, keeps those whose terminal state is verified as correct by
\mathcal{V}
, and trains a model on the filtered dataset. Under a sequence-level coverage assumption
Eq.

1
, each rollout produces a fully correct CoT with probability at least
(\smash{C_{\texttt{seq}}^{(T)}})^{-1}
, and altogether roughly

\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{d\smash{C_{\texttt{seq}}^{(T)}}}{\varepsilon}\right)

(4)

prompts, verifier queries, and training time suffice to find an
\varepsilon
-optimal model. The obvious limitation of this approach is that it requires the reference model to have nontrivial coverage over instances of length
T
. If the model
\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
only satisfies coverage at length
B\ll T
(i.e., it is not strong enough to solve length-
T
instances directly), one can at best hope for a bound of the form
\smash{C_{\texttt{seq}}^{(T)}}\leqslant(C_{\texttt{seq}}^{(B)})^{T/B}
, which scales exponentially in the number of blocks.
10
10

10

For unstructured problems, this is the best possible bound for any algorithm, not just
RLFineTune
; see
16
.

Algorithm 5

\texttt{RLFineTune}\big(D_{\mathrm{input}}\ \|\ B,\varepsilon,\delta\big)

1:

# Sharpening a reference model
\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
satisfying sequence-level coverage using verifier feedback.

2:

Input:
Class of semiautomaton transitions
\Pi
over state space
S
and alphabet
\Sigma
,

3:

Number of steps of semiautomaton simulation
B
,

4:

Reference model
\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
satisfying
Eq.

1
with parameters
(B,C_{\texttt{seq}}^{(B)},C_{\texttt{ans}}^{(B)})
,

5:

Outcome verifier
\mathcal{V}
,

6:

Target error rate
\varepsilon
and failure probability
\delta
,

7:

Dataset of instances
D_{\mathrm{input}}=\{{\bm{x}}^{i}=(s^{i}_{0},\bm{w}^{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
.

8:

Instantiate:

m\leftarrow C_{\texttt{seq}}^{(B)}\log(4n \Cseq^{(B)} / \delta)
.

9:

for

{\bm{x}}^{i}\in D_{\mathrm{input}}

do

10:

Draw CoTs,
\bm{y}^{i,j}\sim\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}_{1:B}(\,\cdot\,|\,{\bm{x}}^{i})
for
j=1,\cdots,m
.

11:

Let
D_{i}
denote the set of deduplicated CoTs among
\{\bm{y}^{i,j}\}_{j=1}^{m}
.

12:

Discard low-probability CoTs which lead to incorrect answers:

\widetilde{D}_{i}\leftarrow\big\{\bm{y}\in D_{i}:\mathcal{V}({\bm{x}}^{i},y_{B})=1\text{ and }\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}_{1:B}(\,\bm{y}\,|\,{\bm{x}}^{i})\geqslant\big(C_{\texttt{seq}}^{(B)}\big)^{-1}\big\}.

13:

Return:

\widehat{\pi}\in\underset{\pi\in\Pi}{\operatorname{argmin}}\ \mathcal{L}(\pi;D_{\mathrm{input}})
, where
\mathcal{L}(\pi;D_{\mathrm{input}})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{I}\big(\pi_{1:B}({\bm{x}}^{i})\not\in\widetilde{D}_{i}\big)
.

4.2
Overview of
\texttt{AutoLearn}.\texttt{RL}

To overcome the coverage requirement for naive RLVR, we adopt a compositional curriculum that decomposes length-
T
problem instances into length-
B
instances, the scale at which
\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
is reliable. Our algorithm,
\texttt{AutoLearn}.\texttt{RL}
(
Algorithm

6
), uses
RLFineTune
as an inner-loop primitive to improve
\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
on length-
B
instances, wrapped in an outer loop that constructs the curriculum using an adapted version of
Algorithm

1
. As in our previous results, we critically use that the semiautomaton is time-homogeneous: the same transition map governs every step, so any length-
B
block of a length-
T
instance—beginning at an intermediate state and treating it as a fresh start state—is itself a valid length-
B
instance. Consequently, the outcome verifier
\mathcal{V}
can be applied to check attempts at solving each such block.

In more detail,
\texttt{AutoLearn}.\texttt{RL}
(
Algorithm

6
) is an invocation of
\texttt{AutoLearn}_{H}
(
Algorithm

1
), but uses a simplified decomposition and branching schedule with
H=1
(in other words, the algorithm considers instances of length
T
at the top level and length
B
at the bottom level, with no intermediate levels):

•

At the top level, we set
L_{1}=1
and
k_{1}=\Theta(\log(1/\varepsilon))
, which corresponds to boosting from constant accuracy at length-
T
to accuracy
1-\varepsilon
at length-
T
.

•

At level
h=\frac{1}{2}
, we set
L_{\frac{1}{2}}=T/B
and
k_{\frac{1}{2}}=\Theta(\log(T/B))
, which corresponds to aggregating and composing short-range models on length-
B
blocks with constant accuracy into a model solving length-
T
instances which also achieves constant accuracy.

•

At the bottom level, we set
L_{0}=1
and
k_{0}=1
, which corresponds to training a length-
B
model to constant accuracy; for this, we use
\texttt{Base}\equiv\texttt{RLFineTune}
using feedback from the outcome verifier
\mathcal{V}
.

Similar to its counterpart in the iSFT setting,
\texttt{AutoLearn}.\texttt{RL}
can be viewed as an outer-loop which constructs a curriculum of independent length-
B
instances, wrapped around an inner loop which improves the reference model
\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
on these length-
B
instances via RL. Our analysis makes use of the following guarantee for
RLFineTune
, which asserts that it can improve
\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
to constant accuracy on length-
B
, but any algorithm with a similar guarantee can be used in its place. In this sense,
\texttt{AutoLearn}.\texttt{RL}
can be viewed as a reduction to RLVR fine-tuning over length-
B
instances.

Proposition 4.1

(Guarantee for
RLFineTune
; adapted from
40
)
.

Fix
\delta\in(0,1)
, and suppose the reference model
\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
satisfies sequence-level coverage with parameter
C_{\texttt{seq}}^{(B)}
at length
B
(
Definition

2.11
). There is an absolute constant
C>0
such that when
\texttt{RLFineTune}(\,\cdot\,\|\,B,\varepsilon,\delta)
is run on a dataset of size

n_{\texttt{sample}}\geqslant\frac{C\big(d\log\big( B |S| \Cblock\big)\log(1/\varepsilon)+\log(1/\delta)\big)}{\varepsilon}

drawn from some target distribution
\rho_{B}
over length-
B
instances, the resulting model
\widehat{\pi}:S\times\Sigma^{B}\to S
satisfies
\mathrm{Pr}_{{\bm{x}}\sim\rho_{B}}\big[\widehat{\pi}({\bm{x}})\neq\pi^{\star}_{B}({\bm{x}})\big]\leqslant\varepsilon
with probability at least
1-\delta
. Here
d
is the Natarajan dimension of
\Pi
. Furthermore, the number of queries
RLFineTune
makes to the outcome verifier
\mathcal{V}
is at most,
n_{\texttt{query}}\leqslant\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\big(\frac{dC_{\texttt{seq}}^{(B)}}{\varepsilon}\big)
, and the computational cost of
RLFineTune
is at most,
n_{\texttt{comp}}\leqslant\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\big(\frac{dBC_{\texttt{seq}}^{(B)}}{\varepsilon}\big)
, where
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\cdot)
hides logarithmic factors in
|S|,B,C_{\texttt{seq}}^{(B)}
,
\varepsilon
and
1/\delta
. Moreover, the verifier is queried only to check predictions for the terminal state of length-
B
instances. Here the computational cost is the number of states generated from
\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
or any other model in
\Pi
over the course of training (cf.
Remark

2.10
).

Algorithm 6

\texttt{AutoLearn}.\texttt{RL}\big(D_{\mathrm{input}}\ \|\ T,\varepsilon,\delta\big)

1:

# Learning semiautomata from verifier feedback using compositional curriculum.

2:

Input:
Class of semiautomaton transitions
\Pi
over state space
S
and alphabet
\Sigma
,

3:

Reference model
\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
satisfying
Eq.

1
with parameters
(B,C_{\texttt{seq}}^{(B)},C_{\texttt{ans}}^{(B)})
,

4:

Outcome verifier
\mathcal{V}
,

5:

Number of steps of semiautomaton simulation,
T
, with
T\pmod{B}\equiv 0
,

6:

Target error rate
\varepsilon
and failure probability
\delta
,

7:

Dataset of instances
D_{\mathrm{input}}=\{{\bm{x}}^{i}=(s_{0}^{i},\bm{w}^{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
.

8:

Return:

\widehat{\pi}\leftarrow\texttt{AutoLearn}_{1}\big(D_{\mathrm{input}}\ \|\ T,\delta\big)
, with parameters:

9:

Feedback oracle:
\texttt{iCoT}(\cdot)\leftarrow\texttt{GuessAndCheck}(\,\cdot\,\|T,B,\delta_{1})
with
\delta_{1}=\delta/n_{\texttt{sample}}^{\star,\texttt{RL}}

10:

\blacktriangleright

\texttt{GuessAndCheck}(\cdot)
defined in
Section

D.1
.

11:

n_{\texttt{sample}}^{\star,\texttt{RL}}
(cf.
Theorem

4.2
) is the sample complexity upper boundof
\texttt{AutoLearn}.\texttt{RL}
.

12:

Weak learner:
\texttt{Base}(\,\cdot\,\|\,\delta^{\prime})\leftarrow\texttt{RLFineTune}(\,\cdot\,\|\,B,\varepsilon_{0},\delta^{\prime})
where
\varepsilon_{0}^{-1}=Ck_{1/2}^{7/2}\log\big(L_{\frac{1}{2}} \big)

13:

Base failure probability
\delta_{0}=\delta/4k_{\frac{1}{2}}k_{1}
,

14:

Decomposition schedule:

\big(L_{\frac{1}{2}},L_{1}\big)=\big(\frac{T}{B},1\big)
,

15:

Branching schedule:

\big(k_{\frac{1}{2}},k_{1}\big)=\big(C\log(T/B),C\log(1/\varepsilon)\big)
for a large constant
C>0

16:

\blacktriangleright

RLFineTune
improves
\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
on length-
B
instances using outcome verifier
\mathcal{V}
.

Simulating intermediate state labels via guess-and-check.

To merge models learned at the length-
B
scale into a model at the length-
T
scale,
\texttt{AutoLearn}.\texttt{RL}
uses the
\texttt{Split}(\,\cdot\,\|\,L)
procedure from
Algorithm

1
, which requires intermediate state labels at the length-
B
scale. Naively such feedback is not available to the learner in the RLVR setting, but the reference model
\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
and outcome verifier
\mathcal{V}
can be used to simulate this feedback using a guess-and-check approach, which we refer to as
\texttt{GuessAndCheck}(\,\cdot\,\|\,T,B,\delta^{\prime})
.

\texttt{GuessAndCheck}(\,\cdot\,\|\,T,B,\delta^{\prime})
.

Given a length-
T
instance
{\bm{x}}
, a reference model which satisfies a bound on
C_{\texttt{ans}}^{(B)}
, outcome coverage at the length-
B
scale, in conjunction with the outcome verifier can be used to split the instance into
T/B
blocks of length
B
using a guess-and-check approach. Fix a prompt
{\bm{x}}
. To label the terminal state of the first block, i.e.,
\pi^{\star}_{B}({\bm{x}})
, we generate
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(C_{\texttt{ans}}^{(B)})
rollouts from the reference model. With high probability, at least one rollout has a correct state at
t=B
, and we can identify it with the verifier. We then proceed to the next block, interpreting
\pi^{\star}_{B}({\bm{x}})
as a start state, and repeating the process to identify
\pi^{\star}_{2B}({\bm{x}})
, and so on. We discuss this in more detail in
Section

D.1
.

For a given prompt, this procedure recovers the terminal states for all blocks using total query complexity
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(T/B\cdot C_{\texttt{ans}}^{(B)})
. A crucial detail, and the source for most of the technical effort behind the algorithm, is that this process does not identify correct intermediate states within each block, only the final state.

4.3
Main Result: Coverage Expansion via Compositional Curriculum

Our main result shows that
\texttt{AutoLearn}.\texttt{RL}
achieves high accuracy on length-
T
instances whenever the reference model
\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
can solve length-
B
instances with constant probability, a form of coverage expansion.

Theorem 4.2

(Main guarantee for
\texttt{AutoLearn}.\texttt{RL}
)
.

Fix
\delta\in(0,1)
, block length
B
, and assume that
T\pmod{B}\equiv 0
without loss of generality. Suppose the reference model
\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
satisfies
Eq.

1
with parameters
(B,C_{\texttt{seq}}^{(B)},C_{\texttt{ans}}^{(B)})
, and suppose the class
\Pi
has Natarajan dimension
d
(
Definition

2.3
). Then,
\texttt{AutoLearn}.\texttt{RL}
(
Algorithm

6
) returns a model
\widehat{\pi}
that satisfies
\mathrm{Pr}_{{\bm{x}}\sim\rho}\big[\widehat{\pi}({\bm{x}})\neq\pi^{\star}_{T}({\bm{x}})\big]\leqslant\varepsilon
with probability at least
1-\delta
. Moreover:

1.

The sample complexity is bounded by,
n_{\texttt{sample}}\leqslant n_{\texttt{sample}}^{\star,\texttt{RL}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon}\right)
.

2.

The total computational cost is bounded by
n_{\texttt{comp}}\leqslant\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{dT}{\varepsilon}\right)+\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(dTC_{\texttt{ans}}^{(B)}+dBC_{\texttt{seq}}^{(B)}\right)
, as measured by the number of states/tokens generated.
11
11

11

A comment on units: the computational cost
n_{\texttt{comp}}
in
Theorem

4.2
counts the total number of individual
state generations
produced by
\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
or by any model in
\Pi
over the course of training, rather than the number of length-
T
sequences rolled out; the latter is used as the measure of computational cost in Part I
(
40
)
. This fact accounts for the
T
dependency in the bounds on
n_{\texttt{comp}}
: to even predict the terminal state on a single length-
T
instance requires generating
T
states.

3.

The query complexity is upper bounded by
n_{\texttt{query}}\leqslant\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon}\right)+\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{T}{B}\cdot dC_{\texttt{ans}}^{(B)}+dC_{\texttt{seq}}^{(B)}\right)
.

Above,
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\cdot)
hides polylogarithmic factors in
T,|S|,C_{\texttt{seq}}^{(B)}
and
\delta^{-1}
.

The proof of
Theorem

4.2
is deferred to
Section

D.3
. The main features of the result are that: (1) All costs only scale with the model’s coverage at the length-
B
scale, representing a form of
coverage expansion
from the length-
B
scale to the length-
T
scale; and (2) the dependence on the model’s coverage—both for statistical and computational costs—is decoupled from the target accuracy
1-\varepsilon
, only appearing in a “burn-in” cost. The latter is a consequence of the boosting effect of the self-generated curriculum, and has a similar flavor to our RL fine-tuning results in Part I
(
40
)
. In what follows, we interpret the sample complexity, computational cost, and query complexity in more detail.

Sample complexity.

The number of prompts required by
\texttt{AutoLearn}.\texttt{RL}
scales as
n_{\texttt{sample}}\leqslant\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(d/\varepsilon)
. Up to logarithmic factors, this is optimal for PAC learning the class
\Pi
to accuracy
1-\varepsilon
, and is required as soon as
C_{\texttt{ans}}^{(B)}>2
, even if
T=1
.

Computational cost.

The computational cost of
\texttt{AutoLearn}.\texttt{RL}
—measured in terms of number of generated states—decomposes into two parts: a “leading order” term, which is polynomial in
\varepsilon^{-1}
but nearly independent of the coverage coefficients, and a “burn-in” term, which is polynomial in the coverage coefficients, but nearly-independent of
\varepsilon^{-1}
:

n_{\texttt{comp}}\leqslant\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\underbrace{\left(\frac{dT}{\varepsilon}\right)}_{\text{leading order}}+\underbrace{\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\big(dTC_{\texttt{ans}}^{(B)}+dBC_{\texttt{seq}}^{(B)}\big)}_{\text{burn-in cost $n_{\texttt{burn-in}}$}}.

(5)

The leading-order term
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(dT/\varepsilon)
reflects the number of state generations from
\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
needed to label the terminal state on each of the
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(d/\varepsilon)
instances in the training dataset; we expect this to be irreducible. The burn-in term depends on the pre-trained model’s coverage, but critically, does so only at the length-
B
scale. The term involving the (smaller) answer-level coverage coefficient
C_{\texttt{ans}}^{(B)}
is scaled by
T
, while the term involving the (larger) block-level coverage coefficient
C_{\texttt{seq}}^{(B)}
is scaled only by the block length
B
. In more detail:

•

Labeling cost.
The first term,
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(dTC_{\texttt{ans}}^{(B)})
, can be interpreted as the computational cost required to label a deliberately chosen set of
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(d)
length-
T
instances
{\bm{x}}
with ground-truth outcomes for each length-
B
block using the guess-and-check strategy described in
Section

4.2
. In particular, each
{\bm{x}}
is decomposed into
T/B
segments, and
\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
generates
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(C_{\texttt{ans}}^{(B)})
rollouts per segment, with overall cost
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(d\times T/B\times C_{\texttt{ans}}^{(B)}\times B)
, where the final factor of
B
arises because
\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
generates
B
states for each segment rollout.

•

Training cost.
The second term,
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(dBC_{\texttt{seq}}^{(B)})
, can be interpreted as the computational cost of using the base learner
RLFineTune
to improve
\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
to constant accuracy on length-
B
instances, as in
Proposition

4.1
. This term can therefore be interpreted as the computational cost emerging from
training
within
\texttt{AutoLearn}.\texttt{RL}
, once again focusing on the cost of generation, and not model updates (cf.
Remark

2.10
).

Query complexity.

The number of queries to the outcome verifier made by
\texttt{AutoLearn}.\texttt{RL}
has a similar decomposition to the computational cost: A leading-order term scaling as
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(d/\varepsilon)
, and a burn-in term which inherits all dependence on the coverage coefficients
C_{\texttt{seq}}^{(B)}
and
C_{\texttt{ans}}^{(B)}
:

n_{\texttt{query}}\leqslant\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon}\right)+\underbrace{\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{T}{B}\cdot dC_{\texttt{ans}}^{(B)}+dC_{\texttt{seq}}^{(B)}\right)}_{\text{burn-in cost}},

Here: (1) The leading-order term reflects the number of queries needed to verify length-
T
terminal state correctly on each of the
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(d/\varepsilon)
instances in the training dataset; (2) the burn-in term
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{T}{B}\cdot dC_{\texttt{ans}}^{(B)}\right)
reflects the number of queries needed to label all of the block-level terminal states for a deliberately chosen subset of
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(d)
instances; (3) the burn-in term
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(dC_{\texttt{seq}}^{(B)})
reflects the number of queries needed for
RLFineTune
to improve
\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
to constant accuracy on length-
B
instances.

4.3.1
Comparison to Part I

In Part I
(
40
)
, we showed that in the general autoregressive setting, a simpler form of self-generated curriculum with
no length curriculum
(i.e., purely selecting length-
T
prompts from the training set adaptively)
can be used to improve a reference model
\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
to accuracy
1-\varepsilon
with computational cost
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\big(dT/\varepsilon+dTC_{\texttt{seq}}^{(T)}\big)
. Our results above recover this guarantee by setting
B=T
, but—as discussed in the previous section—can be exponentially more efficient if
\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
only has constant coverage for blocks of length
B\ll T
.

5
Overview of Analysis Techniques: Recursion and Inverted Sampling

Having presented the high-level principles behind our algorithm
AutoLearn
in
Sections

3
and
4
, this section gives an in-depth overview of the multiscale boosting technique used within the algorithm. To simplify presentation, we restrict our attention to the iSFT setting (
Algorithm

1
), and focus on the regime where the target error
\varepsilon=\frac{1}{4}
is a constant, as this already captures the main technical challenges, and will use
d=\texttt{comp}(\Pi)
to refer to the complexity of the model class (in the sense of
Eq.

50
). The section proceeds in three parts.

•

Warm-up via composition and boosting
(
Section

5.1
). As a warmup, we first show that a single layer of boosting and composition is sufficient to achieve query
and
sample complexity
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(d\sqrt{T})
. This
already improves upon the linear-in-T
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(dT)
full-CoT baseline in
Section

3.4
, but is nonetheless still polynomial in
T
. We then highlight a natural path to improving this guarantee further (
Section

5.2
)—improving the sample complexity required by the
rejection sampling
step within boosting.

•

The reweighted-mixture-of-marginals problem and inverted sampling
(
Section

5.3
).
We isolate an abstract problem,
sampling from a reweighted mixture of marginals
, which captures the key structure of the sampling problem used within our boosting approach (
Section

5.3
), and give a new algorithm,
inverted sampling
, which solves it significantly more efficiently than naive rejection sampling (
Section

5.4
).

•

Putting everything together
(
Sections

5.5
and
5.6
).
We show that using inverted sampling within a single layer of recursion brings the sample complexity down to
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(d)
while maintaining query complexity scaling as
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(d\sqrt{T})
(
Section

5.5
), then show that an iterated version of this approach—recursing to depth
H=\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{\log T})
—yields
2^{\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\sqrt{\log T})}\cdot d
sample and query complexity (
Section

5.6
).

We begin by introducing notation used throughout the section.

Notation.

Recall that
\rho
denotes the target distribution over length-
T
instances. For
1\leqslant t_{1}<t_{2}\leqslant T
, we let
\rho_{t_{1}:t_{2}}
denote the law of
(s_{t_{1}-1},\bm{w}_{t_{1}:t_{2}})
, where
(s_{0},\bm{w}_{1:T})={\bm{x}}\sim\rho
and
s_{t_{1}-1}=\pi^{\star}_{t_{1}-1}({\bm{x}})
.
\rho_{t_{1},t_{2}}
can be viewed as a distribution over instances of length
t_{2}-t_{1}+1
. For some
\tau<T
define the uniform mixture of length-
\tau
marginals obtained from
\rho
as follows,

\overline{\rho}\ =\ \frac{1}{L}\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}\rho_{\tau i+1:\tau(i+1)}.

(6)

This is the distribution over instances by breaking down length-
T
instances drawn from
\rho
into segments of length
\tau
and selecting one uniformly at random (where the start state of the segment is labeled by
\pi^{\star}
).

5.1
A Single Level of Recursion: Achieving
n_{\texttt{sample}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(d\sqrt{T})
and
n_{\texttt{query}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(d\sqrt{T})

We first recap the idea of
composition
in
AutoLearn
. For a model
\widehat{\pi}:S\times\Sigma^{\tau}\to S
on length-
\tau
instances, we let
\widehat{\pi}^{\circ L}
denote its
L
-fold composition (
Definition

3.1
), which operates on length-
T
instances by chunking them into
L=T/\tau
chunks of length
\tau
. The composition framework (
3.2
) asserts that whenever
\widehat{\pi}
has error at most
\varepsilon_{0}=\frac{1}{4L}
under the distribution
\overline{\rho}
, the composed model
\widehat{\pi}^{\circ L}
has error at most
\frac{1}{4}
under the original distribution
\rho
. Thus, compositional generalization gives a mechanism by which length can be traded off for accuracy at the length-
\tau
scale. However, this tradeoff is not sufficient on its own to achieve sublinear-in-
T
query complexity: a single model
\widehat{\pi}
learned to error
\varepsilon_{0}=\frac{1}{4L}
under
\overline{\rho}
by querying full CoTs on length-
\tau
instances has query complexity scaling as
\mathcal{O}\big(d\tau/\varepsilon_{0}\big)=\mathcal{O}(dT)
, matching the full-CoT baseline of
Section

3.4.1
.

Breaking the
\mathcal{O}(dT)
barrier requires combining composition with
boosting
, building on Part I
(
40
)
. There, in the general autoregressive setting (without composition), we showed that boosting can be used to decide which instances to query
full CoTs
on, adaptively focusing on harder instances as determined by outcome correctness of the current ensemble of models. Given target error
\varepsilon>0
, boosting trains
\mathcal{O}(\log(1/\varepsilon))
weak models to constant accuracy on an adaptively chosen sequence of distributions, each of which requires only
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(d)
CoTs. The total query complexity required to train a length-
T
model in our iSFT setting is
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(dT+d/\varepsilon)
, where the former term accounts for the query complexity of training the weak learners (recall that one full CoT requires
T
iSFT queries), and the latter term accounts for the query complexity required to evaluate the weak learners and update the training distribution. Furthermore, the sample complexity is
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(d)
(more general,
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(d/\varepsilon)
for error
\varepsilon
).

Combining boosting with composition gives the following template:

1.

For a parameter
\tau\ll T
and
L=T/\tau
, use boosting to train a model
\widehat{\pi}
to accuracy
\varepsilon_{0}=1/4L
on length-
\tau
sequences drawn from
\overline{\rho}
.

2.

Return the
L
-fold composed model
\widehat{\pi}^{\circ L}
, giving error
\varepsilon=\varepsilon_{0}L=\frac{1}{4}
as desired.

Implementing boosting in this fashion requires rejection sampling from reweighted versions of the distribution
\overline{\rho}
—a non-trivial detail, as we will highlight below. After accounting for this, the total query complexity for this idealized template is:

n_{\texttt{query}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(d\tau+d/\varepsilon_{0}\right)=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(d\tau+dL\right).

Recalling that
L=T/\tau
and choosing
\tau=\sqrt{T}
to balance the two terms yields
n_{\texttt{query}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(d\sqrt{T})
query complexity, improving over the
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(dT)
query complexity of the full CoT baseline. Furthermore, the sample complexity is
n_{\texttt{sample}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(d/\varepsilon_{0})=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(dL)=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(d\sqrt{T})
.

5.2
Improving Rejection Sampling: A Path to Sub-Polynomial Query Complexity

The full version of
AutoLearn
(
Algorithm

1
) uses multiple levels of recursion, solving the length-
\tau
blocks above by breaking them into smaller blocks, and so on. The main bottleneck in making this recursion fruitful is the
sample complexity
in the single-level algorithm in the prequel, which scales as
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(d/\varepsilon_{0})=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(dL)
. To ensure that errors do not compound as we recurse, it is essential to improve this guarantee so that it scales as
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(d)
—i.e., nearly independent of the number of blocks
L
. We will return to the full derivation in
Section

5.6
, but for now we set our sights on improving the sample complexity to
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(d)
.

Showing that the sample complexity can be improved to be strictly better than
\mathcal{O}(d/\varepsilon_{0})
is essential for the recursion to be fruitful. With multiple rounds of recursion, instances are decomposed into shorter ones, following the schedule
T\to T/L\to\cdots\to T/L^{H}
. At each depth, a model with error
\frac{1}{4}
is desired; this is obtained by taking the
L
-fold composition of a model achieving error
\varepsilon_{0}=\frac{1}{4L}
under the appropriate mixture distribution of instances (akin to
\overline{\rho}
). This model itself is trained by boosting, by training and aggregating an ensemble of models each of which achieves error
\frac{1}{4}
across appropriately reweighted versions of the mixture distribution, and so on. As the depth increases, the length of instances decreases geometrically and at the deepest level, models are trained to solve instances of length
T/L^{H}
. As we discuss in the next paragraph, generating samples from the appropriate reweighted mixture distributions at the deepest level via rejection sampling will require drawing
d/\varepsilon_{0}^{H}
instances at the top level (i.e., from
\rho
). Since the length of instances at the bottom level is
\mathcal{O}(T/L^{H})=\mathcal{O}(T\varepsilon_{0}^{H})
, all in all, this gives query complexity,

n_{\texttt{query}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(dT\varepsilon_{0}^{H}+d/\varepsilon_{0}^{H})

And optimizing over
\varepsilon_{0}
and
H
still results in a guarantee that is no better than
\widetilde{\Theta}(d\sqrt{T})
.

Key bottleneck: Rejection sampling.

To highlight why the sample complexity for the naive approach in the prequel scales as
n_{\texttt{sample}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(d/\varepsilon_{0})=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(dL)
, let us dig into the details of the boosting stage. Formally, boosting proceeds in
k=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\log\varepsilon_{0}^{-1})=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\log L)
iterations. At iteration
j
, given weak models
\widehat{\pi}^{0},\ldots,\widehat{\pi}^{j-1}
, we train a new model
\widehat{\pi}^{j}
on a distribution
\overline{\rho}_{j}
, which reweights
\overline{\rho}
to focus on instances where the current ensemble
\big(\widehat{\pi}^{0},\cdots,\widehat{\pi}^{j-1}\big)
errs;
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(d)
samples from this distribution are required to ensure that
\widehat{\pi}^{j}
achieves constant accuracy. After all iterations conclude, we aggregate the models through a majority vote to produce a final length-
\tau
model

\widehat{\pi}=\texttt{Plu}\big(\{\widehat{\pi}^{0},\cdots,\widehat{\pi}^{k-1}\}\big).

As discussed above, this model has error
\varepsilon_{0}=1/4L
on
\overline{\rho}
, leading to error
\frac{1}{4}
for the composed model
\widehat{\pi}^{\circ L}
. This is depicted pictorially in
Figure

3(a)
.

Training the
j
th model above requires generating
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(d)
inputs from the reweighted distribution
\overline{\rho}_{j}
, which has the structure
\overline{\rho}_{j}(\cdot)\propto\overline{\rho}(\cdot)w_{j}(\cdot)
for a non-negative weight function
w_{j}
; informally,
w_{j}
downweights regions where the ensemble already has high accuracy and upweights regions with low accuracy (refer to
Eq.

2
for a formal definition of
w_{j}
). Standard boosting—as in Part I
(
40
)
—generates samples from this distribution using
rejection sampling

(
48
;
8
)
: We repeatedly draw
{\bm{x}}\sim\overline{\rho}
, then draw
\eta\sim\operatorname{Unif}([0,1])
and accept if
\eta\leqslant\frac{w_{j}({\bm{x}})}{\|w_{j}\|_{\infty}}
. Accepted samples are guaranteed to follow
\overline{\rho}_{j}
, so the only question is how many attempts are required. For this, one of two good situations must occur. First, if

\mathrm{Pr}_{{\bm{x}}\sim\overline{\rho},\eta\overset{\text{i.i.d.}}{\sim}\operatorname{Unif}([0,1])}\left(\eta\leqslant\frac{w_{j}({\bm{x}})}{\|w_{j}\|_{\infty}}\right)\geqslant\varepsilon_{0}

(7)

then only
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon_{0}^{-1})
attempts are required in expectation, leading to
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(d\varepsilon_{0}^{-1})
attempts in total to generate the full dataset for
\widehat{\pi}^{j}
. On the other hand, if the condition
Eq.

7
fails, one can show that the current ensemble
\big(\widehat{\pi}^{0},\cdots,\widehat{\pi}^{j-1}\big)
must already be good enough (
\varepsilon_{0}
-accurate under
\overline{\rho}
), and there is no need to continue training.

While the
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon_{0}^{-1})
sample complexity for rejection sampling that we sketch above is tight for generic sampling problems
(
8
)
, our compositional application has additional structure. In particular,
\rho_{j}
has the form

\overline{\rho}_{j}(\cdot)\propto\overline{\rho}(\cdot)w_{j}(\cdot)=\frac{1}{L}\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}\rho_{\tau i+1:\tau(i+1)}(\cdot)w_{j}(\cdot),

which we recall arises from mixing blocks at different positions. Writing the details explicitly, standard rejection sampling with this distribution takes the following form:

•

Generate a length-
T
instance
{\bm{x}}\sim\rho
.

•

Sample
i\sim\{0,\ldots,L-1\}
uniformly at random.

•

Query the start state
\pi^{\star}_{\tau i}({\bm{x}})
for block
i
and define the length-
\tau
instance
{\bm{x}}^{i}=(\pi^{\star}_{\tau i}({\bm{x}}),\bm{w}_{\tau i+1:\tau(i+1)})
, which is distributed according to
\rho_{\tau i+1:\tau(i+1)}
. This requires a single query to the
\texttt{iCoT}(\cdot)
oracle.

•

Draw
\eta\sim\operatorname{Unif}([0,1])
and accept if
\eta\leqslant w_{j}({\bm{x}}^{i})/\|w_{j}\|_{\infty}
.

Our first key observation is as follows: Instead of only generating the start state
{\bm{x}}^{i}
for the uniformly sampled block
i
, we can generate the start states
{\bm{x}}^{1},\ldots,{\bm{x}}^{L}

for all
L
blocks simultaneously
, at the cost of increasing the number of
\texttt{iCoT}(\cdot)
queries to
L
. Can we use this extra information to more efficiently generate a sample from the target distribution
\overline{\rho}_{j}
?

5.3
Detour: Rejection Sampling from Reweighted Mixtures of Marginals

\begin{subarray}{c}\big(T,\frac{1}{4}\big)\\
\text{Target dist: }\rho\\
\widehat{\pi}^{\circ L}\end{subarray}
\begin{subarray}{c}\big(\tau,\varepsilon_{0}\big)\\
\text{Target dist: }\overline{\rho}\\
\widehat{\pi}\leftarrow\texttt{Plu}(\{\widehat{\pi}^{k-1},\cdots,\widehat{\pi}^{0}\})\end{subarray}
\begin{subarray}{c}\Big(\tau=\tfrac{T}{L},\ \varepsilon_{0}=\tfrac{1}{4L}\end{subarray}\Big)
\begin{subarray}{c}\big(\frac{T}{L},\frac{1}{4}\big)\\[1.0pt]
\text{Target dist: }\overline{\rho}_{k-1}\\
\widehat{\pi}^{k-1}\end{subarray}
\begin{subarray}{c}\big(\frac{T}{L},\frac{1}{4}\big)\\[1.0pt]
\text{Target dist: }\overline{\rho}_{0}\\
\widehat{\pi}^{0}\end{subarray}
\begin{subarray}{c}\cdots\end{subarray}
\begin{subarray}{c}\textbf{Boosting:}\\
k\,=\,\Theta(\log(L))\\
\text{weak learners}\\[1.5pt]
\text{at length-}T/L\end{subarray}
\begin{subarray}{c}\textbf{Composition:}\\
\text{ trading-off length}\\
\text{for target error}\end{subarray}
\begin{subarray}{c}\textbf{Target:}\\
\text{Learner}\\[1.5pt]
\text{at length-}T\end{subarray}

(a)

Multiscale boosting for semiautomata. The tuple
(t,\varepsilon)
in each box indicates the length of the instances and the target error for the model trained on these instances.
\widehat{\pi}^{0},\cdots,\widehat{\pi}^{k-1}
, are combined by plurality vote to give
\widehat{\pi}
, and its
L
-fold composition is a model on length
T
instances.

s_{0}
s_{\tau}
s_{2\tau}
\dots
s_{T-\tau}
s_{T}
{\bm{x}}=(s_{0},\bm{w}_{1:T})
\texttt{iCoT}(\cdot)
\ldots
T
{\bm{x}}
L
\tau
({\bm{x}}^{0},\cdots,{\bm{x}}^{L-1})
{\bm{x}}^{i}=(s_{\tau i},\bm{w}_{\tau i+1:\tau(i+1)})

(b)

Each length-
T
instance
{\bm{x}}
can be viewed as a collection of
L
shorter instances,
({\bm{x}}^{0},\cdots,{\bm{x}}^{L-1})
, by labeling every
\tau^{\text{th}}
state (via querying the
\texttt{iCoT}(\cdot)
oracle).

Figure 3
:
Left:
Multiscale boosting.
Right:
Decomposing longer instances into shorter ones.

To highlight our goal as crisply as possible, we define an abstract setting,
sampling from reweighted mixtures of marginals
, which captures the essential structure of the problem above, but may be of interest in its own right. In this setting, we assume sampling access to a joint distribution
\mu
over potentially correlated random variables
({\bm{x}}^{0},\cdots,{\bm{x}}^{L-1})
, and aim to sample from a reweighted mixture of their marginals according to a weight function
w({\bm{x}})
. In the boosting context, these random variables correspond to the length-
\tau
segments of a length-
T
input instance, with
\mu
playing the role of
\rho
.

Definition 5.1

(Sampling from reweighted mixtures of marginals)
.

Let
\bm{X}
denote a probability space, and fix
L\in\mathbb{N}
. We are given sampling access to a joint distribution
\mu
over
\bm{X}^{L}
, and query access to a weight function
w:\bm{X}\to[0,1]
. Define
\nu(\cdot)\propto\overline{\mu}(\cdot)w(\cdot)\in\Delta_{\bm{X}}
, where
\overline{\mu}(\cdot)=\frac{1}{L}\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}\mu(X_{i}=\cdot\,)
is the uniform mixture of marginals of
\mu
. Our objective is to generate samples (approximately) from
\nu
, making as few queries to
w(\cdot)
and drawing as few joint samples from
\mu
as possible.

For this setting, standard rejection sampling takes the following form:

(a)

Draw
X=(X_{0},\cdots,X_{L-1})\sim\mu
. Sample an index
I\sim\operatorname{Unif}(\{0,\cdots,L-1\})
and select
X_{I}
. Since
I
was selected uniformly at random, the random variable
X_{I}
is sampled according to
\overline{\mu}
.

(b)

Use rejection sampling to accept/reject
X_{I}
. Namely, draw
\eta\sim\operatorname{Unif}([0,1])
and accept
X_{I}
if
\eta\leqslant w(X_{I})/\|w\|_{\infty}
(where the RHS is an upper bound on the density ratio
\nu/\overline{\mu}
). Repeat from
(a)
until a sample is accepted and declare success if a sample is accepted.

As discussed in the prequel, a standard sufficient condition for this process to accept in
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon_{0}^{-1})
attempts is that

\mathrm{Pr}_{X\sim\mu,i\sim\operatorname{Unif}(\{0,\ldots,L-1\}),\eta_{i}\sim\operatorname{Unif}([0,1])}\left(\eta_{i}\leqslant\frac{w(X_{i})}{\|w\|_{\infty}}\right)\geqslant\varepsilon_{0}.

(8)

To see why we might hope for better, consider the case where
X_{0},\ldots,X_{L-1}
are independent and identically distributed. If each
X_{i}
has acceptance probabilty
\varepsilon_{0}
marginally, then
Eq.

8
holds with parameter
\varepsilon_{0}
, and vanilla rejection sampling will indeed use
\varepsilon_{0}^{-1}
draws from
\mu
. However, this is wasteful—we are effectively throwing away
L-1
perfectly good samples—and we can improve our probability of success by a factor of
L
by simply testing and accepting/rejecting all
X_{0},\ldots,X_{L-1}
.

Motivated by this observation, a more optimistic sampling procedure should aim for the following condition:

p_{\mu,w}=\mathrm{Pr}_{X\sim\mu,\eta_{i}\overset{\text{i.i.d.}}{\sim}\operatorname{Unif}([0,1])}\left(\exists i\in\{0,\ldots,L-1\}:\eta_{i}\leqslant\frac{w(X_{i})}{\|w\|_{\infty}}\right)\geqslant\varepsilon.

(9)

This condition asserts that
counterfactually, at least one of samples
X_{i}
would have been accepted had we chosen the index
i
to begin with
. Note that we have
\varepsilon\approx\varepsilon_{0}\cdot L
in the case where
X_{i}
are independent, suggesting that the quantity in
Eq.

9
captures the easiness of this setting.

A barrier to further improvement: Hardness of sampling.

Taking advantage of the condition in
Eq.

9
is more challenging when
X_{0},\ldots,X_{L-1}
are correlated. For example, the following lemma shows that vanilla rejection sampling incurs
\Omega(L)
sample complexity even when
Eq.

9
holds with
\varepsilon=1
.

Lemma 5.2

(Informal; see
Lemma

E.6
)
.

Even when
\varepsilon=1
in
Eq.

9
, the expected number of samples from
\mu
required for rejection sampling to succeed scales with
L
.

More generally, we show now that
any approach
to generate a sample from a distribution
\widehat{\nu}
such that
\mathrm{TV}(\widehat{\nu},\nu)<\frac{1}{2}
requires drawing at least
\Omega(\mathrm{poly}(L))
samples from
\mu
in the worst case. The full version of this result (
Lemma

E.6
) holds even when
w
and
\mu
satisfy
Eq.

9
with
\varepsilon=1
; we sketch a simplified version below.

Example 5.3

(An example showcasing the hardness of sampling from
\nu
)
.

Consider the setting where
\bm{X}=\{0,1,2\}
, and the weight function
w:\bm{X}\to[0,1]
is defined as
w(z)=\mathbb{I}(z\in\{1,2\})
. Now, consider the distribution
\mu\in\Delta_{\bm{X}^{L}}
which is uniform on a set of
L+1
strings, namely,
\{(1,1,\cdots,1),(2,0,\cdots,0),(0,2,\cdots,0),\cdots,(0,0,\cdots,2)\}
. The distribution
\mu
with probability
\approx\frac{1}{L}
generates the all-
1
sequence, and with remaining probability sets a uniformly random position as
2
and the remaining positions as
0
. A simple calculation by Bayes rule shows that the distribution
\overline{\mu}
satisfies
\overline{\mu}(1)=\overline{\mu}(2)\approx\frac{1}{L}
and
\overline{\mu}(0)\approx 1-\frac{1}{L}
. Consequently,
\nu\propto\overline{\mu}(\cdot)w(\cdot)
is the uniform distribution over
\{1,2\}
.

For this example,
Eq.

9
holds with
\varepsilon=1
, since every string contains either
1
or
2
at some position. However, note the structure of the distribution
\mu
: the symbol
1
is only ever seen in an instance drawn from
\mu
with probability
\approx\frac{1}{L}
, even though this symbol is highly represented under
\nu
. Thus, any natural algorithm—including rejection sampling—to sample from
\nu
(or a nearby distribution with, say,
\mathrm{TV}\left(\widehat{\nu},\nu\right)\leqslant\frac{1}{2}
) requires drawing
\Omega(L)
samples from
\mu
.

Thus, in the worst case, sampling (even very approximately) from
\overline{\rho}_{j}
cannot be carried out with fewer than
\mathrm{poly}(L)
samples from
\rho
. This makes it unlikely to improve the sample and query complexity by developing better methods for sampling from the reweighted mixture of marginals (
Definition

5.1
) under standard notions of distribution approximation (say, TV-distance or KL divergence).

5.4
Inverted Sampling: More Efficient Sampling from Reweighted Mixtures

Recall that standard rejection sampling—when applied to the problem of sampling from reweighted mixtures of marginals—samples a shorter instance from each longer instance uniformly at random, and checks whether it passes a filter. The remaining
L-1
sub-instances contained within are thrown away. This is inherently wasteful since one of the remaining (unselected) instances may have passed the filter, but the index of the uniformly random instance selected by rejection sampling will only “hit” this index with small probability
1/L
. A natural algorithm which does not suffer from this issue reverses the order of operands: first accept/reject each of the
L
shorter instances by applying rejection sampling independently, then select one of the accepted instances uniformly at random. We refer to this approach as
inverted sampling
, implemented as follows:

(a)

Draw
X=(X_{0},\cdots,X_{L-1})\sim\mu
. Let
\mathcal{I}
denote the set of indices,

\left\{i\in\{0,\ldots,L-1\}:\eta_{i}\leqslant w(X_{i})/\|w\|_{\infty}\right\}

Here
\eta_{i}\sim\operatorname{Unif}([0,1])
independently across
i
. If
\mathcal{I}=\emptyset
, reject and repeat from
(a)
.

(b)

Let
I
be an index drawn uniformly at random from
\mathcal{I}
. Return
X_{I}
.

The benefit of inverted sampling is that it is guaranteed to accept as long as at least one of the
X_{i}
’s passes the filter; this is much less pessimistic than rejection sampling, which may fail to select this
X_{i}
to filter in the first place. However, this approach is still subject to the strong sampling lower bound discussed in
Example

5.3
, and so the increased acceptance rate must come at a large cost in the TV distance to
\nu
.

In spite of the TV distance lower bound, we show that inverted sampling can be used to sample from a distribution which is close to
\nu
in the sense of a weaker notion of approximation, which is nonetheless sufficient for our application within
AutoLearn
.

Lemma 5.4

(Informal; see
Lemmas

E.5
and
E.3
)
.

Fix a constant
c\in(0,1)
. Let
\widehat{\nu}
denote the distribution over instances accepted by inverted sampling. There exists an event
\mathcal{K}\subseteq\bm{X}^{L}\times[0,1]^{L}
which is a measurable function of
(X,\bm{\eta})\sim\mu\times\operatorname{Unif}([0,1])^{\otimes L}
where
\bm{\eta}=(\eta_{i})_{i=0}^{L-1}
, such that
\mathrm{Pr}((X,\bm{\eta})\in\mathcal{K})\geqslant 1-c
, and the following density ratio bound is satisfied,

\left\|\frac{\nu_{\mathcal{K}}(\cdot)}{\widehat{\nu}(\cdot)}\right\|_{\infty}\leqslant\mathcal{O}(c^{-1}\cdot\log(L))

(10)

Here,
\nu_{\mathcal{K}}(\cdot)\propto\frac{1}{L}\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}\mathrm{Pr}(X_{i}=\cdot\,|\,(X,\bm{\eta})\in\mathcal{K})\,w(\cdot)
is the reweighted mixture distribution of marginals when the underlying sample and random coins,
(X,\bm{\eta})
, are conditioned on the event
\mathcal{K}
. Finally, the marginal probability that inverted sampling accepts a sample
X\sim\mu
is
p_{\mu,w}
, defined in
Eq.

9
.

That is, by shifting our target to the distribution
\nu_{\mathcal{K}}
—where
\mathcal{K}
is an appropriate high-probability event defined formally in
Eq.

66
—we can sample from a distribution
\widehat{\nu}
that approximates the target in the density ratio sense up to only a
\log(L)
factor (as opposed to polynomial dependence on
L
), and achieve an acceptance probability that matches the skyline in
Eq.

9
. This result sidesteps the hardness of sampling (
Lemma

E.6
) because the distribution approximated by inverted sampling,
\nu_{\mathcal{K}}
, can be very different from
\nu
. Nonetheless, because
\mathcal{K}
has high probability, this guarantee is sufficient for our application within
AutoLearn
.

How does
Lemma

5.4
sidestep the hard sampling instance of
Example

5.3
?

In order to better understand this result, we put it into context of the hard instance in
Example

5.3
. For the construction in the example, if we define
\mathcal{K}
as
\{X\neq(1,\cdots,1)\}
, we see that
\mathrm{Pr}(\mathcal{K})\geqslant 1-\frac{1}{L}
is a high probability event. This conditioning on
\mathcal{K}
has a dramatic effect on the resulting distribution
\nu_{\mathcal{K}}
: since the all-
1
sequence is the only sequence which contains the symbol
1
, conditioning on
\mathcal{K}
results in
\nu_{\mathcal{K}}
becoming the delta distribution on the symbol
2
. This is a very different target from
\nu=\operatorname{Unif}(\{1,2\})
, and sampling from
\nu_{\mathcal{K}}
no longer presents the same pathologies of sampling from
\nu
.

5.5
Inverted Sampling Achieves
n_{\texttt{sample}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(d)
and
n_{\texttt{query}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(d\sqrt{T})

We now return to the context of
AutoLearn
, where we use inverted sampling to implement boosting at the length-
\tau
scale. Let
\sigma
denote the joint distribution over the
L
length-
\tau
instances obtained by labeling the boundary states on
{\bm{x}}\sim\rho
. In each iteration
j
of the boosting process, we apply inverted sampling guarantees to draw instances from a distribution that
covers

\lambda_{j}
, which is the distribution proportional to
\overline{\sigma_{\mathcal{K}_{j}}}(\cdot)\,w_{j}(\cdot)
where
\overline{\sigma_{\mathcal{K}_{j}}}=\frac{1}{L}\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}\mathrm{Pr}(X_{i}=\cdot\mid(X,\bm{\eta})\in\mathcal{K}_{j})
for
X\sim\sigma
, where
\mathcal{K}_{j}
denotes the high probability event defined in
Lemma

5.4
for an appropriate choice of
c
. The event
\mathcal{K}_{j}
in the boosting context has an intuitive description: Associating
X_{i}={\bm{x}}^{i}
for
i=0,\cdots,L-1
, an instance
X=(X_{0},\cdots,X_{L-1})
is
likely
to belong to
\mathcal{K}_{j}
(with a slight abuse of notation, we define this to mean
\mathrm{Pr}((X,\bm{\eta})\in\mathcal{K}_{j}\mid X)
is large) if, effectively, on some non-empty subset of
X_{i}
’s, the first
j-1
models all tend to make mistakes together. On such instances, querying the label on any of the erroneous
X_{i}
’s is maximally informative for the learner, as it corrects the mistakes of many of the existing models. Furthermore, if it appears challenging to sample an
X
such that any of the
X_{i}
’s are erroneous, i.e.,
p_{\mu,w}
is small, this is an indication that the weak learners are already good, since they agree on the correct label on all
X_{i}
’s.

It remains to argue the conditioning on the event
\mathcal{K}_{j}
does not significantly influence the overall accuracy of the aggregated weak learners. For this, if
\mathcal{K}_{j}
is constructed choosing
c\leqslant\frac{1}{8k}
in
Lemma

5.4
, we are guaranteed that the probability of
\mathcal{K}_{j}^{c}
under
\rho
is at most
c\leqslant\frac{1}{8k}
. Viewing
\mathcal{K}_{j}^{c}
as a “failure region” and accounting for the probability mass which falls in these regions separately, we are guaranteed that
\cup_{j=0}^{k-1}\mathcal{K}_{j}^{c}
has probability at most
ck\leqslant\frac{1}{8}
, which is within the total error budget of
\frac{1}{4}
.

The analysis sketched here requires some subtle additional conditioning arguments to make everything go through, and we defer the discussion of these details to
Section

B.1
. Overall, by using inverted sampling to appropriately define the target weak learning distributions, we obtain an algorithm with the following query and sample complexity guarantees:

n_{\texttt{query}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(d\tau\cdot\mathrm{polylog}(L)+d/\varepsilon_{0}\right),\quad\text{and},\quad n_{\texttt{sample}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\big(d\cdot\mathrm{polylog}(L)\big)

Where
\varepsilon_{0}=\mathcal{O}(1/L)
. The
d/\varepsilon_{0}
term in the query complexity comes from the cost of using inverted sampling to generate
\mathcal{O}(d)
samples for the full-CoT learners at the length-
\tau
scale, while the
d\tau\cdot\mathrm{polylog}(L)
term comes from labeling all
\tau
intermediate states on the instances that are selected by inverted sampling (to train full-CoT learners via next-token prediction).
Optimizing over
L
, we get an algorithm with query complexity scaling as
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(d\sqrt{T})
, but with an
improved sample complexity of
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(d)
compared to the rejection sampling based approach described in
Section

5.1
. This improvement shows that it is possible to achieve sublinear-in-
T
query complexity without paying for a comparable blowup in sample complexity.

For more detail, we refer the reader to
Appendix

B
, which presents full pseudocode for a simplified version of
AutoLearn
that uses a single level of recursion, along with a complete proof that it acheives
n_{\texttt{sample}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(d)
and
n_{\texttt{query}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(d\sqrt{T})
.

5.6
2^{\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\sqrt{\log(T)})}
Query and Sample Complexity via Deeper Recursion

The depth-
1
algorithm described above uses a full-CoT learner (i.e., vanilla next-token prediction) to weakly learn
\pi^{\star}_{\tau}
for each distribution over length-
\tau
instances,
\widehat{\rho}_{j}
. Each invocation of this weak learning algorithm requires
\Omega(\tau)
queries. To achieve the main guarantee for the full version of
AutoLearn
in
Theorem

2.7
, we use a deeper recursion in which the weak learner at the length-
\tau
scale is itself an instantiation of the depth-
1
algorithm in the prequel (for
T=\tau
). We iterate this recursion over multiple levels, decomposing instances into smaller and smaller sub-instances, with the length decreasing by a factor of
L
at each level. We refer the reader back to
Figure

3(a)
for the overall structure of the algorithm, noting that the learner sets
\varepsilon_{0}=\mathcal{O}\big(\frac{1}{L}\big)
at each depth.

In the approach above, the depth of the recursion
H
and the decomposition factor
L
are free parameters, but need to satisfy
T=L^{H}
to ensure that the deepest level of recursion (i.e., depth
0
) corresponds to solving length-
1
problems (which is achieved through fitting a next-token predictor). The analysis of inverted sampling we worked out in
Lemma

5.4
plays a key role in analyzing the sample complexity and query complexity of the resulting algorithm. In particular, when we extend the same analysis to deeper levels of recursion, at any depth
h\leqslant H
(corresponding to solving instances of length
T/L^{H-h}
), generating samples from the appropriate weak learning distribution at this depth requires drawing
(\log(L))^{\mathcal{O}(H-h)}
examples from
\rho
. In particular, this implies that the total sample complexity of the algorithm scales as
n_{\texttt{sample}}=d\cdot(\log(L))^{\mathcal{O}(H)}
, noting that we require
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(d)
samples at the deepest level to train a model via next-token prediction to achieve constant accuracy. On the other hand, the overall query complexity of the algorithm scales as
n_{\texttt{query}}\leqslant LHn_{\texttt{sample}}
: at each depth
h
of the algorithm, inverted sampling subselects a single length-
T/L^{H-h+1}
instance from a length
T/L^{H-h}
-length instance. This means that on each input in the dataset, inverted sampling makes
LH

iCoT
queries, leading to the following bound on the overall query complexity:

n_{\texttt{sample}}=d\cdot(\log(L))^{\mathcal{O}(H)}\text{ and }n_{\texttt{query}}=LH\cdot d\cdot(\log(L))^{\mathcal{O}(H)}

While the sample complexity alone is minimized with
H=1
and
L=T
, the query complexity is large in this regime, scaling as
\mathcal{O}(dT)
. However, by choosing
L
and
H
differently, both quantities can be made to scale subpolynomially in
T
. In particular, with
L=2^{\sqrt{\log T}}
(which corresponds to
H=\sqrt{\log T}
to ensure
L^{H}=T
), we balance the
L
and
(\log(L))^{\mathcal{O}(H)}
terms in the query complexity, which both scale as
2^{2^{2}_{1}}
2^{\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\sqrt{\log(T)})}
. With this choice, both the sample and query complexity scale as
2^{2^{2}_{1}}
2^{\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\sqrt{\log T})}\cdot d
, yielding
Theorem

2.7
.

6
Discussion

This work asks how
curriculum
and
composition
let a learner solve problems far beyond the reach of its base capabilities—a mechanism widely credited for the success of modern reasoning models, yet one with little theoretical foundation. Adopting semiautomaton simulation as a testbed, our results show that composition combined with curriculum can achieve superpolynomial reductions in supervision and computational cost relative to non-curriculum baselines, in both supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR). We close by discussing the limitations of our results, followed by several concrete technical questions they leave open.

6.1
Simplifications in the Problems Formulation

While our work is motivated by language model reasoning, our problem setting makes several simplifications; relaxing these is an important direction for future work.

Non-Markovian models.

Our results use the Markovian structure of semiautomata simulation task to decompose the problem into shorter sub-problems. However, our results also require that the
model we train
is Markovian, which rules out classes like Transformers that can attend to the full history. It is an interesting question to understand how to extend our results to settings in which the underlying transition function is still Markovian, but the model we train may not be.

Stochastic transition functions.

Our main results apply for semiautomata transition functions, which are inherently deterministic. However, in many natural settings, including language model reasoning, it is natural to consider stochastic transition functions
\pi:S\times\Sigma\to\Delta_{S}
. This encompasses Markov chains, and other (controlled) stochastic processes, for which there is a rich history of work studying learnability
(
6
;
24
;
20
)
. A natural objective is to return a terminal-state distribution
\widehat{\pi}:(S\times\Sigma^{T})\to\Delta_{S}
which minimizes some divergence to the ground-truth terminal state distribution
\pi^{\star}_{T}
.
12
12

12

we will use
\pi^{\star}_{T}(\cdot|{\bm{x}})
to denote the marginal distribution of the terminal state generated by
\pi^{\star}
on the instance
{\bm{x}}
.
When the learning objective is the (forward) KL divergence,
\mathbb{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim\rho}\big[D_{\mathrm{KL}}\big(\pi^{\star}_{T}(\cdot|{\bm{x}})\,\big\|\,\widehat{\pi}(\cdot|{\bm{x}})\big)\big]
, the idea of composition is still functional, as suggested by the chain rule for KL divergences.

Proposition 6.1
.

For any
\tau:T\pmod{\tau}\equiv 0
and
L=\frac{T}{\tau}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim\rho}\big[D_{\mathrm{KL}}\big(\pi^{\star}_{T}(\cdot|{\bm{x}})\,\big\|\,\widehat{\pi}^{\circ L}(\cdot|{\bm{x}})\big)\big]\leqslant L\cdot\mathbb{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim\overline{\rho}}\big[D_{\mathrm{KL}}\big(\pi^{\star}_{\tau}(\cdot|{\bm{x}})\,\big\|\,\widehat{\pi}(\cdot|{\bm{x}})\big)\big]

It is worth pointing out that this type of decomposition does not necessarily translate to other divergences (TV or Hellinger distances) which do not satisfy the chain rule. Furthermore, estimating whether
\pi_{t}(\cdot|{\bm{x}})\approx\pi^{\star}_{t}(\cdot|{\bm{x}})
for some model
\pi
(to decide whether an instance
{\bm{x}}
is “solved” or not), also requires observing the probability
\pi^{\star}_{t}(y|{\bm{x}})
for
y\sim\pi^{\star}_{t}(\cdot|{\bm{x}})
, which may require considering other observation models which go beyond just observing states
(
35
)
.

6.2
Technical Questions

Our results leave open several interesting technical questions, including tightening our sample complexity and computational guarantees, as well as further relaxing the coverage assumptions in our RLVR results. Below, we highlight a particularly interesting question: Improving the
T
dependence in our iSFT results.

6.2.1
Optimality of
T
Dependency in
Theorem

3.4

Our main result in the SFT setting establishes query and sample complexity scaling
2^{\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\sqrt{\log T})}
for learning semiautomata. Understanding whether there are statistical or computational barriers to improving this further to achieve
polylogarithmic
dependency on
T
appears to be a fascinating and deep question.
\log(T)
query complexity is a natural endpoint for divide-and-conquer approaches like
AutoLearn
, but our current results fall short due to additional complications around error accumulation. Below we show a special case where
\mathrm{polylog}(T)
is indeed achievable under stronger assumptions on the class
\Pi
than i.i.d. learnability: namely that it admits a finite mistake bound. We define the relevant notation below.

Definition 6.2

(Mistake-bounded online learning for
\Pi

(
34
)
)
.

Consider the following online learning game between an algorithm
\texttt{Alg}_{\mathrm{on}}
and an adversary. The adversary fixes a transition function
\pi^{\star}\in\Pi
, and at each step
i=1,\ldots,N
: (1) the adversary adaptively selects a state-letter pair
(s^{i},w^{i})\in S\times\Sigma
(possibly depending on all prior predictions of
\texttt{Alg}_{\mathrm{on}}
), (2) the algorithm predicts
\widehat{s}^{i}_{+}\in S
based on all prior observations, and (3) the true next-state
\pi^{\star}(s^{i},w^{i})
is revealed. The worst-case number of mistakes of
\texttt{Alg}_{\mathrm{on}}
is defined as,

M_{N}(\texttt{Alg}_{\mathrm{on}})=\sup_{\pi^{\star}\in\Pi}\;\sup\;\sum\nolimits_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{I}\big(\widehat{s}^{i}_{+}\neq\pi^{\star}(s^{i},w^{i})\big)

where the inner supremum is over all adaptive adversary strategies.
\Pi
admits a finite mistake bound if there exists an online learning algorithm
\texttt{Alg}_{\mathrm{on}}
such that
\sup_{N\geqslant 1}M_{N}(\texttt{Alg}_{\mathrm{on}})\triangleq M^{\star}(\texttt{Alg}_{\mathrm{on}})<\infty
.

Remark 6.3
.

A finite mistake bound for
\Pi
is a stronger assumption than i.i.d. learnability. When
S=\{0,1\}
and
\Sigma
is arbitrary, i.i.d. learnability is equivalent to the finiteness of the VC dimension of
\Pi
. A finite mistake bound is equivalent to the finiteness of the Littlestone dimension,
\mathrm{Ldim}(\Pi)

(
34
)
, which is no smaller than the VC dimension. For simple classes such as thresholds,
\mathrm{VC}(\Pi)=1
, while
\mathrm{Ldim}(\Pi)
can scale with the size of the domain of the functions.

Assuming that
\Pi
admits a finite mistake bound, we can improve the sample and query complexity of learning semiautomata to achieve logarithmic dependence on
T
.

Theorem 6.4
.

Let
\texttt{Alg}_{\mathrm{on}}
denote an online learning algorithm with mistake bound
M^{\star}(\texttt{Alg}_{\mathrm{on}})<\infty
. Consider any
\varepsilon\in[0,1]
. There exists an algorithm (
Algorithm

10
) which uses
\texttt{Alg}_{\mathrm{on}}
as a subroutine; the randomized model
\widehat{\pi}
returned by this algorithm satisfies,

\mathbb{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim\rho,y_{T}\sim\widehat{\pi}(\cdot|{\bm{x}})}\big[\mathbb{I}(y_{T}\neq\pi^{\star}_{T}({\bm{x}}))\big]\leqslant\varepsilon,

as long as
n_{\texttt{sample}}\geqslant\Theta\big(M^{\star}(\texttt{Alg}_{\mathrm{on}})\cdot\varepsilon^{-1}\big)
. The query complexity is
n_{\texttt{query}}=\Theta\big(n_{\texttt{sample}}\cdot\log(T)\big)
.

If
\texttt{Alg}_{\mathrm{on}}
is instantiated as the standard optimal algorithm
(
34
)
, we achieve sample complexity
\Theta\big(d\log(|S||\Sigma|)\cdot\varepsilon^{-1}\big)
, where
d
denotes the Littlestone dimension of
\Pi
, and query complexity at most a
\mathcal{O}(\log(T))
factor larger.

The proof of this result is given in
Section

F.8
.

Appendix Contents

Appendix Organization

In
Appendix

A
presents additional related work which studies the problem of learning automata; in
Section

A.1
we discuss a concrete example illustrating the semiautomaton induced by a regular expression.
Appendix

B
gives a self-contained treatment of
\texttt{AutoLearn}.\texttt{warmup}
, which is a simplified, depth-
\frac{1}{2}
version of
AutoLearn
.
Appendix

C
lifts this analysis to the full recursive variant of
AutoLearn
and proves
Theorem

3.4
, while
Section

D.3
analyzes
\texttt{AutoLearn}.\texttt{RL}
and proves
Theorem

4.2
. The remaining appendices collect the supporting technical material:
Appendix

E
proves the main lemmas used in the analysis of
\texttt{AutoLearn}.\texttt{warmup}
, and
Appendix

F
gives auxiliary results, including the non-curriculum lower bounds, the potential and density-ratio calculations, the sampling lower bound, and the discussion-section results on online learning and stochastic model classes.

Appendix A
Additional Related Work

Semiautomata are closely related to
automata
, which have a rich history in theoretical computer science
[
25
]
. In learning theory specifically, automata have been the subject of a rich line of work, originating with Angluin’s seminal
L^{\star}
algorithm
[
3
,
41
,
17
,
30
,
14
]
. The two models differ only in that automata define languages over words
\bm{w}\in\Sigma^{*}
based on state evolution and an acceptance function, whereas semiautomata only define state evolution. This distinction is salient when considering the task of PAC learning: for automata, the learner only accesses the acceptance decisions of the underlying system, while we consider learning semiautomata from feedback that reveals the state evolution more directly.

Most of the literature on learning automata has focused on settings where the state is completely unobserved, and the learner must infer about it through accept/reject decisions. Most closely related to the setting considered in this paper are the works of
1
and
2
who consider learning settings where terminal states are either fully or partially observable. The recent work of
21
studies the statistical query complexity of PAC learning semiautomata from random examples, and establish strong SQ hardness for this task.

A.1
An example of a semiautomaton induced by a regular expression

In this section we expand upon the regular language example in
Section

3.5
, by giving a detailed example of a regular expression with alphabetic length
k
and induced semiautomaton. Consider the regular expression
\texttt{RegEx}\equiv(a|b)^{*}ab
over
\Sigma=\{a,b\}
. This corresponds to the set of all strings which terminate in
ab
. The alphabetic length of
RegEx
is
4
, with symbol occurrences
a_{1},b_{2},a_{3},b_{4}
, where
a_{1}
and
b_{2}
correspond to occurrences in the subexpression
(a|b)^{*}
, while
a_{3}
and
b_{4}
correspond to occurrences in the final
ab
term. Each regular expression can be compiled into a non-deterministic finite automaton, as we discuss next.

Definition A.1

(Non-deterministic finite automaton)
.

Fix an input vocabulary
\Sigma
and a finite state space
Q
. A nondeterministic finite automaton (NFA) is defined by a transition function
f:Q\times\Sigma\to 2^{Q}
, which defines a set of states reachable from the current state for each input symbol, and a subset of states
Q_{\texttt{acc}}\subseteq Q
known as the accepting states. Given an initial state
q_{0}\in Q
and an input sequence
\bm{w}_{1:T}\in\Sigma^{T}
, the NFA realizes the sequences,
(\bm{q}_{t})_{t=0}^{T}
, where
\bm{q}_{0}=\{q_{0}\}
and
\bm{q}_{t}=\cup_{q\in\bm{q}_{t-1}}f(q,w_{t})
captures all states reachable from
q_{0}
under the input sequence
\bm{w}_{1:t}
. The NFA accepts the input string
\bm{w}_{1:T}
if
Q_{\texttt{acc}}\cap\bm{q}_{T}\neq\emptyset
.

22
shows how to write down an automaton corresponding to a regular language by associating one state for each symbol occurrence, as well as an initial state
0
. Namely, the state space of the Glushkov NFA is
Q=\{0,\cdots,4\}
. The NFA is initialized at the state
0
; when the first letter of an input word is processed, the subsequent state can be either
a_{1}\equiv 1
,
b_{2}\equiv 2
or
a_{3}\equiv 3
. The (set-valued) transition function of the corresponding NFA,
\pi^{\texttt{NFA}}
, is described by the following map:

\displaystyle\pi^{\texttt{NFA}}(0,a)=\pi^{\texttt{NFA}}(1,a)=\pi^{\texttt{NFA}}(2,a)

\displaystyle=\{1,3\}

\displaystyle\pi^{\texttt{NFA}}(0,b)=\pi^{\texttt{NFA}}(1,b)=\pi^{\texttt{NFA}}(2,b)

\displaystyle=\{2\}

\displaystyle\pi^{\texttt{NFA}}(3,b)

\displaystyle=\{4\}

\displaystyle\text{For all other }(s,w)\in Q\times\Sigma,\ \pi^{\texttt{NFA}}(s,w)

\displaystyle=\emptyset

An input word is accepted by
\pi^{\texttt{NFA}}
if there exists a computational path which terminates in the state
4
.
\pi^{\texttt{NFA}}
can be translated into a DFA
\pi^{\texttt{DFA}}
using powerset determinization, which tracks the set of all possible states reachable by some computational path in the NFA. Thus, the states in
\pi^{\texttt{DFA}}
correspond to subsets
S\subseteq Q
. For example, on the input word
\bm{w}=(b,a,a,b)
, the subset-state trajectory is:

\{0\}\xrightarrow{b}\{2\}\xrightarrow{a}\{1,3\}\xrightarrow{a}\{1,3\}\xrightarrow{b}\{2,4\}.

\pi^{\texttt{DFA}}
accepts the subset state as long as the final returned set contains any of the accepting state in
\pi^{\texttt{NFA}}
, i.e.,
4
. Thus
(b,a,a,b)
is accepted because its terminal subset is
\{2,4\}
, which contains the accepting position
4
. This is correct as
RegEx
recognizes the set of strings which end in
ab
.

Appendix B
Analyzing a Simpler Variant of
Algorithm

1
:
\texttt{AutoLearn}.\texttt{warmup}

This section presents a complete description and analysis for the simplified version of
AutoLearn
sketched in
Section

5
, which uses only a single level of recursion. The algorithm, which we denote by
\texttt{AutoLearn}.\texttt{warmup}
, decomposes length-
T
instances into length-
\tau
ones, where
\tau=T/L
and
L>1
is a parameter to be specified. Then, a sequence of models
\widehat{\pi}^{0},\cdots,\widehat{\pi}^{k-1}
are trained to solve these length-
\tau
instances, which are then aggregated using majority voting and composed
L
-fold to get a model which solves length-
T
instances. Each
\widehat{\pi}^{j}
is trained by querying full CoTs on length-
\tau
instances and carrying out next-token prediction (
Definition

2.2
), which we refer to as
\texttt{NTP}_{\texttt{Full-CoT}}
(
Algorithm

9
). A pictorial depiction is provided in
Figure

4
, and a formal description of
\texttt{AutoLearn}.\texttt{warmup}
is provided within
Algorithm

7
.

The purpose of analyzing this variant of
AutoLearn
is to expose and understand the mechanism by which inverted sampling (
Algorithm

3
) converts longer length-
T
instances (i.e., the dataset
D_{\mathrm{input}}
) into shorter ones of length
\tau=T/L
(i.e., the datasets
D_{\mathrm{out}}^{j}
for
j=0,\cdots,k-1
). Our main guarantee, established below, shows that
\texttt{AutoLearn}.\texttt{warmup}
achieves sample complexity scaling as
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(d)
and query complexity
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(d\sqrt{T})
in order to achieve constant error on length-
T
instances.

Theorem B.1

(Guarantee for
\texttt{AutoLearn}.\texttt{warmup}
)
.

\texttt{AutoLearn}.\texttt{warmup}
(
Algorithm

7
) with
L=\sqrt{T}
draws a total of,

n_{\texttt{sample}}=\mathcal{O}\big(d\log(|S|)\log(1/\delta)\cdot\log^{9}(T)\big)

instances from
\rho
, and makes
n_{\texttt{query}}=\mathcal{O}(n_{\texttt{sample}}\sqrt{T})
calls to the
\texttt{iCoT}(\cdot)
oracle to return a model
\widehat{\pi}
such that with probability
1-\delta
,

\mathrm{Pr}_{{\bm{x}}\sim\rho}\big[\widehat{\pi}({\bm{x}})\neq\pi^{\star}_{T}({\bm{x}})\big]\leqslant\frac{1}{4}

Proof.
The proof sketch of this result and all necessary lemmas are introduced in
Section

B.1
. The final proof invoking these lemmas is presented in
Section

B.3
.
∎
