B.1
Proof Sketch of
Theorem

B.1

In this section, we sketch the proof of
Theorem

B.1
, first introducing some relevant notation. We will let
\widehat{\pi}
denote the model returned by
Algorithm

7
.

Notation.

Recall the definition of
\rho_{t_{1}:t_{2}}
which is the distribution of
(s_{t_{1}-1},\bm{w}_{t_{1}:t_{2}})
for
(s_{0},\bm{w}_{1:T})={\bm{x}}\sim\rho
and where
s_{t_{1}-1}=\pi^{\star}_{t_{1}-1}({\bm{x}})
. Let
\sigma
denote the joint distribution of
\underline{X}=(X_{0},\cdots,X_{L-1})
, where each
X_{i}=(s_{t},\bm{w}_{t+1:t+\tau})
, for
t=\tau i
and
{\bm{x}}=(s_{0},\bm{w}_{1:T})\sim\rho
. Note that
\underline{X}\sim\sigma
is a measurable function of the initial state
s_{0}
and the word
\bm{w}_{1:T}
, and can be constructed from the instance
{\bm{x}}=(s_{0},\bm{w}_{1:T})\sim\rho
by querying
\texttt{iCoT}(\cdot)
at
L
timesteps (
t=\tau,2\tau,\cdots,T
). Let
\bm{X}=S\times\Sigma^{\tau}
denote the space of length-
\tau
instances. Note that the support of
\sigma
is
\bm{X}^{L}
.
Note that the distributions
\sigma
and
\rho
, while closely related, are different from one another.
We also define the filtration
\mathcal{H}_{j-1}
which captures all the randomness up until the point where the dataset
D_{\mathrm{out}}^{j}
is collected in iteration
j
(i.e., the randomness in the construction of the models
\widehat{\pi}^{0},\cdots,\widehat{\pi}^{j-1}
and the randomness in the instances
{\bm{x}}\sim\rho
which contribute to the datasets
D_{\mathrm{out}}^{0},\cdots,D_{\mathrm{out}}^{j-1}
). In particular,
\mathcal{H}_{k-1}
denotes all the random variables involved in the execution of
\texttt{AutoLearn}.\texttt{warmup}
.

With this, we let
\overline{\sigma}
denote the distribution
\frac{1}{L}\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}\sigma(X_{i}=\cdot\,)
, which is the uniform mixture over marginals of
\sigma
, which is identical to
\overline{\rho}
. Thus, our earlier discussion in
3.2
demonstrating the central role played by
\overline{\rho}
in enabling compositional generalization applies to
\overline{\sigma}
as well. All-in-all, the purpose of introducing additional notation in the form of
\sigma
is to make it easier to write down the distributions over length-
\tau
instances on which the weak learners
\widehat{\pi}^{j}
are trained.

First, we define a sequence of distributions,
\{\overline{\rho}_{j}\}_{j=0}^{k-1}
supported on
\bm{X}
obtained by applying a tilt to
\overline{\sigma}
(or equivalently
\overline{\rho}
),

\displaystyle\overline{\rho}_{j}(\cdot)

\displaystyle\propto\Big(\frac{1}{L}\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}\sigma(X_{i}=\cdot\,)\Big)w_{j}(\cdot)=\overline{\sigma}(\cdot)\ w_{j}(\cdot)

(11)

where
w_{j}(\cdot)
is the rank-based weight function defined in
Eq.

2
.
\overline{\rho}_{j}
is the idealized “good distribution” under which the weak model in iteration
j
should be trained as prescribed by boosting-by-filtering
[
19
]
. Sampling from
\overline{\rho}_{j}
is prohibitive, a point which we discuss more in
Section

E.5
. To this end, we define an alternate sequence of distributions which are easier to sample from, denoted
\{\lambda_{j}\}_{j=0}^{k-1}
,

\displaystyle\lambda_{j}(\cdot)

\displaystyle\propto\overline{\sigma_{\mathcal{K}_{j}}}(\cdot)\ w_{j}(\cdot)\text{, where, }\overline{\sigma_{\mathcal{K}_{j}}}(\cdot)=\Big(\frac{1}{L}\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}\mathrm{Pr}\big(X_{i}=\cdot\mid(\underline{X},\bm{\eta}_{0:j})\in\mathcal{K}_{j}\text{ and }\mathcal{H}_{j-1}\big)\Big)

(12)

where the probability is calculated under
\underline{X}=(X_{i})_{i=0}^{L-1}\sim\sigma
and
\bm{\eta}_{j}=(\eta_{j,i})_{i=0}^{L-1}\sim\operatorname{Unif}([0,1])^{\otimes L}
, is a set of i.i.d. uniform random variables, with
\bm{\eta}_{0:j}=(\bm{\eta}_{j^{\prime}})_{j^{\prime}=0}^{j}
and
\bm{\eta}=\bm{\eta}_{0:k-1}
. This definition hinges on the sequence of “good” events
(\mathcal{K}_{j})_{j=0}^{k-1}
, such that
\mathcal{K}_{0}\subseteq\bm{X}^{L}\times[0,1]^{(j+1)L}
, which we defer the definition of to later in
Eq.

21
. Finally, define the sequence of random variables
\{R_{j}\}_{j=0}^{k-1}
which are measurable functions of
\underline{X}\sim\sigma
,

R_{j}=\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}w_{j}(X_{i}),\text{ where, }w_{j}(X_{i})=\alpha^{j,k}_{\mathrm{rank}_{j}(X_{i})}\text{ and }\mathrm{rank}_{j}(X_{i})=|\{\widehat{\pi}(X_{i})=\pi^{\star}_{\tau}(X_{i}):\widehat{\pi}\in\Pi_{j}\}|,

(13)

where
\alpha^{j,k}_{r}
is defined in
Eq.

2
.
R_{j}
will be referred to as the
regression
in iteration
j
. The name “regression” is used to denote the fact that
R_{j}
is a measure of how much the accuracy of the plurality of the models
\widehat{\pi}^{0},\cdots,\widehat{\pi}^{j-1}
can possibly worsen by, when the new model
\widehat{\pi}^{j}
is added to the ensemble.

Additional Notation.

In order to state our results succinctly, we introduce some additional notation to make it easier to write down expressions involving events on different spaces. In particular for events
\mathcal{A}
defined on
\bm{X}^{L}\times[0,1]^{k_{A}L}
and
\mathcal{B}
defined on
\bm{X}^{L}\times[0,1]^{k_{B}L}
with
k_{A}\geqslant k_{B}
, we can define intersections by considering the smallest subset of
(\underline{X},\bm{\eta})
with respect to which both events are measurable. Namely,

\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{B}\iff\big\{(\underline{X},\bm{\eta}_{0:k_{A}-1}):(\underline{X},\bm{\eta}_{0:k_{A}-1})\in\mathcal{A}\text{ and }(\underline{X},\bm{\eta}_{0:k_{B}-1})\in\mathcal{B}\big\}.

(14)

Similarly, for a third event
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\bm{X}^{L}\times[0,1]^{k_{C}L}
with
k_{C}\geqslant k_{A}
, containment statements of the form,

\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{B}\iff\mathcal{C}\subseteq\big\{(\underline{X},\bm{\eta}_{0:k_{C}-1}):(\underline{X},\bm{\eta}_{0:k_{A}-1})\in\mathcal{A}\text{ and }(\underline{X},\bm{\eta}_{0:k_{B}-1})\in\mathcal{B}\big\}.

(15)

are defined by defining both sides with respect to the minimal subset of
(\underline{X},\bm{\eta})
with respect to which all events are measurable. A similar logic can be used to parse statements of the form
(\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\in\mathcal{K}_{j}
by viewing the LHS as the singleton event in
\bm{X}^{L}\times[0,1]^{kL}
and defining membership via containment:
\{(\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\}\subseteq\mathcal{K}_{j}
.

We first decompose the test error of the model
\widehat{\pi}
returned by
\texttt{AutoLearn}.\texttt{warmup}
by a potential function argument, to track how the test error of the aggregated ensemble improves as more models are added.

Lemma B.2

(Test-error decomposition)
.

Let
\widehat{\pi}
denote the model returned by
\texttt{AutoLearn}.\texttt{warmup}
. Then, for a sequence of events
\mathcal{K}_{k-1}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq\mathcal{K}_{0}\subseteq\bm{X}^{L}\times[0,1]^{kL}
, the prediction error of
\widehat{\pi}
under
\rho
can be upper bounded as,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}_{{\bm{x}}\sim\rho}\big[\widehat{\pi}({\bm{x}})\neq\pi^{\star}_{T}({\bm{x}})\big]

\displaystyle\leqslant L\cdot\beta^{0,k}_{0}+\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}(\texttt{err}_{j}-\texttt{err}_{\star})\cdot\mathbb{E}[R_{j}\cdot\mathbb{I}((\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\in\mathcal{K}_{j})\mid\mathcal{H}_{k-1}]+\mathrm{Pr}((\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\not\in\mathcal{K}_{k-1}\mid\mathcal{H}_{k-1})

Recall that,
\beta^{j,k}_{r}
is the weight function defined across
Eqs.

2
and
3
,
\texttt{err}_{\star}=\frac{1}{4}
, while
\texttt{err}_{j}
is the test error of
\widehat{\pi}^{j}
under the distribution
\lambda_{j}\propto\overline{\sigma_{\mathcal{K}_{j}}}(\cdot)w_{j}(\cdot)
where
w_{j}
is defined in
Eq.

13
,

\texttt{err}_{j}=\mathrm{Pr}_{X\sim\lambda_{j}}\big[\widehat{\pi}^{j}_{\tau}(X)\neq\pi^{\star}_{\tau}(X)\big].

Proof.
The proof of this result is presented in
Section

E.1
.
∎

Note that
Lemma

B.2
allows the events
\mathcal{K}_{j}
to be defined on a subset of
\bm{X}^{L}\times[0,1]^{kL}
and the containment
\mathcal{K}_{j}\subseteq\mathcal{K}_{j-1}
is defined via
Eq.

15
. Overall this result decomposes the test error of
\widehat{\pi}
into
3
terms; the remainder of the proof of
Theorem

B.1
is to show that, with high probability, each of these terms is upper bounded by a constant.

The proof of
Lemma

B.2
introduces a potential function
\Phi_{j}
which tracks the performance of the plurality of the models
\widehat{\pi}^{0},\cdots,\widehat{\pi}^{j}
trained until step
j+1
. The term
L\cdot\beta^{0,k}_{0}
bounds the initial value of this potential,
\Phi_{0}
. The quantity
\beta^{0,k}_{0}
decays exponentially with
k
, and so, as long as
k=\Omega(\log(L))
, the first term is bounded by a sufficiently small absolute constant, say
\frac{1}{16}
, which is within the target error guarantee of
Theorem

B.1
. This bound on
k
should be viewed as a requirement on the number of weak models to be trained for the plurality of the models to achieve error
\mathcal{O}(1/L)
under
\rho
.

The remaining terms in
Lemma

B.2
are easiest to interpret when
\mathcal{K}_{k-1}=\cdots=\mathcal{K}_{0}=\bm{X}^{L}\times[0,1]^{kL}
, where they reduce to
\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}(\texttt{err}_{j}-\texttt{err}_{\star})\cdot\mathbb{E}[R_{j}\mid\mathcal{H}_{k-1}]
. This expression brings out the role of the regression
R_{j}
and a clean tradeoff to establish: in iterations where generating training examples from
\lambda_{j}
is easy,
\texttt{err}_{j}
is likely to be smaller than the threshold
\texttt{err}_{\star}=\frac{1}{4}
, resulting in
(\texttt{err}_{j}-\texttt{err}_{\star})\cdot\mathbb{E}[R_{j}\mid\mathcal{H}_{k-1}]\leqslant 0
. In the iterations where sampling from
\lambda_{j}
is expensive, we need to argue that
R_{j}
is also likely to be small. The interpretation of such a result is that, in the iterations where sampling from
\lambda_{j}
is expensive, there is sufficient leeway in the plurality of the existing models that adding an arbitrary
\widehat{\pi}^{j}
to the ensemble does not hurt the error significantly.

Toward establishing such a guarantee, we first define an “abort” event which captures whether the dataset collected in iteration
j
is sufficiently large. This is a proxy for whether sampling from
\lambda_{j}
is easy, and will be a sufficient condition to capture whether
\texttt{err}_{j}\leqslant\texttt{err}_{\star}
. We first introduce some notation in order to define this event. Let,

p_{j}=\mathrm{Pr}_{{\bm{x}}\sim\rho}\big(\texttt{InvSampling}(D_{\bm{x}}\ \|\ \Pi_{j},k)\neq\perp\big|\ \mathcal{H}_{j-1}\big),\text{ where, }D_{\bm{x}}=\texttt{Split}({\bm{x}}\|L)

(16)

denote the probability that an instance
{\bm{x}}\sim\rho
contributes a shorter instance
O
to the dataset
D_{\mathrm{out}}^{j}
through split-and-inverted-sampling (cf.
Algorithms

3
and
4
). It is a short calculation to derive the formula,

\displaystyle p_{j}=\mathrm{Pr}(|\mathcal{I}_{j}|\geqslant 1\mid\mathcal{H}_{j-1})=\mathbb{E}\left[1-\prod_{i=0}^{L-1}\bigg(1-\frac{\alpha^{j,k}_{\mathrm{rank}_{j}(X_{i})}}{\alpha^{j,k}_{\max}}\bigg)\ \middle|\ \mathcal{H}_{j-1}\right]

(17)

where
\mathcal{I}_{j}=\Big\{0\leqslant i\leqslant L-1:\eta_{j,i}\leqslant\frac{w_{j}(X_{i})}{\|w_{j}\|_{\infty}}\Big\}
denotes the random set of accepted indices in a run of
InvSampling
(
Algorithm

3
) when applied to
D_{{\bm{x}}}
for
{\bm{x}}\sim\rho
in the
j^{\text{th}}
iteration;
\underline{X}=(X_{i})_{i=0}^{L-1}
is obtained by decomposing the length-
T
instance
{\bm{x}}
into
L
length-
\tau
instances.
13
13

13

i.e., define
\underline{X}=(X_{j})_{j=0}^{L-1}
with
X_{j}=(s_{j\tau},\bm{w}_{j\tau+1:(j+1)\tau})
, is constructed from
{\bm{x}}=(s_{0},\bm{w}_{1:T})
.
For completeness, we prove
Eq.

17
later via the chain of equations in
Eq.

61
to
Eq.

62
. We also define a slight modification of
p_{j}
, which captures the same acceptance probability for split-and-inverted-sampling, but conditioned on the event
\mathcal{K}_{j-1}
,

\widetilde{p}_{j}=\mathrm{Pr}\left(|\mathcal{I}_{j}|\geqslant 1\ \middle|\ \mathcal{K}_{j-1},\mathcal{H}_{j-1}\right),\text{ and define }p_{j}^{\star}=\frac{1}{1024k^{5/2}}.

(18)

While
p_{j}
above captures the probability that a sample
{\bm{x}}\sim\rho
is accepted by split-and-inverted-sampling, we define the abort event in terms of
\widetilde{p}_{j}
. In particular, this implies that there may be iterations
j
where the dataset
D_{\mathrm{out}}^{j}
is large (i.e.,
p_{j}
is large), but the iteration still aborts because the effective dataset collected (as determined by
\widetilde{p}_{j}
) is too small. Define,

\texttt{ABORT}[j]=\big\{\widetilde{p}_{j}\leqslant p_{j}^{\star}\big\}

(19)

B.1.1
Analyzing terms in the test-error decomposition of
Lemma

B.2

Our main argument to analyze the main summation
\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}(\texttt{err}_{j}-\texttt{err}_{\star})\cdot\mathbb{E}[R_{j}\cdot\mathbb{I}((\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\in\mathcal{K}_{j})\mid\mathcal{H}_{k-1}]
in
Lemma

B.2
is shown next. We will split our analysis of the main summation into two parts, one across iterations that abort, and the other across iterations that don’t abort. The high-level idea is to show that in any iteration
j
which does not abort,
\texttt{err}_{j}\leqslant\texttt{err}_{\star}
with high probability, which means that the corresponding term in the summation is non-positive; in any iteration
j
which aborts, the regression
R_{j}
is small with moderate probability, and using the event
\mathcal{K}
to avoid the potentially long tails of this random variable. Throughout the sketch, we will emphasize the conditions required to define the event
\mathcal{K}
, before ultimately defining it.

Aborted iterations.

In any iteration
j
which aborts, we show in
Lemma

B.5
that this implies that
\widetilde{p}_{j}
must be small with high probability. This is further used to imply, in
Lemma

B.7
, that the regression
R_{j}
is typically small: it will only ever exceed some threshold
\mathfrak{r}_{j}
with moderately low probability
q_{j}\lesssim\frac{1}{k}
. Suppose the event
\mathcal{K}_{j}
satisfies the following condition in any iteration
j
,

(\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\in\mathcal{K}_{j}\implies\big\{\texttt{ABORT}[j]\text{ and }R_{j}>\mathfrak{r}_{j}\big\}^{c}.

(C1)

Then, in any iteration
j
which aborts, we can bound the corresponding term in the summation in
Lemma

B.2
by,

\displaystyle(\texttt{err}_{j}-\texttt{err}_{\star})\cdot\mathbb{E}[R_{j}\cdot\mathbb{I}((\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\in\mathcal{K}_{j})\mid\mathcal{H}_{k-1}]

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbb{E}[R_{j}\cdot\mathbb{I}(R_{j}\leqslant\mathfrak{r}_{j})\mid\mathcal{H}_{k-1}]

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbb{E}[R_{j}\cdot\mathbb{I}(R_{j}\leqslant\mathfrak{r}_{j})\mid\mathcal{H}_{k-1}]

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathfrak{r}_{j}.

The
\mathfrak{r}_{j}
’s are shown to satisfy the upper bound,
\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\mathfrak{r}_{j}\leqslant\frac{1}{8}
in
Lemma

B.8
. This means that the contribution to the summation across all aborted iterations is bounded by a constant. The role of the failure probability on the regression upper bound,
q_{j}
, will appear later in showing that the (yet to be defined) sequence of events
\mathcal{K}_{0},\cdots,\mathcal{K}_{k-1}
satisfies with high probability,

\mathrm{Pr}((\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\not\in\mathcal{K}_{j-1}\mid\mathcal{H}_{j-1})\leqslant\frac{1}{16}

(C2)

With
j=k
, this is the final term in the error decomposition in
Lemma

B.2
and is established in
Lemma

B.4
. The same lower bound on the probability of each
\mathcal{K}_{j}
will also be useful in showing guarantees for the weak models,
\widehat{\pi}^{j}
, trained in non-aborted iterations. This is discussed as a part of the next section.

Non-aborted iterations.

If iteration
j
does not abort, we show in
Lemma

B.6
that with high probability,
\texttt{err}_{j}\leqslant\texttt{err}_{\star}
. By extension, this implies that the corresponding term in the summation in
Lemma

B.2

(\texttt{err}_{j}-\texttt{err}_{\star})\cdot\mathbb{E}[R_{j}\cdot\mathbb{I}((\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\in\mathcal{K}_{j})\mid\mathcal{H}_{k-1}]\leqslant 0
. Recall
\texttt{err}_{j}=\mathrm{Pr}_{X\sim\lambda_{j}}\big[\widehat{\pi}^{j}_{\tau}(X)\neq\pi^{\star}_{\tau}(X)\big]
itself is the test error of
\widehat{\pi}^{j}
under the distribution of instances
\lambda_{j}\propto\overline{\sigma_{\mathcal{K}_{j}}}(\cdot)w_{j}(\cdot)
, a distribution which depends on the event
\mathcal{K}_{j}
. Letting
\widehat{\lambda}_{j}
denote the distribution over instances which examples in the i.i.d. dataset
D_{\mathrm{out}}^{j}
follow,
Lemma

B.6
is proved by showing that the following condition,

\left\|\frac{\lambda_{j}}{\widehat{\lambda}_{j}}\right\|_{\infty}\leqslant\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\log(L)}{\widetilde{p}_{j}}\right)

(C3)

is satisfied for all iterations
j
which do not abort, as long as
\mathcal{K}_{j}
is defined appropriately in terms of
\mathcal{K}_{j-1}
and
\mathcal{U}_{j}
(
Lemma

B.3
). From the definition of an aborted iteration, we know that
\widetilde{p}_{j}
is at least
p_{j}^{\star}
, which implies a density ratio upper bound scaling as
k^{\mathcal{O}(1)}\log(L)
. Note finally, that examples in the distribution
D_{\mathrm{out}}^{j}
come from
\widehat{\lambda}_{j}
, and the acceptance probability of split-and-inverted-sampling to accept
{\bm{x}}\sim\rho
to generate a sample from this distribution is
p_{j}
; noticing that,

p_{j}=\mathrm{Pr}\big(|\mathcal{I}_{j}|\geqslant 1\mid\mathcal{H}_{j-1}\big)\geqslant\mathrm{Pr}\big(|\mathcal{I}_{j}|\geqslant 1\mid\mathcal{K}_{j-1},\mathcal{H}_{j-1}\big)\cdot\mathrm{Pr}\big(\mathcal{K}_{j-1}\mid\mathcal{H}_{j-1}\big)\gtrsim\widetilde{p}_{j}

where the last inequality uses (
C2
).

Finally,
\texttt{AutoLearn}.\texttt{warmup}
uses next-token prediction to learn the model
\widehat{\pi}^{j}
on this dataset. Using prior guarantees for next-token prediction
[
29
]
and a change-of-measure argument using (
C2
), we have learning guarantees for
\widehat{\pi}^{j}
under
\lambda_{j}
.

The existence of an event
\mathcal{K}_{j}
establishing (
C1
), (
C2
) and (
C3
) is established in
Lemmas

B.3
and
B.4
. The above analysis across aborted and non-aborted iterations combines to simplify the summation in
Lemma

B.2
to the following inequality: with high probability,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}_{{\bm{x}}\sim\rho}\big[\widehat{\pi}({\bm{x}})\neq\pi^{\star}_{T}({\bm{x}})\big]

\displaystyle\leqslant L\cdot\beta^{0,k}_{0}+\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\mathfrak{r}_{j}+\mathrm{Pr}((\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\not\in\mathcal{K}_{k-1}\mid\mathcal{H}_{k-1})

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{16}+\frac{1}{8}+\frac{1}{16}=\frac{1}{4}

(20)

The final inequality follows from the bound on
\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\mathfrak{r}_{j}\leqslant\frac{1}{8}
, and choosing
k
to be sufficiently large (as
\Theta(\log(L))
), which gives
L\cdot\beta_{0}^{0,k}\leqslant\frac{1}{16}
. This uses a bound which shows that
\beta^{0,k}_{0}
decays exponentially fast in
k
(
Lemma

B.9
).

B.2
Proving
Theorem

3.4
: relevant lemmata

Having established the broader program, we introduce the lemmas mentioned above. We will focus on the non-aborted iterations first, and then discuss the aborted iterations next.

B.2.1
Non-aborted iterations
\big(\,\widetilde{p}_{j}\geqslant p^{\star}_{j}\big)
.

In iterations
j
which do not abort, we show that
\widehat{\pi}^{j}
achieves low test error under
\lambda_{j}
with high probability, establishing a weak learning guarantee. Since the proof relies heavily on the
\mathcal{K}_{j}
events, we first define these events before proving that they satisfy the structural conditions laid out in (
C1
) to (
C3
).

Definition of
(\mathcal{K}_{j})_{j=0}^{k-1}
.

For each
0\leqslant j\leqslant k-1
, the event
\mathcal{K}_{j}\subseteq\bm{X}^{L}\times[0,1]^{(j+1)L}
, we construct, is a function of
\underline{X}\sim\sigma
and
\bm{\eta}_{0:j}
; recall
\bm{\eta}=(\bm{\eta}_{j^{\prime}}:0\leqslant j^{\prime}\leqslant k-1)
, where
\bm{\eta}_{j^{\prime}}=(\eta_{j^{\prime},i})_{i=0}^{L-1}\sim\operatorname{Unif}([0,1])^{\otimes L}
is a sequence of i.i.d. random variables. The
\mathcal{K}_{j}
events will be constructed recursively: for all
0\leqslant j\leqslant k-1
,

\mathcal{K}_{j}\leftarrow\texttt{ConstructEvent}(\mathcal{U}_{j}\cap\mathcal{K}_{j-1}\,\|\,c,\sigma,\mathcal{H}_{j-1})\text{ where }c=\frac{1}{32k},

(21)

with
\mathcal{K}_{-1}=\bm{X}^{L}
. The definition of
ConstructEvent
is deferred to later (
Lemma

B.3
), and we define the
(\mathcal{U}_{j})_{j=0}^{k-1}
events next. Each
\mathcal{U}_{j}\subseteq\bm{X}^{L}
is a
random
event which is a measurable function of
\mathcal{H}_{j-1}
, and is defined as,

\mathcal{U}_{j}=\big\{\widetilde{p}_{j}\leqslant p^{\star}_{j}\text{ and }R_{j}>\mathfrak{r}_{j}\big\}^{c}.

(22)

Here, the regression,
R_{j}
was defined earlier in
Eq.

13
and the thresholds
\mathfrak{r}_{j}>0
are defined later in
Eq.

31
, and the conditional acceptance probability
\widetilde{p}_{j}
is defined in
Eq.

18
.

In order to complete the definition of the
\mathcal{K}_{j}
’s, we still need to define the
ConstructEvent
algorithm which defines
\mathcal{K}_{j}
in terms of
\mathcal{U}_{j}
and
\mathcal{K}_{j-1}
. In the subsequent lemma, we show the existence of such an algorithm which enables the resulting
\mathcal{K}_{j}
’s to satisfy the structural constraints in (
C1
) to (
C3
).

Lemma B.3

(
\mathcal{K}_{j}
’s satisfy (
C1
) and (
C3
))
.

Fix some
0\leqslant j\leqslant k-1
, and recall that
\widehat{\lambda}_{j}
denotes the distribution over instances the i.i.d. dataset
D_{\mathrm{out}}^{j}
is sampled from. Consider any filtration
(\mathcal{H}_{j})_{j=0}^{k-1}
and sequence of events,
(\mathcal{U}_{j})_{j=0}^{k-1}
such that
\mathcal{U}_{j}\subseteq\bm{X}^{L}
and is a measurable function of
\mathcal{H}_{j-1}
. Fix some constant
c\in(0,1)
. There exists an algorithm
\texttt{ConstructEvent}(\,\cdot\,\|\,c,\sigma,\mathcal{H}_{k-1})
which iteratively constructs a sequence of events
\mathcal{K}_{j}\in\bm{X}^{L}\times[0,1]^{(j+1)L}
as:

\mathcal{K}_{j}\leftarrow\texttt{ConstructEvent}(\mathcal{U}_{j}\cap\mathcal{K}_{j-1}\,\|\,c,\sigma,\mathcal{H}_{j-1})

where
\mathcal{K}_{-1}=\bm{X}^{L}
. The
\mathcal{K}_{j}
events satisfy the following conditions:

\mathcal{K}_{j}\subseteq\mathcal{U}_{j}\cap\mathcal{K}_{j-1}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathrm{Pr}((\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\not\in\mathcal{K}_{j}\mid(\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\in\mathcal{U}_{j}\cap\mathcal{K}_{j-1},\mathcal{H}_{j-1})\leqslant c

(23)

Finally, the distribution
\lambda_{j}(\cdot)\propto\overline{\sigma_{\mathcal{K}_{j}}}(\cdot)w_{j}(\cdot)
over
\bm{X}
introduced in
Eq.

12
, which depends on
\mathcal{K}_{j}
satisfies the following condition,

\left\|\frac{\lambda_{j}}{\widehat{\lambda}_{j}}\right\|_{\infty}\lesssim\frac{c^{-1}\log(L)}{\mathrm{Pr}\left(\mathcal{U}_{j}\cap\mathcal{K}_{j-1}\,\middle|\,|\mathcal{I}_{j}|\geqslant 1,\mathcal{H}_{j-1}\right)}

(24)

Here,
\mathcal{I}_{j}
, defined in
Eq.

17
, is the set of accepted indices in a run of split-and-inverted-sampling (
Algorithms

4
and
3
) for
{\bm{x}}\sim\rho
in the
j^{\text{th}}
iteration.

Proof.
The proof of this lemma is discussed in
Section

E.2
.
\texttt{ConstructEvent}(\cdot)
is defined in
Eq.

69
.
∎

Interpretation of
Lemma

B.3
.

In order to interpret this result, consider the special case where
k=1
,
\mathcal{U}_{0}=\bm{X}^{L}
and
c
is small (we remove the
j
subscript on
\mathcal{K}_{j}
,
w_{j}
for succinctness). In this setting,
ConstructEvent
creates an event
\mathcal{K}\subseteq\bm{X}^{L}\times[0,1]^{L}
such that,
(a)

\mathrm{Pr}(\mathcal{K})\geqslant 1-c
is a high probability event, and furthermore,
(b)

\big\|\frac{\nu}{\widehat{\nu}}\big\|_{\infty}\lesssim c^{-1}\log(L)
where
\nu
is the distribution
\propto\sigma_{\mathcal{K}}(\cdot)w(\cdot)
and
\widehat{\nu}
is the distribution over inputs realized by split-and-inverted-sampling for
{\bm{x}}\sim\rho
. We will focus on this special case and discuss it further in
Section

E.2
, showing that this guarantee is an exponential improvement over the best guarantee achievable if the event
\mathcal{K}
was forced to equal
\bm{X}^{L}\times[0,1]^{L}
.

While
Lemma

B.3
gives us a bound on the density ratio between
\lambda_{j}
and
\widehat{\lambda}_{j}
, the term in the denominator depends on the quantity
\mathrm{Pr}\big(\mathcal{U}_{j}\cap\mathcal{K}_{j-1}\mid|\mathcal{I}_{j}|\geqslant 1,\mathcal{H}_{j-1}\big)
which is a-priori different from the bound we hinted at earlier in (
C3
), involving
\widetilde{p}_{j}
. Our main argument will be to show that this term cannot be too small in an iteration which does not abort. Observe that when
\widetilde{p}_{j}>p^{\star}_{j}
, the event
\mathcal{U}_{j}
is satisfied, and therefore,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}(\mathcal{U}_{j}\cap\mathcal{K}_{j-1}\mid|\mathcal{I}_{j}|\geqslant 1,\mathcal{H}_{j-1})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Pr}(\mathcal{K}_{j-1}\mid|\mathcal{I}_{j}|\geqslant 1,\mathcal{H}_{j-1})

(25)

\displaystyle\geqslant\mathrm{Pr}(|\mathcal{I}_{j}|\geqslant 1\text{ and }\mathcal{K}_{j-1}\mid\mathcal{H}_{j-1})

\displaystyle\geqslant\mathrm{Pr}(|\mathcal{I}_{j}|\geqslant 1\mid\mathcal{K}_{j-1},\mathcal{H}_{j-1})\cdot\mathrm{Pr}(\mathcal{K}_{j-1}\mid\mathcal{H}_{j-1})\geqslant\frac{15}{16}\cdot\widetilde{p}_{j}

(26)

where we use (
C2
) (which we establish next in
Lemma

B.4
) to lower bound
\mathrm{Pr}(\mathcal{K}_{j-1}\mid\mathcal{H}_{j-1})
. By definition of the iteration
j
not aborting, we know that
\widetilde{p}_{j}\geqslant p^{\star}_{j}
is lower bounded. And furthermore, by the definition of
p_{j}^{\star}
as well as the manner in which we designed the sequence of events
\mathcal{K}_{j}
in
Eq.

21
choosing
c=\frac{1}{32k}
, we have the density ratio bound,

\left\|\frac{\lambda_{j}}{\widehat{\lambda}_{j}}\right\|_{\infty}\lesssim k^{7/2}\log(L)

(27)

Next we establish that the
\mathcal{K}_{j}
events satisfy the condition (
C2
), showing that it covers a moderate amount of mass.

Lemma B.4

(
\mathcal{K}_{j}
’s satisfy (
C2
))
.

For all
0\leqslant j\leqslant k-1
,
\mathrm{Pr}(\mathcal{K}_{j}\mid\mathcal{H}_{j})=\mathrm{Pr}(\mathcal{K}_{j}\mid\mathcal{H}_{j-1})\geqslant\frac{15}{16}
.

Proof.
The proof of this result is discussed in
Section

E.3
.
∎

Lemma

B.4
is proved by an iterative argument which peels off the contribution of the previous iterations
j^{\prime}\leqslant j
to
\mathcal{K}_{j}
one step at a time. Note that these events depend on the behavior of the regression
R_{j}
across aborted iterations, and so the tools required to prove this result are discussed later in
Section

B.2.2
.

While
Lemma

B.4
is used to arrive at the density ratio bound in
Eq.

27
, this result also gives us a handle on
\mathrm{Pr}(\mathcal{K}_{k-1}\mid\mathcal{H}_{k-1})
, which appears in the breakdown of the test-loss of
\widehat{\pi}
in
Lemma

B.2
.

Finally, note that while
Eq.

27
gives us a bound on the density ratio between
\lambda_{j}
and
\widehat{\lambda}_{j}
in iterations which don’t abort, we still need to argue that the learner can generate sufficiently many samples from the latter distribution (i.e., a sufficiently large dataset
D_{\mathrm{out}}^{j}
) to learn from in these iterations. This will follow from the fact that a lower bound on
\widetilde{p}_{j}
in the non-aborted iteration,
also certifies a lower bound on
p_{j}
in these iterations.
Namely,

\widetilde{p}_{j}=\mathrm{Pr}(|\mathcal{I}_{j}|\geqslant 1\mid\mathcal{K}_{j-1},\mathcal{H}_{j-1})\leqslant\frac{\mathrm{Pr}(|\mathcal{I}_{j}|\geqslant 1\text{ and }\mathcal{K}_{j-1}\mid\mathcal{H}_{j-1})}{\mathrm{Pr}(\mathcal{K}_{j-1}\mid\mathcal{H}_{j-1})}\leqslant\frac{p_{j}}{(15/16)}

(28)

Where the last inequality is by
Lemma

B.4
and the definition of
p_{j}
. All in all, via a concentration argument, this implies that in iterations which don’t abort, the dataset
D_{\mathrm{out}}^{j}
is unlikely to be small, and is at least
|D_{\mathrm{input}}|/k^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
in size. This is shown formally in the following lemma.

Lemma B.5

(Large
\widetilde{p}_{j}\implies\texttt{node}_{j}
instantiated with a large dataset)
.

Suppose for a large constant
C_{1}>0
,

|D_{\mathrm{input}}|\geqslant C_{1}k^{7}\log(L)\cdot(d\log(|S|)+\log(k/\delta)).

(29)

If
\widetilde{p}_{j}>p_{j}^{\star}
in iteration
j
, then
\mathrm{Pr}\big(|D_{\mathrm{out}}^{j}|<C_{2}k^{7/2}\log(L)\cdot(d\log(|S|)+\log(k/\delta))\mid\mathcal{H}_{j-1}\big)\leqslant\frac{\delta}{2k}
.

Proof.
The proof of this lemma is discussed in
Section

E.4
.
∎

Finally, we invoke the density ratio bound in
Eq.

27
, along with the sufficiently large size of the dataset
D_{\mathrm{out}}^{j}
in any non-aborted iteration
j
(
Lemma

B.5
) to establish weak learning guarantees for
\widehat{\pi}^{j}
. Recall from
\texttt{AutoLearn}.\texttt{warmup}
, that
\widehat{\pi}^{j}
is trained via next-token prediction on
D_{\mathrm{out}}^{j}
, which is a dataset of length-
\tau
instances drawn from
\widehat{\lambda}_{j}
. The prior work of
[
29
]
established learning guarantees for next-token prediction, and via a change-of-measure argument to transfer learning guarantees under
\widehat{\lambda}_{j}
to
\lambda_{j}
(facilitated by the density ratio bound in
Lemma

B.3
), this implies learning guarantees for
\widehat{\pi}^{j}
under the distribution
\lambda_{j}
. This is shown in the following lemma.

Lemma B.6

(Weak learning guarantee for
\widehat{\pi}^{j}
)
.

Consider any iteration
j
where
\widetilde{p}_{j}>p_{j}^{\star}
. With probability at least
1-\frac{\delta}{k}
, the model
\widehat{\pi}^{j}
trained in iteration
j
satisfies,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}_{X\sim\lambda_{j}}\big[\widehat{\pi}^{j}(X)\neq\pi^{\star}_{\tau}(X)\big]=\texttt{err}_{j}\leqslant\texttt{err}_{\star}=\frac{1}{4}

Proof.
The proof of this result is discussed in
Section

E.5
.
∎

With this, we complete the analysis in the non-aborted iterations. Next we move on to the aborted iterations.

B.2.2
Aborted iterations
\big(\,\widetilde{p}_{j}\leqslant p^{\star}_{j}\big)

In the aborted iterations,
\widetilde{p}_{j}
may be too small, so we are no longer guaranteed a small bound on the density ratio between
\lambda_{j}
and
\widehat{\lambda}_{j}
through
Lemma

B.3
. In such iterations, our argument will be to show that the regression
R_{j}
, which controls the test-error of
\widehat{\pi}
(cf.
Lemma

B.2
) is small with moderate probability. In particular, from its definition in
Eq.

18
, and the structure of
\mathcal{I}_{j}
as being a sum of independent indicators,

\displaystyle\widetilde{p}_{j}=\mathrm{Pr}\big(|\mathcal{I}_{j}|\geqslant 1\mid\mathcal{H}_{j-1},\mathcal{K}_{j-1}\big)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[1-\prod_{i=0}^{L-1}\bigg(1-\frac{\alpha^{j,k}_{\mathrm{rank}_{j}(X_{i})}}{\alpha^{j,k}_{\max}}\bigg)\ \middle|\ \mathcal{K}_{j-1},\mathcal{H}_{j-1}\right]

(30)

On the other hand, we have
R_{j}=\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}\alpha^{j,k}_{\mathrm{rank}_{j}(X_{i})}
. When
\widetilde{p}_{j}
is small, we can show a moderate probability upper bound on
R_{j}
via an application of Markov’s inequality. This implies that in iterations which abort, the regression is likely to be small. In order to state the bound on the regression, we define the threshold,

\mathfrak{r}_{j}\triangleq\frac{\alpha^{j,k}_{\max}}{16\sqrt{k}}

(31)

Lemma B.7

(Aborted iterations have low regression)
.

If
\widetilde{p}_{j}\leqslant p_{j}^{\star}
, then,
\mathrm{Pr}\big(R_{j}>\mathfrak{r}_{j}\mid\mathcal{K}_{j-1},\mathcal{H}_{j-1}\big)\leqslant\frac{1}{32k^{2}}
.

Proof.
The proof of this result is given in
Section

E.6
.
∎

While the above result shows that
R_{j}
is small with moderate probability, the bound on the error of
\widehat{\pi}
in
Lemma

B.2
relies on the
expected
regression, which is a-priori not guaranteed to be small. This is handled by the fact that we are guaranteed
(\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\in\mathcal{K}_{j}\implies\{\widetilde{p}_{j}\leqslant p^{\star}_{j}\text{ and }R_{j}>\mathfrak{r}_{j}\}^{c}
. This means that the regression in
Lemma

B.2
in aborted iterations is gated by the
\mathfrak{r}_{j}
threshold, which is small, and we avoid the tails of
R_{j}
. We introduce a short lemma proving that the cumulative regression threshold,
\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\mathfrak{r}_{j}
is indeed small, necessary to complete this argument.

Lemma B.8

(Bound on cumulative regression)
.

\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\mathfrak{r}_{j}\leqslant\frac{1}{8}
.

Proof.
The proof of this lemma is similar to that of
[
19
, Lemma 3.9]
. Noting that for
\texttt{err}_{\star}=\frac{1}{4}
, the mode of the binomial PMF gives for
0\leqslant j\leqslant k-2
,
\alpha_{\max}^{j,k}\leqslant\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\cdot\frac{3}{16}}}\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{k-j-1}}\leqslant\frac{0.95}{\sqrt{k-j-1}}
, while for
\alpha_{\max}^{k-1,k}\leqslant 1
. As a consequence,

\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\mathfrak{r}_{j}\leqslant\frac{1}{16\sqrt{k}}\left(\sum_{j=0}^{k-2}\frac{0.95}{\sqrt{k-j-1}}+1\right)\leqslant\frac{1}{8}.

∎

Having established all the necessary tools, we are ready to furnish a proof of
Theorem

B.1
via bounding the test-error decomposition for
\widehat{\pi}
in
Lemma

B.2
.

B.3
Proof of
Theorem

B.1

We prove accuracy, sample complexity and query complexity guarantees for
\texttt{AutoLearn}.\texttt{warmup}
below.

Accuracy bound.

Let
\mathcal{J}_{\texttt{ABORT}}\triangleq\big\{0\leqslant j\leqslant k-1:\widetilde{p}_{j}\leqslant p^{\star}_{j}\big\}
denote the set of aborted iterations, which is
\mathcal{H}_{k-2}
measurable. By the test-error decomposition in
Lemma

B.2
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}_{{\bm{x}}\sim\rho}\big[\widehat{\pi}({\bm{x}})\neq\pi^{\star}_{T}({\bm{x}})\big]

\displaystyle\leqslant L\cdot\beta^{0,k}_{0}+\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}(\texttt{err}_{j}-\texttt{err}_{\star})\cdot\mathbb{E}[R_{j}\cdot\mathbb{I}((\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\in\mathcal{K}_{j})\mid\mathcal{H}_{k-1}]+\mathrm{Pr}((\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\not\in\mathcal{K}_{k-1}\mid\mathcal{H}_{k-1})

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{\leqslant}\frac{1}{16}+\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}(\texttt{err}_{j}-\texttt{err}_{\star})\cdot\mathbb{E}[R_{j}\cdot\mathbb{I}((\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\in\mathcal{K}_{j})\mid\mathcal{H}_{k-1}]+\frac{1}{16}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{8}+\sum_{j\in\mathcal{J}_{\texttt{ABORT}}}\mathbb{E}[R_{j}\cdot\mathbb{I}((\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\in\mathcal{K}_{j})\mid\mathcal{H}_{k-1}]+\sum_{j\not\in\mathcal{J}_{\texttt{ABORT}}}(\texttt{err}_{j}-\texttt{err}_{\star})\cdot\mathbb{E}[R_{j}\cdot\mathbb{I}((\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\in\mathcal{K}_{j})\mid\mathcal{H}_{k-1}]

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{8}+\sum_{j\in\mathcal{J}_{\texttt{ABORT}}}\mathbb{E}[R_{j}\cdot\mathbb{I}\big(\{\widetilde{p}_{j}\leqslant p_{j}^{\star}\text{ and }R_{j}>\mathfrak{r}_{j}\}^{c}\big)\mid\mathcal{H}_{k-1}]+L\sum_{j\not\in\mathcal{J}_{\texttt{ABORT}}}(\texttt{err}_{j}-\texttt{err}_{\star})_{+}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{8}+\sum_{j\in\mathcal{J}_{\texttt{ABORT}}}\mathfrak{r}_{j}+L\sum_{j\not\in\mathcal{J}_{\texttt{ABORT}}}(\texttt{err}_{j}-\texttt{err}_{\star})_{+}

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{\leqslant}\frac{1}{4}+L\sum_{j\not\in\mathcal{J}_{\texttt{ABORT}}}(\texttt{err}_{j}-\texttt{err}_{\star})_{+},

where in
(a)
we use the choice of
k=\Theta(\log(L))
and the exponential decay of
\beta^{0,k}_{0}
as a function of
k
(cf.
Lemma

B.9
), the upper bound on
\mathrm{Pr}((\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\not\in\mathcal{K}_{k-1}\mid\mathcal{H}_{k-1})
in
Lemma

B.4
, and the definition of
\mathcal{K}_{j}
. And in
(b)
we invoke
Lemma

B.8
below, showing
\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\mathfrak{r}_{j}\leqslant\frac{1}{8}
. In any iteration
j
where
\texttt{ABORT}[j]
does not occur, by
Lemma

B.6
, with probability
1-\frac{\delta}{k}
,
\texttt{err}_{j}\leqslant\texttt{err}_{\star}
. This implies, with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}_{{\bm{x}}\sim\rho}\big[\widehat{\pi}({\bm{x}})\neq\pi^{\star}_{T}({\bm{x}})\big]

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{4}.

Bound on sample complexity of
\texttt{AutoLearn}.\texttt{warmup}
.

The bound on the size of the instance dataset required to establish the above guarantee for the depth-
1
construction is demonstrated in
Lemma

B.5
and is,

|D_{\mathrm{input}}|\geqslant\log^{8}(T)\cdot\big[d\log(|S|)+\log(T/\delta)\big],

where we set
L=\sqrt{T}
and
k=\log(L)
. This gives the desired bound on the size of the dataset of instances in
Theorem

B.1
.

Bound on query complexity of
\texttt{AutoLearn}.\texttt{warmup}
.

Observe that on every instance in the dataset
D_{\mathrm{input}}
,
\texttt{AutoLearn}.\texttt{warmup}
queries
\texttt{iCoT}(\cdot)
exactly
T/\tau=L
times within the
\texttt{Split}(\,\cdot\,\|\,L)
subroutine. On the other hand, to train the weak model
\widehat{\pi}^{j}
,
\texttt{AutoLearn}.\texttt{warmup}
makes a call to
\texttt{NTP}_{\texttt{Full-CoT}}\big(D_{\mathrm{out}}^{j}\,\|\,\tau,\delta/2k\big)
which queries
\texttt{iCoT}(\cdot)

\tau
times per instance in
D_{\mathrm{out}}^{j}
. Summation across the invocations of this subroutine for
j=0,\cdots,k-1
, the overall query complexity is upper bounded by,

\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\tau|D_{\mathrm{out}}^{j}|+L|D_{\mathrm{input}}|\leqslant(\tau+L)|D_{\mathrm{input}}|=2\sqrt{T}|D_{\mathrm{input}}|.

where in the last equation we plug in the choice
L=\tau=\sqrt{T}
. This gives the desired bound on the query complexity in
Theorem

B.1
.

Lemma B.9

(Exponential decay of
\beta^{0,k}_{0}
)
.

There exists an absolute constant
c>0
such that,
\beta^{0,k}_{0}\leqslant e^{-ck}
.

Proof.
Consider a set of
k
biased coins,
Z_{1},\cdots,Z_{k}
, with probability of heads equal to
1-\texttt{err}_{\star}=\frac{3}{4}
. It is a short calculation to see that
\beta^{0,k}_{0}
equals the probability that at most
\frac{k}{2}
of them come up heads. Since the expected number of heads is
\frac{3k}{4}
, by standard concentration arguments (say, Hoeffding’s inequality), the statement of the lemma is established.
∎

Appendix C
Analysis of
AutoLearn
(
Algorithm

2
): Proof of
Theorem

3.4

This section presents the full analysis of
AutoLearn
; for first-time readers, we recommend first reading the analysis of the simplified depth-1 version of
AutoLearn
in
Appendix

B
before proceeding to this section. We prove the following generalized version of
Theorem

3.4
, which states the guarantee in terms of the sample complexity of the weak learner for next-state/token data,
\texttt{Base}(\cdot)
, without assuming it satisfies a “generic” rate:
n_{\texttt{weak}}(\delta^{\prime})\leqslant\texttt{comp}(\Pi)\cdot\log(1/\delta')
.

Theorem C.1

(
Theorem

3.4
for general weak learners)
.

For any
\delta^{\prime}\in(0,1)
, let
\texttt{Base}(\,\cdot\,\|\,\delta^{\prime})
be any algorithm which learns an unknown
\pi^{\star}\in\Pi
to constant error from i.i.d. next-state data (
Definition

3.3
). Let
n_{\texttt{weak}}(\delta^{\prime})
denote its sample complexity to achieve error
\frac{1}{4}
with probability at least
1-\delta^{\prime}
.

Let
\varepsilon,\delta\in(0,1)
and suppose
H=\sqrt{\log_{2}(T)}\in\mathbb{N}
. Suppose
\texttt{AutoLearn}(\,\cdot\,\|\,T,\varepsilon,\delta)
(
Algorithm

2
) is invoked using the weak learner
Base
on a dataset of
n_{\texttt{sample}}
i.i.d. instances drawn from
\rho
, and queries the
\texttt{iCoT}(\cdot)
oracle
n_{\texttt{query}}
times. The sample and query complexity required by the algorithm for the resulting model
\widehat{\pi}:S\times\Sigma^{T}\to S
to achieve
\mathrm{Pr}_{{\bm{x}}\sim\rho}\big[\widehat{\pi}({\bm{x}})\neq\pi^{\star}_{T}({\bm{x}})\big]\leqslant\varepsilon
with probability at least
1-\delta
are upper bounded by,

n_{\texttt{sample}},n_{\texttt{query}}\leqslant h_{\varepsilon}(T)\cdot\left[\frac{n_{\texttt{weak}}(\delta/h_{\varepsilon}(T))\vee\log(h_\varepsilon(T)/\delta)}{\varepsilon}\right],

where
h_{\varepsilon}(T)=2^{C\sqrt{\log(T)}\cdot\log\log(T)}\cdot\log^{2}(1/\varepsilon)
for an absolute constant
C>0
. Furthermore,
n_{\texttt{base}}\leqslant h_{\varepsilon}(T)
, where
n_{\texttt{base}}
is the number of invocations of
Base
.

Proof.
The main proof is discussed in
Section

C.3
.
∎

Theorem

3.4
itself follows from this result by setting
n_{\texttt{weak}}(\delta^{\prime})=\texttt{comp}(\Pi)\log(1/\delta')
and simplifying.

AutoLearn
is an invocation of
\texttt{AutoLearn}_{H+\frac{1}{2}}
with an appropriately chosen decomposition and branching schedule.

itself follows a tree structure where each instance of

invokes
\texttt{AutoLearn}_{h-\frac{1}{2}}(\,\cdot\,\|\,t,\delta)

k_{h}
times. This is depicted in

. Prior to this we introduce some notation.

Figure 5
:
The recursive call of invocations within structure of
AutoLearn
when the target error is
\varepsilon
: each green box corresponds to a call to
\texttt{AutoLearn}_{h}(\cdot)
for some
h\in\{0,\frac{1}{2},1,\ldots,H+\frac{1}{2}\}
. From each integer level
h
to the next one, instances decrease by a factor
L_{h}=L
in length.

Notation.

For
h\in\mathbb{Z}
, let
\bm{X}_{h}=S\times\Sigma^{\tau_{h}}
denote the space of
\tau_{h}
-length instances. Let
\bm{X}_{h+\frac{1}{2}}=\bm{X}_{h}
for all such
h
.

Tree diagram corresponding to the recursive structure of
Algorithm

1
.

The recursive curriculum proceeds with
\texttt{AutoLearn}_{h}
calling
k_{h}
instances of
\texttt{AutoLearn}_{h-\frac{1}{2}}
. The diagram in
Figure

5
arranges these invocations into a tree: the root corresponds to a call of
\texttt{AutoLearn}_{H+\frac{1}{2}}
and going down the tree recursively invokes
\texttt{AutoLearn}_{H},\texttt{AutoLearn}_{H-\frac{1}{2}}
and so on. The number of nodes at any level
h
is
k_{h}\times k_{h+\frac{1}{2}}\times\cdots\times k_{H+\frac{1}{2}}
. We let “
node
” denote a node in the tree, let
\texttt{Par}(\texttt{node})
denote the parent of
node
,
\texttt{Ch}(\texttt{node})
denote the ordered set of children of
node
14
14

14

Note that the children of a node are not interchangeable
and
\texttt{Sib}(\texttt{node})=\texttt{Ch}(\texttt{Par}(\texttt{node}))
denote its siblings. Models are
trained
only at nodes at level
0
of the tree, but are hierarchically combined to define models induced at intermediate nodes of the tree. We also refer to a node
node
being
resolved
to indicate that the model corresponding to
node
has been learned. This is only possible if all level-
0
nodes in the induced subtree with
node
as root have been trained. Furthermore, the construction can be thought of as resolving nodes in the tree in a depth-first fashion: at any level
h
, the instantiations of
\texttt{AutoLearn}_{h}
can only be resolved when the ones “prior” to it (in the depth-first ordering) at the same level have been resolved. We use the notation
\mathcal{T}
to collect the set of all nodes in the tree, and
\mathcal{T}_{h}
to denote the set of all nodes at a given level
h\in\{\frac{1}{2},\cdots,H+\frac{1}{2}\}
.
root
denotes the singular node in
\mathcal{T}_{H+\frac{1}{2}}
(i.e., the root node of
\mathcal{T}
).

Each node is associated with a collection of objects we define next. For any
h\in\{0,\frac{1}{2},\cdots,H+\frac{1}{2}\}
and
\texttt{node}\in\mathcal{T}_{h}
, let
\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{node})}
denote a model associated with it, defined recursively as,

\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{node})}=\widehat{\pi}^{\circ L_{h}},\text{ where, }\widehat{\pi}\leftarrow\texttt{Plu}\big(\big\{\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{node}^{\prime})}:\texttt{node}^{\prime}\in\texttt{Ch}(\texttt{node})\big\}\big).

(32)

where
\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{node})}
for
\texttt{node}\in\mathcal{T}_{0}
are induced by invocations of
Base
. Next, as we carried out in
Lemma

B.6
, define a sequence of distributions for each node. For
node
at level
h
, let
j
denote its index within its ordered set of siblings,
\texttt{Sib}(\texttt{node})
and let the first
j
siblings of
node
be denoted by the set
\texttt{Sib}_{<j}(\texttt{node})
. Then, for
X\in\bm{X}_{h}
,

w_{\texttt{node}}(X)=\alpha^{j,k}_{\mathrm{rank}(X\|\texttt{node})}\text{, where, }\mathrm{rank}(X\|\texttt{node})=\big|\big\{\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{node}^{\prime})}(X)=\pi^{\star}_{\tau_{h}}(X):\texttt{node}^{\prime}\in\texttt{Sib}_{<j}(\texttt{node})\big\}\big|\in[0,j]

(33)

and
\alpha^{j,k}_{r}
is defined in
Eq.

2
. The rank of a node captures how many of its earlier siblings collect any reward on
X
. Finally, for any
\texttt{node}\in\mathcal{T}
we use the notation
D_{\texttt{node}}
to indicate the dataset of instances which the corresponding invocation of
\texttt{AutoLearn}_{h}
processes. First, we prove a short lemma which argues that for any
node
,
D_{\texttt{node}}
is composed of independent instances.

Lemma C.2

(Independence of per-node datasets)
.

For any
\texttt{node}\in\mathcal{T}
the instances in the corresponding dataset,
D_{\texttt{node}}
, are independent.

Proof.
This is proved recursively. When
\texttt{node}=\texttt{root}
, this is true since
D_{\texttt{node}}=D_{\mathrm{input}}
. For its subsequent children nodes,
\texttt{AutoLearn}_{H}
constructs the dataset
D_{\texttt{node}}\leftarrow D_{\mathrm{out}}^{j}
by using inverted sampling (
Algorithm

3
) to process instances in
D_{\mathrm{input}}
to generate shorter instances. This sampling routine takes in a single instance
{\bm{x}}
and returns either
\perp
or a single shorter instance computed from
{\bm{x}}
. The instance
{\bm{x}}
is never reused elsewhere in the recursion, implying that independence is preserved across the dataset of shorter instances computed. The same argument inductively shows that
D_{\texttt{node}}
is composed of independent instances so long as
D_{\texttt{Par}(\texttt{node})}
is composed of independent instances.
∎

With this, for
node
at level
h
or
h+\frac{1}{2}
, we let
\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}}
denote the distribution over instances of length
\tau_{h}
from which the elements of
D_{\texttt{node}}
are sampled. Define
\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}}
as the target distribution over instances of length
\tau_{h}
. At level
h=H+\frac{1}{2}
, we have that,
\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{root}}=\rho
. With this notation introduced, we are ready to state two inductive assumptions on the correctness of invocations of
\texttt{AutoLearn}_{h}
at level
h
(for integer and non-integer values of
h
), which together show that with high probability, the depth
h+1
version succeeds conditioned on this. Define the following sequences recursively,

\displaystyle\text{If }h\in\mathbb{N},\ n_{\texttt{sample}}^{\star}(h+\tfrac{1}{2},2k_{h+\frac{1}{2}}\delta)

\displaystyle=n_{\texttt{sample}}^{\star}(h,\delta)\cdot 2k_{h+\frac{1}{2}}e^{ck_{h+\frac{1}{2}}},

\displaystyle\text{And, }n_{\texttt{sample}}^{\star}(h+1,2k_{h+1}\delta)

\displaystyle=n_{\texttt{sample}}^{\star}(h+\tfrac{1}{2},\delta)\cdot C_{3}k_{h+1}^{7/2}\log(L_{h+1})

(34)

\displaystyle\text{And, }\forall h\geqslant 0,\ n_{\texttt{query}}^{\star}(h+\tfrac{1}{2},2k_{h}\delta)

\displaystyle=L_{h}\cdot n_{\texttt{sample}}^{\star}(h+\tfrac{1}{2},\delta)+k_{h}\cdot n_{\texttt{query}}^{\star}(h,\delta),

(35)

where
n_{\texttt{sample}}^{\star}(0,\delta)=n_{\texttt{query}}^{\star}(0,\delta)=\big[n_{\texttt{weak}}(\delta)\vee\log(1/\delta)\big]
, and
C_{3}>0
is a large absolute constant, while in the definition of
n_{\texttt{sample}}^{\star}
,
c
is chosen to be the same constant as in the exponent of
\beta^{0,k}_{0}
in
Lemma

B.9
. With the choices of decomposition schedule
(L_{h})_{h\geqslant 0}
and branching schedule
(k_{h})_{h\geqslant 0}
as described in
Algorithm

2
, we get that, for
h\leqslant H
,

\displaystyle n_{\texttt{sample}}^{\star}(h,\delta),\ n_{\texttt{query}}^{\star}(h,\delta)

\displaystyle\leqslant(\log(T))^{\mathcal{O}(h)}\cdot\big[n_{\texttt{weak}}(\delta/(\log(T))^{\mathcal{O}(h)})\vee\log( 1/\delta)\big]

and for
h=H+\frac{1}{2}
,

\displaystyle n_{\texttt{sample}}^{\star}(H+\tfrac{1}{2},\delta),\ n_{\texttt{query}}^{\star}(H+\tfrac{1}{2},\delta)

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{h_{\varepsilon}(T)}{\varepsilon}\cdot\big[n_{\texttt{weak}}\big(\delta/h_{\varepsilon}(T)\big)\vee\log(1/\delta)\big].

where
h_{\varepsilon}(T)=2^{C\sqrt{\log(T)}\log\log(T)}\cdot\log^{2}(1/\varepsilon)
. The proof of
Theorem

C.1
relies on establishing inductive guarantees on the sample and query complexity needed at each level of the algorithm. This is separated into guarantees for integer levels, and for half-integer levels in the two hypotheses below.

\textbf{Hypothesis}_{\bm{h+\frac{1}{2}}}(\delta)

Consider any
\texttt{node}\in\mathcal{T}_{h+\frac{1}{2}}
and semiautomata simulation instances of length
\tau_{h}
over the target distribution over instances,
\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}}
. When invoked on a dataset of independent instances drawn from
\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}}
of size
n_{\texttt{sample}}(h+\tfrac{1}{2},\delta)\geqslant n_{\texttt{sample}}^{\star}(h+\tfrac{1}{2},\delta)
,

\texttt{AutoLearn}_{h+\frac{1}{2}}
returns a deterministic model
\widehat{\pi}
such that with probability
1-\delta
,

\mathrm{Pr}_{X\sim\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}}}\big(\widehat{\pi}(X)\neq\pi^{\star}_{\tau_{h}}(X)\big)\leqslant 3\sqrt{k_{h+\frac{1}{2}}}\cdot e^{-ck_{h+\frac{1}{2}}}.

where
c>0
is the constant in the exponent of
\beta^{0,k}_{0}
in
Lemma

B.9
. In the process, the number of queries made to
\texttt{iCoT}(\cdot)
is upper bounded by
n_{\texttt{query}}^{\star}(h+\tfrac{1}{2},\delta)
.

\textbf{Hypothesis}_{\bm{h+1}}(\delta)

Consider any
\texttt{node}\in\mathcal{T}_{h+1}
and semiautomata simulation instances of length
\tau_{h+1}
over the target distribution over instances,
\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}}
. When invoked on a dataset of independent instances drawn from
\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}}
of size at least,
n_{\texttt{sample}}(h+1,\delta)\geqslant n_{\texttt{sample}}^{\star}(h+1,\delta)
, then the invocation
\texttt{AutoLearn}_{h+1}
corresponding to
node
returns a deterministic model
\widehat{\pi}
such that with probability
1-\delta
,

\mathrm{Pr}_{X\sim\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}}}\big(\widehat{\pi}(X)\neq\pi^{\star}_{\tau_{h+1}}(X)\big)\leqslant\frac{1}{4}.

The number of queries made to
\texttt{iCoT}(\cdot)
is at most
n_{\texttt{query}}^{\star}(h+1,\delta)
.

Lemma C.3

(Aggregation steps)
.

For any
h\in\mathbb{Z}
,
\mathrm{Hypothesis}_{h}(\delta)\implies\mathrm{Hypothesis}_{h+\frac{1}{2}}(2k_{h+\frac{1}{2}}\delta)
.

Proof.
This lemma is restated in more detail and proved in
Lemma

C.8
.
∎

Lemma C.4

(Aggregation+composition steps)
.

Consider any value of
h\in\{0,1,\cdots,H-1\}
and suppose
k_{h+1}=\log(L)=c_{2}\sqrt{\log(T)}
and
k_{h+\frac{1}{2}}=c_{3}\log\log(T)
for appropriate constants
c_{2},c_{3}>0
. Then, we have the recursion,
\mathrm{Hypothesis}_{h+\frac{1}{2}}(\delta)\implies\mathrm{Hypothesis}_{h+1}(2k_{h+1}\delta)
.

Proof.
The base case (sample and query complexity of
\mathrm{Hypothesis}_{0}(\delta^{\prime})
at
h=0
) are directly implied by the definition of the sample and query complexity of
\texttt{Base}(\,\cdot\,\|\,\delta^{\prime})
in terms of
n_{\texttt{weak}}(\delta^{\prime})
. This lemma is restated in more detail and proved in
Lemma

C.15
.
∎

Of the two claims, the first one is easier to show, since it is a direct consequence of boosting-by-filtering
[
19
]
. In particular, since there is no composition step involved in going from level
h
to level
h+\frac{1}{2}
, the distributional shift is only due to the boosting step, and the analysis of this is standard. Next, we sketch how to bootstrap
\mathrm{Hypothesis}_{h}
into
\mathrm{Hypothesis}_{h+\frac{1}{2}}
, implying the statement of
Lemma

C.3
.

C.1
Analysis of half-integer steps: Proof of
Lemma

C.3

In this section we analyze
Lemma

C.3
. We fix a level
h\in\mathbb{Z}
and a particular
\texttt{node}\in\mathcal{T}_{h+\frac{1}{2}}
. We let
\widehat{\pi}^{0},\cdots,\widehat{\pi}^{k-1}
denote the
k
models returned by
\texttt{AutoLearn}_{h}
, corresponding to nodes in
\texttt{Ch}(\texttt{node})
(and which are invoked on distributions
\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}^{\prime}}
for
\texttt{node}^{\prime}\in\texttt{Ch}(\texttt{node})
). Let us index the nodes in
\texttt{Ch}(\texttt{node})
as
(\texttt{node}_{0},\cdots,\texttt{node}_{k-1})
. To prove
Lemma

C.3
, we translate the analysis of
\texttt{AutoLearn}.\texttt{warmup}
(cf.
Theorem

B.1
) for the case of
L=1
and setting
\bm{X}\leftarrow\bm{X}_{h}
. Since
L=1
, some parts of the analysis greatly simplify.

First we begin by introducing some notation before jumping into the proof of the result. Let
(\mathcal{H}_{\texttt{node}_{j}})_{j=0}^{k-1}
denote the filtration where
\mathcal{H}_{\texttt{node}_{j-1}}
captures all randomness up until the point where the dataset
D_{\texttt{node}_{j}}
is collected: this includes the randomness in the construction of the models
\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{node}_{0})},\cdots,\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{node}_{j-1})}
corresponding to nodes
\texttt{node}_{0},\cdots,\texttt{node}_{j-1}\in\texttt{Ch}(\texttt{node})
, as well as the randomness in the instances from
D_{\texttt{node}}
which contribute to the datasets
D_{\texttt{node}_{0}},\cdots,D_{\texttt{node}_{j-1}}
. Define the following distribution over
\bm{X}_{h}
,

\lambda_{\texttt{node}_{j}}(\cdot)\propto\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}}(\cdot)w_{\texttt{node}_{j}}(\cdot),

(36)

where
w_{\texttt{node}_{j}}({\bm{x}})=\alpha_{r}^{j,k}
for
r=\mathrm{rank}({\bm{x}}\|\,\texttt{node}_{j})
.
Recall that
\mathrm{rank}(\,\cdot\,\|\,\texttt{node}_{j})
(
Eq.

33
) captures the number of models among
\big\{\widehat{\pi}^{0},\cdots,\widehat{\pi}^{j-1}\big\}
which correctly predict the terminal state of the instance
X\in\bm{X}_{h}
. First, we write down a test-error decomposition, similar to the one in
Lemma

B.2
, where we break down the accuracy of the model returned by an instantiation of
\texttt{AutoLearn}_{h+\frac{1}{2}}
(i.e., corresponding to some
node
) into the accuracy of the models returned by the instances of
\texttt{AutoLearn}_{h}
it spawns (i.e., corresponding to
\texttt{Ch}(\texttt{node})
).

For the purpose of this section, we will denote
k\leftarrow k_{h+\frac{1}{2}}
and
L\leftarrow L_{h+\frac{1}{2}}=1
to simplify notation.

Lemma C.5

(Test-error decomposition)
.

Let
\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{node})}
denote the model returned by the instantiation of
\texttt{AutoLearn}_{h+\frac{1}{2}}
corresponding to
\texttt{node}\in\mathcal{T}_{h+\frac{1}{2}}
. Let
\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{node}_{j})}
denote the model returned by
\texttt{AutoLearn}_{h}
corresponding to each
\texttt{node}_{j}\in\texttt{Ch}(\texttt{node})
. Then,

\mathrm{Pr}_{X\sim\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}}}\big[\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{node})}(X)\neq\pi^{\star}_{\tau_{h}}(X)\big]\leqslant\beta^{0,k}_{0}+\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}(\texttt{err}_{j}-\texttt{err}_{\star})\cdot\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}}}\big[\alpha^{j,k}_{\mathrm{rank}(X\|\texttt{node}_{j})}\mid\mathcal{H}_{\texttt{node}_{k-1}}\big]

Here,
\beta^{j,k}_{r}
is defined in
Eq.

2
, and
\texttt{err}_{j}
is the test error of
\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{node}_{j})}
under the distribution
\lambda_{\texttt{node}_{j}}
,

\texttt{err}_{j}=\mathrm{Pr}_{X\sim\lambda_{\texttt{node}_{j}}}\big[\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{node}_{j})}(X)\neq\pi^{\star}_{\tau_{h}}(X)\big]

Proof.
Since there is no composition step involved, this result is immediate from the proof of
Lemma

B.2
by the choice
L=1
,
\mathcal{K}=\bm{X}^{L}
, and
\rho\leftarrow\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}}
,
\sigma\leftarrow\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}}
and
\lambda_{j}\leftarrow\lambda_{\texttt{node}_{j}}
.
∎

Since there is no composition involved in going from step
h
to
h+\frac{1}{2}
, the learner has a sampling oracle for
\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}}
, which can be used to generate samples from
\lambda_{\texttt{node}_{j}}
by rejection sampling (which coincides with inverted sampling when
L=1
). In order to analyze the error decomposition in
Lemma

C.5
first we define events which track whether the instances of
\texttt{AutoLearn}_{h}
corresponding to nodes in
\texttt{Ch}(\texttt{node})
are invoked on sufficiently large datasets to satisfy the inductive guarantee we assume for nodes at level
h
. To this end, define for
0\leqslant j\leqslant k-1
,

\texttt{ABORT}_{h}[\texttt{node}_{j}]=\big\{p_{\texttt{node}_{j}}\leqslant p^{\star}_{j}\big\},\text{ where, }p^{\star}_{j}=e^{-ck}

where
c>0
is the constant in
Lemma

B.9
. With this, the next lemma simply uses the definition of
\mathrm{Hypothesis}_{h}
to argue that if any node
\texttt{node}_{j}\in\texttt{Ch}(\texttt{node})
does not abort, we can get a weak learning guarantee for
\texttt{node}_{j}
.

Lemma C.6

(Weak learner guarantee for
\texttt{AutoLearn}_{h}
)
.

Assume the inductive hypothesis
\mathrm{Hypothesis}_{h}(\delta)
. Conditioned on the event that
\texttt{ABORT}_{h}[\texttt{node}_{j}]
is false, with probability at least
1-\delta
, the model
\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{node}_{j})}
returned by
\texttt{node}_{j}
satisfies,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}_{X\sim\lambda_{\texttt{node}_{j}}}\big[\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{node}_{j})}(X)\neq\pi^{\star}_{\tau_{h}}(X)\big]=\texttt{err}_{j}\leqslant\texttt{err}_{\star}\triangleq\frac{1}{4}

Proof.
This result is a direct consequence of the definition of
\mathrm{Hypothesis}_{h}(\delta)
.
∎

Next we argue about the probability that any of the nodes in
\texttt{Ch}(\texttt{node})
abort. Define,

\displaystyle p_{\texttt{node}_{j}}=\mathrm{Pr}_{{\bm{x}}\sim\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}}}\big(\texttt{InvSampling}(\{{\bm{x}}\}\ \|\ \Pi_{j},k)\neq\perp\big),\text{ where, }\Pi_{j}=\big\{\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{node}_{0})},\cdots,\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{node}_{j-1})}\big\}.

This is the probability with which an instance
{\bm{x}}\sim\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}}
is accepted by split-and-inverted-sampling (
Algorithms

4
and
3
). Next we show that if
p_{\texttt{node}_{j}}
is large, then the instantiation of
\texttt{AutoLearn}_{h}
corresponding to
\texttt{node}_{j}
is likely to be instantiated with a large dataset, implying that
\texttt{node}_{j}
indeed returns a weak learner with high probability.

Lemma C.7

(Truthful aborts at half-levels)
.

Suppose
\texttt{AutoLearn}_{h+\frac{1}{2}}(\,\cdot\,\|\,\tau_{h+\frac{1}{2}},\delta)
(cf.
Algorithm

1
) is invoked on a dataset of at least
n_{\texttt{sample}}^{\star}(h+\frac{1}{2},\delta)=2ke^{ck}\cdot n_{\texttt{sample}}^{\star}(h,\delta)
instances where
c>0
is the constant in the exponent of
\beta^{0,k}_{0}
in
Lemma

B.9
. Then, for any index
j
such that
p_{\texttt{node}_{j}}\geqslant e^{-ck}
,
\mathrm{Pr}\big(|D_{\texttt{node}_{j}}|<n_{\texttt{sample}}^{\star}(h,\delta)\mid\mathcal{H}_{\texttt{node}_{j-1}}\big)\leqslant\delta
.

Proof.
The proof of this result is identical to that of
Lemma

B.5
, where we use the Chernoff bound to control the deviations of
n^{\prime}
coins each having probability of heads at least
p_{\texttt{node}_{j}}
.
∎

The complement case is when
p_{\texttt{node}_{j}}
is small and
\texttt{node}_{j}
is no longer likely to succeed. Similar to in the analysis of
\texttt{AutoLearn}.\texttt{warmup}
where we established an explicit formula for
p_{j}
,
p_{\texttt{node}_{j}}
satisfies the equation,

p_{\texttt{node}_{j}}=\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}}}\left[\frac{\alpha^{j,k}_{\mathrm{rank}(X\|\texttt{node}_{j})}}{\alpha^{j,k}_{\max}}\ \middle|\ \mathcal{H}_{\texttt{node}_{j-1}}\right]

Which corresponds to setting
L=1
in
Eq.

17
. In particular, combining with
Lemma

C.7
, the test error decomposition in
Lemma

C.5
, the upper bound on
\beta_{0}^{0,k}
from
Lemma

B.8
and simplifying, we arrive at the following result which implies
Lemma

C.3
.

Lemma C.8

(Analysis of aggregation step)
.

Let
\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{node})}
denote the model returned by the instantiation of
\texttt{AutoLearn}_{h+\frac{1}{2}}
corresponding to some
\texttt{node}\in\mathcal{T}_{h+\frac{1}{2}}
. Then, assuming the hypothesis
\mathrm{Hypothesis}_{h}(\delta)
, we have that with probability at least
1-2k\delta
,

\mathrm{Pr}_{X\sim\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}}}\big[\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{node})}(X)\neq\pi^{\star}_{\tau_{h}}(X)\big]\leqslant 3\sqrt{k}\cdot e^{-ck}

Further, under
\mathrm{Hypothesis}_{h}(\delta)
, this guarantee is achieved if
\texttt{AutoLearn}_{h+\frac{1}{2}}
draws
n_{\texttt{sample}}^{\star}(h+\frac{1}{2},2k\delta)
samples from
\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}}
. The number of queries made to
\texttt{iCoT}(\cdot)
is at most
n_{\texttt{query}}^{\star}(h+\frac{1}{2},2k\delta)
.

Proof.
We first prove the bound on the accuracy, and subsequently show the recursion on the sample and query complexity.

Bound on accuracy.

Conditioned on the event that
\texttt{ABORT}_{h}[\texttt{node}_{j}]
is false for some
j
, we have that
\texttt{err}_{j}\leqslant\texttt{err}_{\star}
. By a union bound, across all iterations where
\texttt{ABORT}_{h}[\texttt{node}_{j}]
is true, by
Lemma

C.5
, and using the definition of
p_{\texttt{node}_{j}}
, w.p. at least
1-2k\delta
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}_{X\sim\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}}}\big[\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{node})}(X)\neq\pi^{\star}_{\tau_{h}}(X)\big]

\displaystyle\leqslant\beta_{0}^{0,k}+\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}e^{-k}\cdot\alpha^{j,k}_{\max}

\displaystyle\overset{(i)}{\leqslant}e^{-ck}+2\sqrt{k}\cdot e^{-ck}

\displaystyle\leqslant 3\sqrt{k}\cdot e^{-ck}

In
(i)
, we use
Lemmas

B.8
and
B.9
(the bound on
\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\alpha^{j,k}_{\max}
is implied by the bound on
\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\mathfrak{r}_{j}
). The bound on the number of samples drawn required from
\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}}
is derived in
Lemma

C.7
.

Bound on query and sample complexity of
\texttt{AutoLearn}_{h+\frac{1}{2}}
.

\texttt{AutoLearn}_{h+\frac{1}{2}}
must be invoked on a dataset of size
n_{\texttt{sample}}^{\star}\big(h+\frac{1}{2},2k\delta\big)=n_{\texttt{sample}}^{\star}(h,\delta)\cdot 2ke^{ck}
to be able to invoke
Lemma

C.7
. On the other hand, the query complexity of
\texttt{AutoLearn}_{h+\frac{1}{2}}
is upper bounded by,

\displaystyle n_{\texttt{query}}^{\star}\big(h+\tfrac{1}{2},2k\delta\big)

\displaystyle=n_{\texttt{sample}}^{\star}(h+\tfrac{1}{2},2k\delta)+k\cdot n_{\texttt{query}}^{\star}(h,\delta).

(37)

The
n_{\texttt{sample}}^{\star}(h+\tfrac{1}{2},2k\delta)
term accounts for labeling the terminal state in each invocation of
InvSampling
- note that
\texttt{AutoLearn}_{h+\frac{1}{2}}
processes a dataset of at most
n_{\texttt{sample}}^{\star}(h+\tfrac{1}{2},2k\delta)
instances (cf.
14
of
Algorithm

1
). The
k\cdot n_{\texttt{query}}^{\star}(h,\delta)
term arises as the cost of training the
k
children invocations of
\texttt{AutoLearn}_{h}
.
∎

C.2
Proof of
Lemma

C.4

This is the more challenging of the two induction steps (
Lemmas

C.4
and
C.3
), since we have to deal with the composition step. Fortunately, the analysis is quite similar to the one we carried out for
\texttt{AutoLearn}.\texttt{warmup}
. To draw the parallel most easily, in this section we will denote the branching factor as
k\leftarrow k_{h+1}
, and the decomposition factor as
L\leftarrow L_{h+1}
.

We define a sequence of events
\mathcal{K}_{\texttt{node}_{0}},\cdots\mathcal{K}_{\texttt{node}_{k-1}}\subseteq\bm{X}_{h}^{L}\times[0,1]^{kL}
which play the same role as the
\mathcal{K}_{0},\cdots,\mathcal{K}_{k-1}
events in
Lemma

B.2
; here
\mathcal{K}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}\subseteq\bm{X}_{h}^{L}\times[0,1]^{(j+1)L}
. We index the nodes in
\texttt{Ch}(\texttt{node})
as
(\texttt{node}_{0},\cdots,\texttt{node}_{k-1})
. To prove
Lemma

C.4
, we translate the analysis of
\texttt{AutoLearn}.\texttt{warmup}
(cf.
Theorem

B.1
) by setting
\bm{X}\leftarrow\bm{X}_{h}
,
\rho\leftarrow\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}}
. In correspondence with this choice, we denote
\underline{\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}}}
as the distribution obtained from
\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}}
by the same operation which takes
\rho\mapsto\sigma
, by converting it into a distribution over
(S\times\Sigma^{\tau_{h}})^{L}
from a distribution over
S\times\Sigma^{\tau_{h+1}}
by labeling every
L^{\text{th}}
state via
f^{\star}
. On the other hand, the equivalent of
\lambda_{j}
in
Lemma

B.3
is the following distribution over
\bm{X}_{h}
,

\lambda_{\texttt{node}_{j}}(\cdot)\propto\left(\frac{1}{L}\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}\mathrm{Pr}\big(X_{i}=\cdot\mid(\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\in\mathcal{K}_{\texttt{node}_{j}},\mathcal{H}_{\texttt{node}_{j-1}}\big)\right)w_{\texttt{node}_{j}}(\cdot),

(38)

where the probability is computed over
X=(X_{0},\cdots,X_{L-1})\sim\underline{\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}}}
and
\bm{\eta}\sim\operatorname{Unif}([0,1])^{\otimes kL}
, and recall
\mathcal{H}_{\texttt{node}_{j-1}}
captures all randomness until the dataset
D_{\texttt{node}_{j}}
is collected. The definition of the events
\mathcal{K}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}
is deferred to below
Eq.

39
, while the weight function
w_{\texttt{node}_{j}}(X)=\alpha_{r}^{j,k}
for
r=\mathrm{rank}(X\|\texttt{node}_{j})
is as defined in
Eq.

33
. For
\underline{X}=(X_{0},\cdots,X_{L-1})\sim\underline{\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}}}
, we analogously define the regression of
\texttt{node}_{j}
as the random variable,

R_{\texttt{node}_{j}}=\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}\alpha^{j,k}_{\mathrm{rank}(X_{i}\|\texttt{node}_{j})}.

We first present a decomposition of the test error analogous to
Lemma

B.2
in the analysis of
\texttt{AutoLearn}.\texttt{warmup}
. Prior to doing so, we mention that expressions involving events on different spaces are interpreted as described in
Eqs.

14
and
15
.

Lemma C.9

(Test-error decomposition)
.

Let
\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{node})}
denote the model returned by the instantiation of
\texttt{AutoLearn}_{h+1}
corresponding to
\texttt{node}\in\mathcal{T}_{h+1}
. Let
\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{node}_{j})}
denote the model returned by
\texttt{AutoLearn}_{h+\frac{1}{2}}
corresponding to
\texttt{node}_{j}\in\texttt{Ch}(\texttt{node})
. For any sequence of events
\mathcal{K}_{\texttt{node}_{k-1}}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq\mathcal{K}_{\texttt{node}_{0}}\subseteq\bm{X}_{h}^{L}\times[0,1]^{kL}
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}_{X\sim\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}}}\big[\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{node})}(X)\neq\pi^{\star}_{\tau_{h+1}}(X)\big]

\displaystyle\leqslant L\cdot\beta^{0,k}_{0}+\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}(\texttt{err}_{j}-\texttt{err}_{\star})\cdot\mathbb{E}\big[R_{\texttt{node}_{j}}\cdot\mathbb{I}((\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\in\mathcal{K}_{\texttt{node}_{j}})\mid\mathcal{H}_{\texttt{node}_{k-1}}\big]+\mathrm{Pr}\big((\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\not\in\mathcal{K}_{\texttt{node}_{k-1}}\mid\mathcal{H}_{\texttt{node}_{k-1}}\big)

where,
\underline{X}\sim\underline{\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}}}
,
\bm{\eta}\sim\operatorname{Unif}([0,1])^{\otimes kL}
, the weight function
\beta^{j,k}_{r}
is defined in
Eqs.

2
and
3
while
\texttt{err}_{j}
is the test error of
\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{node}_{j})}
under the distribution
\lambda_{\texttt{node}_{j}}
,
\texttt{err}_{j}=\mathrm{Pr}_{X\sim\lambda_{\texttt{node}_{j}}}\big[\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{node}_{j})}(X)\neq\pi^{\star}_{\tau_{h}}(X)\big]
.

Proof.
The proof of this result follows from the statement of
Lemma

B.2
by setting
\rho\leftarrow\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}}
, which corresponds to setting
\sigma\leftarrow\underline{\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}}}
and
\lambda_{j}\leftarrow\lambda_{\texttt{node}_{j}}
, and
\mathcal{K}_{j}\leftarrow\mathcal{K}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}
and
\mathcal{H}_{j}\leftarrow\mathcal{H}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}
.
∎

The prediction error of
\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{node}_{j})}
is measured under
\lambda_{\texttt{node}_{j}}
in the definition of
\texttt{err}_{j}
, but the corresponding invocation of
\texttt{AutoLearn}_{h+\frac{1}{2}}
trains on data drawn from a different distribution
\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}
. Our next result shows that these two distributions satisfy a density ratio bound when the event
\mathcal{K}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}
is chosen appropriately.

Lemma C.10

(Density ratio bound at depth
h+\frac{1}{2}
)
.

Fix some
0\leqslant j\leqslant k-1
, and recall that
\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}
denote the distribution over instances the i.i.d. dataset
D_{\texttt{node}_{j}}
is sampled from. Consider the filtration
(\mathcal{H}_{\texttt{node}_{j}})_{j=0}^{k-1}
and sequence of events,
(\mathcal{U}_{\texttt{node}_{j}})_{j=0}^{k-1}
such that
\mathcal{U}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}\subseteq\bm{X}_{h}^{L}
and is a measurable function of
\mathcal{H}_{j-1}
. Fix some constant
c\in(0,1)
. Construct a sequence of events
\mathcal{K}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}\in\bm{X}_{h}^{L}\times[0,1]^{(j+1)L}
via:

\mathcal{K}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}\leftarrow\texttt{ConstructEvent}(\mathcal{U}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}\cap\mathcal{K}_{{\texttt{node}_{j-1}}}\,\|\,c,\underline{\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}}},\mathcal{H}_{{\texttt{node}_{j-1}}})

where
\mathcal{K}_{\texttt{node}_{-1}}=\bm{X}_{h}^{L}
and
\texttt{ConstructEvent}(\cdot)
is defined in
Lemma

B.3
. Then, for each
0\leqslant j\leqslant k-1
,

\mathcal{K}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}\subseteq\mathcal{U}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}\cap\mathcal{K}_{{\texttt{node}_{j-1}}}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathrm{Pr}(\mathcal{K}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}\mid\mathcal{U}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}\cap\mathcal{K}_{\texttt{node}_{j-1}},\mathcal{H}_{\texttt{node}_{j-1}})\geqslant 1-c

And the following density ratio bound is satisfied,

\left\|\frac{\lambda_{\texttt{node}_{j}}}{\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}}\right\|_{\infty}\lesssim\frac{c^{-1}\log(L)}{\mathrm{Pr}\big(\mathcal{U}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}\cap\mathcal{K}_{{\texttt{node}_{j-1}}}\mid|\mathcal{I}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}|\geqslant 1,\mathcal{H}_{{\texttt{node}_{j-1}}}\big)}

Here,
\mathcal{I}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}
is the set of accepted indices in a run of split-and-inverted-sampling (
Algorithms

4
and
3
) for
{\bm{x}}\sim\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}}
(i.e.,
\underline{X}\sim\underline{\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}}}
).

Proof.
The statement and proof of this result mirrors that of
Lemma

B.3
. The relationship between
\underline{\lambda_{\texttt{node}}}
,
\lambda_{\texttt{node}_{j}}
,
\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}
is identical to the relationship between
\sigma
,
\lambda_{j}
and
\widehat{\lambda}_{j}
in the earlier lemma.
∎

The density ratio bound from this lemma allows the transfer of learning guarantees under
\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}
to
\lambda_{\texttt{node}_{j}}
via a change-of-measure argument in iterations where
\mathrm{Pr}\big(\mathcal{U}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}\cap\mathcal{K}_{{\texttt{node}_{j-1}}}\mid|\mathcal{I}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}|\geqslant 1,\mathcal{H}_{{\texttt{node}_{j-1}}}\big)
is sufficiently large. As in the analysis of
\texttt{AutoLearn}.\texttt{warmup}
, we define marginal and conditional acceptance probability of split-and-inverted-sampling (similar to
\widetilde{p}_{j}
in
Eq.

18
), and a corresponding abort event to track which child invocations of
\texttt{AutoLearn}_{h}
are invoked on sufficiently large effective datasets. For
0\leqslant j\leqslant k-1
, define,

\widetilde{p}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}=\mathrm{Pr}(|\mathcal{I}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}|\geqslant 1\mid\mathcal{K}_{\texttt{node}_{j-1}},\mathcal{H}_{\texttt{node}_{j-1}})=\mathbb{E}\left[1-\prod_{i=0}^{L-1}\Bigg(1-\frac{\alpha^{j,k}_{\mathrm{rank}(X_{i}\|\texttt{node}_{j})}}{\alpha^{j,k}_{\max}}\Bigg)\,\middle|\,\mathcal{K}_{\texttt{node}_{j-1}},\mathcal{H}_{\texttt{node}_{j-1}}\right]

Note that
\widetilde{p}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}
is closely related to
p_{\texttt{node}_{j}}=\mathrm{Pr}(|\mathcal{I}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}|\geqslant 1\mid\mathcal{H}_{\texttt{node}_{j-1}})
, which is the probability that split-and-inverted-sampling accepts
{\bm{x}}\sim\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}}
. With this in place, we define,

\texttt{ABORT}_{h+\frac{1}{2}}[\texttt{node}_{j}]=\big\{\widetilde{p}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}\leqslant p^{\star}_{j}\big\}

where
p^{\star}_{j}
is as defined earlier in
Eq.

18
. With this in place, we explicitly instantiate the sequence of
\mathcal{K}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}
events. First, we define,

\mathcal{U}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}=\big\{\widetilde{p}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}\leqslant p^{\star}_{j}\text{ and }R_{\texttt{node}_{j}}\geqslant\mathfrak{r}_{j}\big\}^{c},

(39)

where the threshold
\mathfrak{r}_{j}
is as defined earlier in
Eq.

31
.
\mathcal{U}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}
captures the event that the regression is controlled across all aborted iterations. This definition is the extension of the version we defined earlier in the analysis of
\texttt{AutoLearn}.\texttt{warmup}
in
Eq.

22
. With this definition of
\mathcal{U}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}
,
\mathcal{K}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}
is defined recursively as,

\mathcal{K}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}\leftarrow\texttt{ConstructEvent}(\mathcal{U}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}\cap\mathcal{K}_{{\texttt{node}_{j-1}}}\,\|\,c,\underline{\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}}},\mathcal{H}_{{\texttt{node}_{j-1}}})\text{ with }c=\frac{1}{32k},

with
\mathcal{K}_{\texttt{node}_{-1}}=\bm{X}_{h}^{L}
.

In the following lemma, we control the total probability mass of
\mathcal{K}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}
.

Lemma C.11

(Mass on good events)
.

For all
0\leqslant j\leqslant k-1
,
\mathrm{Pr}(\mathcal{K}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}\mid\mathcal{H}_{\texttt{node}_{j-1}})\geqslant\frac{15}{16}
.

Proof.
The proof of this result is identical to that of
Lemma

B.4
.
∎

With this in place, in any iteration
j
which does not abort, i.e.,
\widetilde{p}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}\geqslant p^{\star}_{j}
, following the analysis we described earlier in
Eq.

25
to
Eq.

26
, allows lower bounding
\mathrm{Pr}\big(\mathcal{U}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}\cap\mathcal{K}_{\texttt{node}_{j-1}}\mid|\mathcal{I}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}|\geqslant 1,\mathcal{H}_{\texttt{node}_{j-1}}\big)
by
\Omega(\widetilde{p}_{\texttt{node}_{j}})
. This implies that in any such iteration we have the density ratio bound,

\left\|\frac{\lambda_{\texttt{node}_{j}}}{\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}}\right\|_{\infty}\lesssim k^{7/2}\log(L)

(40)

Furthermore, in any non-aborted iteration
j
, we also show that the size of the dataset
D_{\texttt{node}_{j}}
passed on to the child node
\texttt{node}_{j}
to train a model via
\texttt{AutoLearn}_{h}
.

Lemma C.12

(Large
p_{\texttt{node}_{j}}\implies\texttt{node}_{j}
instantiated with a large dataset)
.

Fix any
0\leqslant j\leqslant k-1
such that
\widetilde{p}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}\geqslant p^{\star}_{j}
. If
\texttt{AutoLearn}_{h+1}
is invoked on a dataset with at least
C_{2}k^{7/2}\log(L)\cdot n_{\texttt{sample}}^{\star}(h+\frac{1}{2},\delta)
instances for sufficiently large
C_{2}>0
, then
\mathrm{Pr}(|D_{\texttt{node}_{j}}|<n_{\texttt{sample}}^{\star}(h+\frac{1}{2},\delta)\mid\mathcal{H}_{\texttt{node}_{j}})\leqslant\delta
.

Proof.
The proof of this result follows the same approach as that of
Lemma

B.5
, where we use a Chernoff bound to control the deviations of
|D_{\texttt{node}_{j}}|
which is an independent subsample of a
1/k
fraction of
D_{\texttt{node}}
with probability
p_{\texttt{node}_{j}}
, and noticing that
p_{\texttt{node}_{j}}\geqslant\Omega(p_{j}^{\star})
in non-aborted iterations (cf.
Eq.

28
). Using the definition of
p^{\star}_{j}
completes the proof.
∎

Next, we use the density ratio bound in
Eq.

40
, along with the sufficiently large size of the dataset
D_{\texttt{node}_{j}}
in any non-aborted iteration
j
(as implied by
Lemma

C.12
) to establish weak learning guarantees for
\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{node}_{j})}
. By the induction hypothesis at the previous depth (
\mathrm{Hypothesis}_{h+\frac{1}{2}}
), and a change of measure argument, we next show that the model
\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{node}_{j})}
satisfies a weak learning guarantee under the distribution
\lambda_{\texttt{node}_{j}}
.

Lemma C.13

(Weak learning guarantee at depth
h+\frac{1}{2}
)
.

Consider any iteration
j
such that
\widetilde{p}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}\geqslant p^{\star}_{j}
. Assume
\mathrm{Hypothesis}_{h+\frac{1}{2}}(\delta)
and furthermore that
k_{h+1},\log(L_{h+1})\leqslant\log^{\mathcal{O}(1)}(T)
. Suppose
k_{h+\frac{1}{2}}=c_{1}\log\log(T)
for a sufficiently large constant
c_{1}
, then with probability at least
1-2\delta
, the model
\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{node}_{j})}
returned by
\texttt{node}_{j}
satisfies,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}_{X\sim\lambda_{\texttt{node}_{j}}}\big[\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{node}_{j})}(X)\neq\pi^{\star}_{\tau_{h}}(X)\big]=\texttt{err}_{j}\leqslant\texttt{err}_{\star}=\frac{1}{4}

Proof.
The proof of this result uses the density ratio bound we derived in
Equation

27
, the accuracy guarantee implied by
\mathrm{Hypothesis}_{h+\frac{1}{2}}(\delta)
, and a change-of-measure argument. In particular, if
\texttt{ABORT}_{h+\frac{1}{2}}[\texttt{node}_{j}]
does not occur, then with probability at least
1-\delta
, the instance of
\texttt{AutoLearn}_{h+\frac{1}{2}}
corresponding to
\texttt{node}_{j}
is invoked on a dataset of size at least
n_{\texttt{sample}}^{\star}(h+\tfrac{1}{2},\delta)
(cf.
Lemma

C.12
). By
\mathrm{Hypothesis}_{h+\frac{1}{2}}(\delta)
, with probability at least
1-\delta
, the model
\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{node}_{j})}
returned by this node satisfies,

\mathrm{Pr}_{X\sim\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}}\big[\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{node}_{j})}(X)\neq\pi^{\star}_{\tau_{h}}(X)\big]\leqslant 3\sqrt{k_{h+\frac{1}{2}}}\cdot e^{-ck_{h+\frac{1}{2}}}.

By the density ratio bound in
Eq.

40
, with probability at least
1-2\delta
,

\mathrm{Pr}_{X\sim\lambda_{\texttt{node}_{j}}}\big[\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{node}_{j})}(X)\neq\pi^{\star}_{\tau_{h}}(X)\big]\leqslant\mathcal{O}\big(k_{h+1}^{7/2}\log(L_{h+1})\big)\cdot\mathrm{Pr}_{X\sim\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}}\big[\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{node}_{j})}(X)\neq\pi^{\star}_{\tau_{h}}(X)\big]\leqslant\frac{1}{4}.

for
k_{h+1}=\log(L)=\log^{\mathcal{O}(1)}(T)
, choosing
k_{h+\frac{1}{2}}=\Omega(\log\log(T))
with a sufficiently large implicit constant gives the final inequality.
∎

Next we analyze the aborted iterations, i.e.,
\widetilde{p}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}\leqslant p_{j}^{\star}
. In these iterations, we analyze the regression
R_{\texttt{node}_{j}}
and show that if
p_{\texttt{node}_{j}}
is small, then
R_{\texttt{node}_{j}}
is also likely to be small.

Lemma C.14

(Aborted iterations have low regression)
.

Define
\mathfrak{r}_{j}
as in
Eq.

31
. Supposing that
\widetilde{p}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}\leqslant p_{j}^{\star}
, then,
\mathrm{Pr}_{\underline{X}\sim\underline{\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}}}}\big(R_{\texttt{node}_{j}}\geqslant\mathfrak{r}_{j}\mid\mathcal{K}_{\texttt{node}_{j-1}},\mathcal{H}_{\texttt{node}_{j-1}}\big)\leqslant\frac{1}{32k}
.

Proof.
The proof of this structural result is identical to the analysis of
Lemma

B.7
, connecting
\widetilde{p}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}
and
R_{\texttt{node}_{j}}
via Markov’s inequality.
∎

Finally, we combine these results to prove
Lemma

C.4
. We restate this result in slightly more detail below.

Lemma C.15

(Analysis of composition step)
.

Let
\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{node})}
denote the model returned by the instantiation of
\texttt{AutoLearn}_{h+1}
corresponding to some
\texttt{node}\in\mathcal{T}_{h+1}
. Then, assuming
L_{h+1}=L=2^{\sqrt{\log_{2}(T)}}
,
k_{h+1}=k=c_{1}\sqrt{\log(T)}
and
k_{h+\frac{1}{2}}=c_{2}\log\log(T)
for appropriate
c_{1},c_{2}>0
, as well as
\mathrm{Hypothesis}_{h+\frac{1}{2}}(\delta)
, with probability at least
1-4\delta k_{h+1}
,

\mathrm{Pr}_{X\sim\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}}}\big[\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{node})}(X)\neq\pi^{\star}_{\tau_{h+1}}(X)\big]\leqslant\frac{1}{4}

Assuming
\mathrm{Hypothesis}_{h+\frac{1}{2}}(\delta)
, this guarantee is achieved as long as
\texttt{AutoLearn}_{h+1}
draws,

n_{\texttt{sample}}^{\star}(h+1,2k\delta)=n_{\texttt{sample}}^{\star}(h+\tfrac{1}{2},\delta)\cdot C_{2}k_{h+1}^{7/2}\log(L_{h+1})

samples from
\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}}
, for some large constant
C_{2}>0
. In the process, the number of queries made to
\texttt{iCoT}(\cdot)
is upper bounded by,

n_{\texttt{query}}^{\star}(h+1,2k\delta)=L_{h+1}\cdot n_{\texttt{sample}}^{\star}(h+1,2k\delta)+k_{h+1}\cdot n_{\texttt{query}}^{\star}(h+\tfrac{1}{2},\delta).

Proof.
We first prove the bound on the accuracy, and subsequently show the recursion on the sample and query complexity.

Bound on accuracy.

Let
\mathcal{J}_{\texttt{ABORT}}[\texttt{node}]\triangleq\big\{0\leqslant j\leqslant k-1:\widetilde{p}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}\leqslant p^{\star}_{j}\big\}
denote the set of aborted iterations, which is
\mathcal{H}_{\texttt{node}_{k-2}}
measurable. By the test-error decomposition in
Lemma

C.9
, the choice of
k=\Omega(\log(L))
(along with the upper bound on
\beta^{0,k}_{0}
in
Lemma

B.9
) and the lower bound we prove in
Lemma

C.11
showing
\mathrm{Pr}(\mathcal{K}_{\texttt{node}_{k-1}}\mid\mathcal{H}_{\texttt{node}_{k-1}})\geqslant\frac{15}{16}
,
