\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}_{X\sim\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}}}\big[\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{node})}(X)\neq\pi^{\star}_{\tau_{h+1}}(X)\big]

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{16}+\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}(\texttt{err}_{j}-\texttt{err}_{\star})\cdot\mathbb{E}\big[R_{\texttt{node}_{j}}\cdot\mathbb{I}((\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\in\mathcal{K}_{\texttt{node}_{j}})\mid\mathcal{H}_{\texttt{node}_{k-1}}\big]+\frac{1}{16}

(41)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{8}+\sum_{j\in\mathcal{J}_{\texttt{ABORT}}}\mathbb{E}[R_{\texttt{node}_{j}}\cdot\mathbb{I}\big(\{\widetilde{p}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}\leqslant p_{\texttt{node}_{j}}^{\star}\text{ and }R_{\texttt{node}_{j}}>\mathfrak{r}_{j}\}^{c}\big)\mid\mathcal{H}_{\texttt{node}_{k-1}}]+L\sum_{j\not\in\mathcal{J}_{\texttt{ABORT}}}(\texttt{err}_{j}-\texttt{err}_{\star})_{+}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{8}+\sum_{j\in\mathcal{J}_{\texttt{ABORT}}}\mathfrak{r}_{j}+L\sum_{j\not\in\mathcal{J}_{\texttt{ABORT}}}(\texttt{err}_{j}-\texttt{err}_{\star})_{+}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{4}+L\sum_{j\not\in\mathcal{J}_{\texttt{ABORT}}}(\texttt{err}_{j}-\texttt{err}_{\star})_{+}

(42)

where the last inequality uses
Lemma

B.8
, which shows that
\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\mathfrak{r}_{j}\leqslant\frac{1}{8}
. By
Lemma

C.13
, with probability
1-2\delta
, in any iteration
j\not\in\mathcal{J}_{\texttt{ABORT}}[\texttt{node}]
,
\texttt{err}_{j}\leqslant\texttt{err}_{\star}
. A union bound completes the bound on accuracy.

Query and sample complexity of
\texttt{AutoLearn}_{h+1}
.

The sample complexity of
\texttt{AutoLearn}_{h+1}
is
n_{\texttt{sample}}\big(h+1,2k\delta\big)=n_{\texttt{sample}}^{\star}(h+\tfrac{1}{2},\delta)\cdot C_{2}k^{7/2}\log(L)
as required by
Lemma

C.12
. The query complexity of
\texttt{AutoLearn}_{h+1}
is then bounded as,

\displaystyle n_{\texttt{query}}^{\star}\big(h+1,2k\delta\big)

\displaystyle=L\cdot n_{\texttt{sample}}^{\star}(h+1,2k\delta)+k\cdot n_{\texttt{query}}^{\star}(h+\tfrac{1}{2},\delta).

(43)

where the
L\cdot n_{\texttt{sample}}^{\star}(h+1,2k\delta)
term accounts for the query cost of invoking
\texttt{Split}(D_{\bm{x}}\|L)
and the
k\cdot n_{\texttt{query}}^{\star}(h+\tfrac{1}{2},\delta)
term for training the
k
children via
\texttt{AutoLearn}_{h+\frac{1}{2}}
.
∎

With this, we are ready to furnish a proof of the final recursive guarantee.

C.3
Final Recursive Guarantee: Proof of
Theorem

C.1

Correctness guarantee.

By
Lemmas

C.3
and
C.4
, we know that
\mathrm{Hypothesis}_{H+\frac{1}{2}}(\delta_{H+\frac{1}{2}})
is true where
\delta_{H+\frac{1}{2}}=\delta\cdot\prod_{h^{\prime}\in\{0,\frac{1}{2},\cdots,H+\frac{1}{2}\}}2k_{h^{\prime}}
. With
k_{H+\frac{1}{2}}
chosen as
c^{-1}\log(1/\varepsilon)\log\log(1/\varepsilon)
and redefining
\delta\leftarrow\delta/\prod_{h^{\prime}\in\{0,\frac{1}{2},\cdots,H+\frac{1}{2}\}}2k_{h^{\prime}}
, we get that with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\mathrm{Pr}_{{\bm{x}}\sim\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{root}}}\left[\widehat{\pi}({\bm{x}})\neq\pi^{\star}_{T}({\bm{x}})\right]\leqslant\varepsilon.

Noting that
\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{root}}
is the distribution over
D_{\texttt{node}}
with
\texttt{node}=\texttt{root}
, which is simply the initial distribution over instances,
\rho
, this proves the correctness guarantee for
AutoLearn
. Note that
\prod_{h^{\prime}\in\{0,\frac{1}{2},\cdots,H+\frac{1}{2}\}}2k_{h^{\prime}}\leqslant h_{\varepsilon}(T)=(\log(T))^{\mathcal{O}(H)}\cdot\log^{2}(1/\varepsilon)
. This guarantee requires
Base
to be instantiated with
\delta_{0}=\delta/h_{\varepsilon}(T)
.

Sample and query complexity.

By unrolling the induction guarantees in
Lemmas

C.4
and
C.3
, and using the definition of
\mathrm{Hypothesis}(H+\frac{1}{2},\delta)
, the sample complexity requirement of
AutoLearn
resolves to,
n_{\texttt{sample}}(H+\frac{1}{2},\delta)\geqslant n_{\texttt{sample}}^{\star}(H+\frac{1}{2},\delta)
where, plugging in the choices of
(k_{h})_{h\geqslant 0}
and
(L_{h})_{h\geqslant 0}
,

n_{\texttt{sample}}^{\star}(H+\tfrac{1}{2},\delta)\leqslant\frac{h_{\varepsilon}(T)}{\varepsilon}\cdot\big[n_{\texttt{weak}}\big(\delta/h_{\varepsilon}(T)\big)\vee\log(1/\delta)\big]

where
h_{\varepsilon}(T)=(\log(T))^{\mathcal{O}(H)}\cdot\log^{2}(1/\varepsilon)
upper bounds
\prod_{h^{\prime}\in\{0,\frac{1}{2},\cdots,H+\frac{1}{2}\}}2k_{h^{\prime}}
. The query complexity upper bound also resolves to the same quantity,

\displaystyle n_{\texttt{query}}^{\star}(H+\tfrac{1}{2},\delta)\leqslant\frac{h_{\varepsilon}(T)}{\varepsilon}\cdot\big[n_{\texttt{weak}}\big(\delta/h_{\varepsilon}(T)\big)\vee\log(1/\delta)\big]

This completes the proof of
Theorem

C.1
.

Appendix D
Analysis of
\texttt{AutoLearn}.\texttt{RL}
(
Algorithm

6
): Proof of
Theorem

4.2

\texttt{AutoLearn}.\texttt{RL}
is an invocation of
\texttt{AutoLearn}_{1}
, but where the base learner is instantiated as
\texttt{Base}(\,\cdot\,\|\,\delta^{\prime})\equiv\texttt{RLFineTune}\big(\cdot\big\|\,B,\varepsilon_{0},\delta^{\prime}\big)
(cf
Algorithm

5
) where
\varepsilon_{0}^{-1}\asymp k_{\frac{1}{2}}^{7/2}\log\big( L_{\frac 12} \big)
, with decomposition schedule
L_{\frac{1}{2}}=L\triangleq T/B
and
L_{1}=1
, and branching schedule
k_{\frac{1}{2}}=C\log(T/B)
and
k_{1}=C\log(1/\varepsilon)
for a sufficiently large absolute constant
C>0
.

We inherit the tree notation from the previous section to arrange the recursive calls of
\texttt{AutoLearn}_{h}
. Here, the tree
\mathcal{T}
has levels
\{0,\frac{1}{2},1\}
, with
\mathcal{T}_{1}=\{\texttt{root}\}
. For
h\in\{\frac{1}{2},1\}
and each
\texttt{node}\in\mathcal{T}_{h}
, let
\texttt{Ch}(\texttt{node})=(\texttt{node}_{0},\ldots,\texttt{node}_{k_{h}-1})
denote the ordered collection of
k_{h}
children of
node
, each lying at level
h-\frac{1}{2}
, and set

\mathcal{T}_{h-\frac{1}{2}}=\bigcup_{\texttt{node}\in\mathcal{T}_{h}}\texttt{Ch}(\texttt{node}).

For
1\leqslant j\leqslant k_{h}
, write
\texttt{Ch}_{<j}(\texttt{node})=\{\texttt{node}_{0},\ldots,\texttt{node}_{j-1}\}
for the first
j
children of
node
. In particular,
\mathcal{T}_{\frac{1}{2}}=\texttt{Ch}(\texttt{root})
and
|\mathcal{T}_{\frac{1}{2}}|=k_{1}
, while
|\mathcal{T}_{0}|=k_{1}k_{\frac{1}{2}}
. As before,
D_{\texttt{node}}
denotes the dataset processed by the invocation of
\texttt{AutoLearn}_{h}
corresponding to
node
, and
\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}}
denotes the distribution from which the elements of
D_{\texttt{node}}
are sampled; in particular,
\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{root}}=\rho
. If
\texttt{node}\in\mathcal{T}_{0}
, then
\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{node})}
is the model returned by
Base
. If
\texttt{node}\in\mathcal{T}_{h}
for
h\in\{1,\frac{1}{2}\}
, define

\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{node})}=\big(\widetilde{\pi}^{(\texttt{node})}\big)^{\circ L_{h}},\text{ where, }\widetilde{\pi}^{(\texttt{node})}=\texttt{Plu}\big(\{\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{node}^{\prime})}:\texttt{node}^{\prime}\in\texttt{Ch}(\texttt{node})\}\big)

Thus the root has
k_{1}
level-
\frac{1}{2}
children which it aggregates with
L_{1}=1
, while each level-
\frac{1}{2}
node returns the
L
-fold composition of
k_{\frac{1}{2}}
models which solve length-
B
instances.

D.1
Guess-and-Check: Simulating
\texttt{iCoT}(\cdot)
via
\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
and
\mathcal{V}

The
\texttt{iCoT}(\cdot)
oracle is used in
Algorithm

1
in two places: first, to compute the block-boundary states needed by
\texttt{Split}(\,\cdot\,\|\,L)
, and second, to compute the ranks used by
\texttt{InvSampling}(\cdot)
. In
Algorithm

6
, the
\texttt{iCoT}(\cdot)
oracle is not available to the learner. Instead, the learner has access to a reference model
\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
and an outcome verifier
\mathcal{V}
. However, both uses of
\texttt{iCoT}(\cdot)
can be simulated using the reference model and the outcome verifier via a guess-and-check procedure.

Observation D.1

(Simulating
\texttt{iCoT}(\cdot)
via guess-and-check)
.

Fix any length-
B
instance
{\bm{x}}\in S\times\Sigma^{B}
. By rolling out
\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
on
{\bm{x}}
and querying
\mathcal{V}
to verify each candidate terminal state, the correct terminal state
\pi^{\star}_{B}({\bm{x}})
can be identified with probability at least
1-\delta^{\prime}
using at most
\mathcal{O}(C_{\texttt{ans}}^{(B)}\log(1/\delta'))
calls to
\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
and
\mathcal{V}
.

The proof is immediate from the outcome coverage assumption: each independent rollout produces the correct terminal state with probability at least
(C_{\texttt{ans}}^{(B)})^{-1}
. Therefore, for a length-
T
instance, the boundary states required by
\texttt{Split}({\bm{x}}\|L)
can be identified by applying
D.1
sequentially to the
L=T/B
consecutive length-
B
blocks, where the blocks are processed sequentially, utilizing the terminal state of each instance as the starting state for the next block. This costs
\mathcal{O}(LC_{\texttt{ans}}^{(B)}\log(L/\delta'))
verifier queries and
\mathcal{O}(TC_{\texttt{ans}}^{(B)}\log(L/\delta'))
reference-model state generations per instance. We will refer to this approach as
\texttt{GuessAndCheck}(\,\cdot\,\|\,T,B,\delta^{\prime})
.

Similarly, to compute ranks in
InvSampling
, for a child
\texttt{node}_{j}
of a level-
\frac{1}{2}
node
node
, the learner evaluates
\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{node}^{\prime})}({\bm{x}})
for
\texttt{node}^{\prime}\in\texttt{Ch}_{<j}(\texttt{node})
and queries
\mathcal{V}({\bm{x}},\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{node}^{\prime})}({\bm{x}}))
to test whether this terminal prediction is correct. This requires at most
k_{\frac{1}{2}}
verifier queries per length-
B
sub-instance.

D.2
Analysis of the
h=\frac{1}{2}
Level: Boosting with Composition

We first analyze a node
\texttt{node}\in\mathcal{T}_{\frac{1}{2}}
, which is one of the
k_{1}
invocations of
\texttt{AutoLearn}_{\frac{1}{2}}
within
\texttt{AutoLearn}.\texttt{RL}
. The analysis closely follows the proof of
AutoLearn
in
Lemma

C.4
with
\tau=B
, and with
\texttt{NTP}_{\texttt{Full-CoT}}
replaced by
RLFineTune
. Calls to
\texttt{iCoT}(\cdot)
are simulated as described in
D.1
. In this section, we will let
k
denote
k_{\frac{1}{2}}
for succinctness.

We define an abstract sequence of events
\mathcal{K}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}
for
0\leqslant j\leqslant k-1
, which will be useful in defining the distributions under which we instantiate weak learning guarantees for
\widehat{\pi}^{0},\cdots,\widehat{\pi}^{k-1}
. We translate the analysis of
\texttt{AutoLearn}.\texttt{warmup}
to this setting, with the translation
\bm{X}\to S\times\Sigma^{B}
and
\rho\to\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}}
. In correspondence with this choice, let
\underline{\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}}}
denote the joint distribution of
\underline{X}=(X_{0},\ldots,X_{L-1})
, where
X_{i}=(s_{Bi},\bm{w}_{Bi+1:B(i+1)})
and
s_{Bi}=\pi^{\star}_{Bi}({\bm{x}})
for
{\bm{x}}=(s_{0},\bm{w}_{1:T})\sim\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}}
. Similar to how we defined it earlier in
Eq.

38
in the analysis of
AutoLearn
,
\lambda_{\texttt{node}_{j}}
is defined as the following distribution over
S\times\Sigma^{B}
,

\lambda_{\texttt{node}_{j}}(\cdot)\propto\left(\frac{1}{L}\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}\mathrm{Pr}\big(X_{i}=\cdot\mid(\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\in\mathcal{K}_{\texttt{node}_{j}},\mathcal{H}_{\texttt{node}_{j-1}}\big)\right)w_{\texttt{node}_{j}}(\cdot),

(44)

where the probability is computed under
\underline{X}\sim\underline{\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}}}
and
\bm{\eta}\sim\operatorname{Unif}([0,1])^{\otimes kL}
. The
\mathcal{K}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}
events are defined later, below
Eq.

47
, while the weight function
w_{\texttt{node}_{j}}(X)
equals
\alpha^{j,k}_{r}
with
r=\mathrm{rank}(X\|\texttt{node}_{j})=\big|\big\{\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{node}^{\prime})}(X)=\pi^{\star}_{B}(X):\texttt{node}^{\prime}\in\texttt{Sib}_{<j}(\texttt{node})\big\}\big|
. For
\underline{X}=(X_{0},\ldots,X_{L-1})\sim\underline{\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}}}
, we define the regression of
\texttt{node}_{j}
as,

R_{\texttt{node}_{j}}=\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}\alpha^{j,k}_{\mathrm{rank}(X_{i}\|\texttt{node}_{j})}.

As a consequence of the test error breakdown in
Lemma

C.9
, we have the following decomposition: for any sequence of events
\mathcal{K}_{\texttt{node}_{k-1}}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq\mathcal{K}_{\texttt{node}_{0}}\subseteq\bm{X}_{h}^{L}\times[0,1]^{kL}
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}_{X\sim\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}}}\big[\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{node})}(X)\neq\pi^{\star}_{T}(X)\big]

\displaystyle\leqslant L\cdot\beta^{0,k}_{0}+\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}(\texttt{err}_{j}-\texttt{err}_{\star})\cdot\mathbb{E}\big[R_{\texttt{node}_{j}}\cdot\mathbb{I}((\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\in\mathcal{K}_{\texttt{node}_{j}})\mid\mathcal{H}_{\texttt{node}_{k-1}}\big]+\mathrm{Pr}\big((\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\not\in\mathcal{K}_{\texttt{node}_{k-1}}\mid\mathcal{H}_{\texttt{node}_{k-1}}\big)

(45)

where probabilities and expectations are over
\underline{X}\sim\underline{\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}}}
,
\bm{\eta}\sim\operatorname{Unif}([0,1])^{\otimes kL}
, the weight function
\beta^{j,k}_{r}
is defined in
Eqs.

2
and
3
, while
\texttt{err}_{\star}=\frac{1}{4}
and
\texttt{err}_{j}
is the test error of the model
\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{node}_{j})}
returned by
Base
corresponding to
\texttt{node}_{j}\in\texttt{Ch}(\texttt{node})
under the distribution
\lambda_{\texttt{node}_{j}}
. That is,

\texttt{err}_{j}=\mathrm{Pr}_{X\sim\lambda_{\texttt{node}_{j}}}\big[\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{node}_{j})}(X)\neq\pi^{\star}_{B}(X)\big].

(46)

Note that the model
\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{node}_{j})}
is trained under i.i.d. examples drawn from the distribution
\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}
, but the prediction error
\texttt{err}_{j}
above is calculated under the distribution
\lambda_{\texttt{node}_{j}}
. In the sequel, we will show the
\mathcal{K}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}
events can be chosen in such a way that the two distributions satisfy a bound on the density ratio. To this end, define,

\mathcal{U}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}=\big\{\widetilde{p}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}\leqslant p_{j}^{\star}\text{ and }R_{\texttt{node}_{j}}\geqslant\mathfrak{r}_{j}\big\}^{c}

(47)

where
\mathfrak{r}_{j}
is defined in
Eq.

31
. This event captures the regression across all aborted iterations not growing to be too large, and extends the definitions we introduced earlier (e.g.,
Eq.

39
) in the analysis of
\texttt{AutoLearn}.\texttt{warmup}
and
AutoLearn
. The event
\mathcal{K}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}
is obtained recursively via,

\mathcal{K}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}\leftarrow\texttt{ConstructEvent}\big(\mathcal{U}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}\cap\mathcal{K}_{\texttt{node}_{j-1}}\mid c,\underline{\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}}},\mathcal{H}_{\texttt{node}_{j-1}}\big)\text{ where }c=\frac{1}{32k}

where
\texttt{ConstructEvent}(\cdot)
was defined earlier in
Lemma

C.10
. With this definition, the
\mathcal{K}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}
events inherit the property we established earlier in
Lemma

C.11
: for all
0\leqslant j\leqslant k-1
,

\mathrm{Pr}(\mathcal{K}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}\mid\mathcal{H}_{\texttt{node}_{j-1}})\geqslant\frac{15}{16}.

(48)

By following the same argument we used to derive
Eq.

40
, in any iteration
j
which does not abort, i.e.,
\widetilde{p}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}\geqslant p^{\star}_{j}
, where
p^{\star}_{j}
was defined in
Eq.

18
, we have,

\left\|\frac{\lambda_{\texttt{node}_{j}}}{\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}}\right\|_{\infty}\lesssim k^{7/2}\log(L).

(49)

Define
n_{\texttt{weak}}^{\mathrm{RL}}(\delta)
as any upper bound on the sample complexity of
RLFineTune
(
Proposition

4.1
) at the target error
\varepsilon_{0}=\frac{1}{C_{1}k^{7/2}\log(L)}
, say,

n_{\texttt{weak}}^{\mathrm{RL}}(\delta)=\mathcal{O}\Big(k^{7/2}\log(L)\cdot\big(d\log(B |S| \Cblock)+\log(1/\delta)\cdot\log(k)\log\log(L)\big)\Big).

(50)

where
C_{1}
is sufficiently large.

Lemma D.2

(Large
\widetilde{p}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}\implies\texttt{node}_{j}
instantiated with a large dataset)
.

Suppose,
|D_{\texttt{node}}|\geqslant n_{\texttt{sample}}^{\star}(\frac{1}{2},\delta)=C_{1}k^{7/2}\log(L)\cdot n_{\texttt{weak}}^{\mathrm{RL}}(\delta/2k)
and consider any iteration
j
which does not abort, i.e.,
\widetilde{p}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}\geqslant p^{\star}_{j}
. Then, with probability
1-\frac{\delta}{2k}
,
|D_{\texttt{node}_{j}}|\geqslant n_{\texttt{weak}}^{\mathrm{RL}}(\delta/2k)
.

Proof.
The proof is identical to that of
Lemma

C.12
.
∎

Next, we establish weak learning guarantees for the model
\widehat{\pi}^{j}
in iterations
j
which did not abort. Recall that
\texttt{AutoLearn}.\texttt{RL}
uses
RLFineTune
(
Algorithm

5
), which is trained to solve length-
\tau
instances in the datasets
D_{\texttt{node}_{j}}
for
j=0,\cdots,k-1
. Our prior work
[
40
]
established guarantees for
RLFineTune
, and via a change-of-measure argument to transfer learning guarantees under
\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}
to
\lambda_{\texttt{node}_{j}}
(facilitated by the density ratio bound in
Eq.

49
), this implies learning guarantees for
\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{node}_{j})}
under the distribution
\lambda_{\texttt{node}_{j}}
, which is precisely
\texttt{err}_{j}
.

Lemma D.3

(Weak learning guarantee for
RLFineTune
)
.

Fix a child
\texttt{node}_{j}\in\texttt{Ch}(\texttt{node})
. With probability at least
1-\frac{\delta}{k}
, in any iteration
j
which does not abort,
\texttt{err}_{j}\leqslant\texttt{err}_{\star}=\frac{1}{4}
, where
\texttt{err}_{j}
is defined in
Eq.

46
and
\texttt{err}_{\star}=\frac{1}{4}
.

Proof.
By
Equation

49
,
\|\lambda_{\texttt{node}_{j}}/\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}\|_{\infty}\lesssim k^{7/2}\log(L)
. In order for
\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{node}_{j})}
to achieve error
\frac{1}{4}
under
\lambda_{\texttt{node}_{j}}
, it suffices for it to achieve prediction error at most
\varepsilon_{0}=(C_{1}k^{7/2}\log(L))^{-1}
under
\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}
as long as
C_{1}>0
is sufficiently large. This is guaranteed by
Proposition

4.1
and noting that by
Lemma

D.2
, with probability at least
1-\frac{\delta}{2k}
,
|D_{\texttt{node}_{j}}|\geqslant n_{\texttt{weak}}^{\mathrm{RL}}(\delta/2k)
meets the required sample threshold for
RLFineTune
to achieve error
\varepsilon_{0}
with probability at least
1-\frac{\delta}{2k}
.
∎

With these guarantees in place, we can finally derive a guarantee for
\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{node})}
.

Lemma D.4

(Constant-accuracy guarantee for
h=\frac{1}{2}
learners)
.

Suppose
\texttt{node}\in\mathcal{T}_{\frac{1}{2}}
is invoked on a dataset
D_{\texttt{node}}
consisting of at least
n_{\texttt{sample}}^{\star}(\tfrac{1}{2},\delta)
independent instances drawn from
\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}}
, where
n_{\texttt{sample}}^{\star}(\tfrac{1}{2},\delta)
is defined in
Lemma

D.2
. The associated model
\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{node})}:S\times\Sigma^{T}\to S
satisfies, with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\mathrm{Pr}_{{\bm{x}}\sim\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}}}\big[\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{node})}({\bm{x}})\neq\pi^{\star}_{T}({\bm{x}})\big]\leqslant\frac{1}{4}.

Furthermore, the query complexity in achieving this guarantee is upper bounded by,

\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(n_{\texttt{sample}}^{\star}(\tfrac{1}{2},\delta)\left(\frac{TC_{\texttt{ans}}^{(B)}}{B}+C_{\texttt{seq}}^{(B)}\right)\right),

and computational cost
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(n_{\texttt{sample}}^{\star}(\tfrac{1}{2},\delta)\cdot\big(TC_{\texttt{ans}}^{(B)}+BC_{\texttt{seq}}^{(B)}\big)\right)
.

Proof.
Let
\mathcal{J}_{\texttt{ABORT}}[\texttt{node}]=\{0\leqslant j\leqslant k-1:\widetilde{p}_{\texttt{node}_{j}}\leqslant p_{j}^{\star}\}
denote the set of aborted iterations. Combining
Eq.

45
and
Lemma

D.3
, we arrive at the following sequence of inequalities,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}_{{\bm{x}}\sim\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}}}\big[\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{node})}({\bm{x}})\neq\pi^{\star}_{T}({\bm{x}})\big]

\displaystyle\leqslant L\cdot\beta^{0,k}_{0}+\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}(\texttt{err}_{j}-\texttt{err}_{\star})\cdot\mathbb{E}\big[R_{\texttt{node}_{j}}\cdot\mathbb{I}((\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\in\mathcal{K}_{\texttt{node}_{j}})\mid\mathcal{H}_{\texttt{node}_{k-1}}\big]+\mathrm{Pr}((\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\not\in\mathcal{K}_{\texttt{node}_{k-1}}\mid\mathcal{H}_{\texttt{node}_{k-1}})

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{\leqslant}\frac{1}{8}+\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}(\texttt{err}_{j}-\texttt{err}_{\star})\cdot\mathbb{E}\big[R_{\texttt{node}_{j}}\cdot\mathbb{I}((\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\in\mathcal{K}_{\texttt{node}_{j}})\mid\mathcal{H}_{\texttt{node}_{k-1}}\big]

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{\leqslant}\frac{1}{8}+\sum_{j\in\mathcal{J}_{\texttt{ABORT}}}\mathfrak{r}_{j}+L\sum_{j\not\in\mathcal{J}_{\texttt{ABORT}}}(\texttt{err}_{j}-\texttt{err}_{\star})_{+}

where
(a)
uses
Eq.

48
and the choice of
k=\Theta(\log L)
, which bounds
L\beta^{0,k}_{0}\leqslant 1/16
(cf.
Lemma

B.9
), and
(b)
uses the same analysis we carried out in
Eq.

41
to
Eq.

42
. By
Lemma

B.8
and
Lemma

D.3
, with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\mathrm{Pr}_{{\bm{x}}\sim\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}}}\big[\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{node})}({\bm{x}})\neq\pi^{\star}_{T}({\bm{x}})\big]\leqslant\frac{1}{8}+\frac{1}{8}=\frac{1}{4}.

Sample and query complexity, and computational cost.

The sample complexity at the
h=\frac{1}{2}
depth is
n_{\texttt{sample}}^{\star}(\tfrac{1}{2},\delta)
from
Lemma

D.4
. For the bounds on the query complexity and computational cost, simulating block boundaries on
n_{\texttt{sample}}^{\star}(\tfrac{1}{2},\delta)
instances contributes a total of
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n_{\texttt{sample}}^{\star}(\tfrac{1}{2},\delta)\cdot(T/B)C_{\texttt{ans}}^{(B)})
verifier queries and
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n_{\texttt{sample}}^{\star}(\tfrac{1}{2},\delta)\cdot TC_{\texttt{ans}}^{(B)})
reference-model state generations. The calls to
RLFineTune
use
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n_{\texttt{sample}}^{\star}(\tfrac{1}{2},\delta))
accepted length-
B
training instances in total, which contributes
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n_{\texttt{sample}}^{\star}(\tfrac{1}{2},\delta)\cdot C_{\texttt{seq}}^{(B)})
verifier queries and requires
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n_{\texttt{sample}}^{\star}(\tfrac{1}{2},\delta)\cdot C_{\texttt{seq}}^{(B)})
length-
B
rollouts from
\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
. Combining both bounds results in the bound on query complexity and computational cost.
∎

D.3
Analysis of the
h=1
Level: Boosting to Error
\varepsilon

We next analyze the root node of
\mathcal{T}
. Since
L_{1}=1
, this node only carries out aggregation and no composition.
root
invokes the constant-accuracy guarantee from
Lemma

D.4
at each node in
\mathcal{T}_{\frac{1}{2}}
, on a sequence of reweighted distributions over length-
T
instances, and aggregates the resulting models by plurality vote. Because
L_{1}=1
,
InvSampling
produces
exact
i.i.d. samples from the tilted distribution
\lambda_{\texttt{root}_{j}}\propto\rho\cdot w_{\texttt{root}_{j}}
, so no change-of-measure or good-event truncation is needed (in contrast with the half level). The analysis exactly mirrors the no-composition aggregation step of
Lemma

C.8
. For the purpose of this section, we will let
k_{1}
be denoted as
k
.

Throughout, we write
\texttt{root}_{j}
for the
j^{\text{th}}
child of the root and set
\rho=\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{root}}
. The models
\{\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{root}_{j})}:0\leqslant j<k\}
produced by the half-level instances at the children of the root play the role of the weak learners at this level. For
X\sim\rho
, the rank
\mathrm{rank}(X\|\texttt{root}_{j})
counts how many of the previous models
\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{root}_{0})},\ldots,\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{root}_{j-1}})
correctly predict
\pi^{\star}_{T}(X)
. On the other hand, the weight
w_{\texttt{root}_{j}}(X)=\alpha^{j,k}_{\mathrm{rank}(X\|\texttt{root}_{j})}
defines the distribution
InvSampling
samples from (via rejection sampling from
\rho
) in the
j^{\text{th}}
child node of
root
. In particular, since
L_{1}=1
,
InvSampling
samples i.i.d. from,

\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{root}_{j}}(\cdot)\propto\rho(\cdot)\,w_{\texttt{root}_{j}}(\cdot),

The marginal acceptance probability of
InvSampling
at round
j
is, by specializing
Eq.

17
to
L=1
,

p_{\texttt{root}_{j}}=\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\rho}\left[\frac{\alpha^{j,k}_{\mathrm{rank}(X\|\texttt{root}_{j})}}{\alpha^{j,k}_{\max}}\,\middle|\,\mathcal{H}_{\texttt{root}_{j-1}}\right],

(51)

where
\mathcal{H}_{\texttt{root}_{j-1}}
is all the randomness within the algorithm up until the dataset
D_{\texttt{root}_{j}}
is collected.

p_{\texttt{root}_{j}}
is the probability with which an instance
{\bm{x}}\sim\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{root}}
is accepted by split-and-inverted-sampling (
Algorithms

4
and
3
). For each child
\texttt{root}_{j}\in\texttt{Ch}(\texttt{root})
, define the abort event,

\texttt{ABORT}_{1}[\texttt{root}_{j}]=\big\{p_{\texttt{root}_{j}}\leqslant p^{\star}_{j}\big\},\text{ where, }p^{\star}_{j}=e^{-ck}

(52)

where
c>0
is the constant in
Lemma

B.9
. Firstly, as a consequence of the test-error decomposition we proved earlier in
Lemma

C.5
, with
\texttt{node}\leftarrow\texttt{root}
and
\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{node}}\leftarrow\rho
, the model
\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{root})}
satisfies,

\mathrm{Pr}_{{\bm{x}}\sim\rho}\big[\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{root})}({\bm{x}})\neq\pi^{\star}_{T}({\bm{x}})\big]\leqslant\beta^{0,k}_{0}+\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}(\texttt{err}_{j}-\texttt{err}_{\star})\cdot\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\rho}\big[\alpha^{j,k}_{\mathrm{rank}(X\|\texttt{root}_{j})}\big],

(53)

where
\texttt{err}_{\star}=\frac{1}{4}
and
\texttt{err}_{j}=\mathrm{Pr}_{X\sim\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{root}_{j}}}\big[\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{root}_{j})}(X)\neq\pi^{\star}_{T}(X)\big]
. First we argue that in any iteration which doesn’t abort, the dataset
D_{\texttt{root}_{j}}
must be sufficiently large.

Lemma D.5

(Truthful aborts at
root
)
.

Suppose
|D_{\texttt{root}}|\geqslant n_{\texttt{sample}}^{\star}(1,2k\delta)\triangleq 2e^{ck}\cdot n_{\texttt{sample}}^{\star}(\frac{1}{2},\delta)
, where
c>0
is the constant in the exponent of
\beta^{0,k}_{0}
in
Lemma

B.9
. Then, for any iteration
j
such that
p_{\texttt{root}_{j}}\geqslant e^{-ck}
,
\mathrm{Pr}\big(|D_{\texttt{root}_{j}}|<n_{\texttt{sample}}^{\star}(\frac{1}{2},\delta)\mid\mathcal{H}_{\texttt{root}_{j-1}}\big)\leqslant\delta
.

Proof.
The proof of this result follows the same structure as
Lemma

C.7
.
∎

As a consequence of this lemma, in the iterations that don’t abort, the corresponding instantiations of
\texttt{AutoLearn}_{\frac{1}{2}}
are invoked on a dataset
D_{\texttt{root}_{j}}
containing at least
n_{\texttt{sample}}^{\star}(\frac{1}{2},\delta)
samples.

Lemma D.6

(Weak learner guarantee for
h=\frac{1}{2}
)
.

Consider any
j
such that
p_{\texttt{root}_{j}}\geqslant p_{j}^{\star}
. With probability at least
1-2\delta
, the model
\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{root}_{j})}
returned by
\texttt{root}_{j}
satisfies,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}_{X\sim\lambda_{\texttt{root}_{j}}}\big[\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{root}_{j})}(X)\neq\pi^{\star}_{T}(X)\big]=\texttt{err}_{j}\leqslant\texttt{err}_{\star}\triangleq\frac{1}{4}

Proof.
Identical to
Lemma

C.6
. If
\texttt{ABORT}_{1}[\texttt{root}_{j}]
is false, then the instance of
\texttt{AutoLearn}_{\frac{1}{2}}
at
\texttt{root}_{j}
is invoked with at least
n_{\texttt{sample}}^{\star}(\tfrac{1}{2},\delta)
accepted instances from
\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{root}_{j}}
. As a consequence of
Lemma

D.4
, we have that,
\mathrm{Pr}_{X\sim\lambda_{\texttt{root}_{j}}}\big[\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{root}_{j})}(X)\neq\pi^{\star}_{T}(X)\big]\leqslant\frac{1}{4}
with probability at least
1-\delta
.
∎

Combining these lemmas, we establish a guarantee for the model
\pi^{(\texttt{root})}
.

Lemma D.7

(Guarantee at
root
)
.

Fix
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
. Let
\widehat{\pi}\leftarrow\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{root})}
denote the model returned by the instantiation of
\texttt{AutoLearn}_{1}
corresponding to
root
, i.e., the output of
\texttt{AutoLearn}.\texttt{RL}
. Suppose
k=C\log(1/\varepsilon)
. Then, with probability at least
1-2k\delta
,
\mathrm{Pr}_{X\sim\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{root}}}\big[\widehat{\pi}(X)\neq\pi^{\star}_{T}(X)\big]\leqslant\varepsilon
. Furthermore, this guarantee is achieved if
root
is invoked on a dataset of
n_{\texttt{sample}}^{\star}(1,2k\delta)
samples from
\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{root}}
.

Proof.
Conditioned on the event that
\texttt{ABORT}_{1}[\texttt{root}_{j}]
is false for some
j
, with probability at least
1-2\delta
, we have that
\texttt{err}_{j}\leqslant\texttt{err}_{\star}
(
Lemma

D.6
). Noting that in any iteration
j
where
\texttt{ABORT}_{1}[\texttt{root}_{j}]
is true,
p_{\texttt{root}_{j}}\leqslant p_{j}^{\star}
. Using the definition of
p_{\texttt{root}_{j}}
and by a union bound, w.p. at least
1-2k\delta
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}_{X\sim\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{root}}}\big[\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{root})}(X)\neq\pi^{\star}_{T}(X)\big]

\displaystyle\leqslant\beta_{0}^{0,k}+\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}e^{-ck}\cdot\alpha^{j,k}_{\max}

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{\leqslant}e^{-ck}+2\sqrt{k}\cdot e^{-ck}

\displaystyle\leqslant 3\sqrt{k}\cdot e^{-ck}

In
(a)
, we use
Lemmas

B.8
and
B.9
(the bound on
\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\alpha^{j,k}_{\max}
is implied by the bound on
\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\mathfrak{r}_{j}
). Finally, plugging in
k=C\log(1/\varepsilon)
for sufficiently large
C>0
completes the proof.
∎

D.4
Proof of
Theorem

4.2

Recall that
\texttt{AutoLearn}.\texttt{RL}
is the invocation of
\texttt{AutoLearn}_{1}
at
root
with the base learner
RLFineTune
. The accuracy guarantee for
\texttt{AutoLearn}.\texttt{RL}
is immediate:
Lemma

D.7
, applied at
root
with
\widehat{\lambda}_{\texttt{root}}=\rho
shows that the model
\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{root})}
returned by
\texttt{AutoLearn}.\texttt{RL}
satisfies
\mathrm{Pr}_{{\bm{x}}\sim\rho}\big[\widehat{\pi}^{(\texttt{root})}({\bm{x}})\neq\pi^{\star}_{T}({\bm{x}})\big]\leqslant\varepsilon
with probability at least
1-2k_{1}\delta
, and redefining
\delta\leftarrow\delta/2k_{1}
gives us the bound on accuracy. It remains to bound the sample, query, and computational complexity of
\texttt{AutoLearn}.\texttt{RL}
.

Bound on sample complexity.

By
Lemma

D.7
,
root
requires a dataset of
n_{\texttt{sample}}^{\star}(1,\delta)=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(d/\varepsilon)
instances drawn from
\rho
, resulting in the upper bound on sample complexity of
\texttt{AutoLearn}.\texttt{RL}
.

Bound on query complexity.

Verifier queries are incurred by
\texttt{AutoLearn}.\texttt{RL}
in two places. First, at
root
, the aggregation step of
\texttt{AutoLearn}_{1}
uses
\mathcal{V}
to compute the rank of each of the
n_{\texttt{sample}}^{\star}(1,\delta)
length-
T
instances with respect to the
k_{1}
child models, contributing
n_{\texttt{sample}}^{\star}(1,\delta)
verifier queries in total. Second, at each child
\texttt{node}\in\mathcal{T}_{\frac{1}{2}}
, by
Lemma

D.4
, each invocation of
\texttt{AutoLearn}_{\frac{1}{2}}
incurs a burn-in query cost of

\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{dTC_{\texttt{ans}}^{(B)}}{B}+dC_{\texttt{seq}}^{(B)}\right)=\frac{n_{\texttt{burn-in}}}{B},

Summing over the
k_{1}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(1)
children in
\mathcal{T}_{\frac{1}{2}}
and combining with the contribution from
root
gives,

n_{\texttt{query}}\leqslant\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon}\right)+\frac{n_{\texttt{burn-in}}}{B}.

Note that these bounds are stated for when the target error probability is
2k_{1}\delta
. Replacing
\delta\leftarrow\delta/2k_{1}
results in a bound which is larger by a factor of
\log(2k_1)=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(1)
.

Bound on computational cost.

The computational cost of
\texttt{AutoLearn}.\texttt{RL}
likewise decomposes into a contribution at
root
and contributions at the children
\texttt{node}\in\mathcal{T}_{\frac{1}{2}}
. At
root
, evaluating each of the
k_{1}
length-
T
child models on each of the
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(d/\varepsilon)
instances costs
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(dT/\varepsilon)
state computations. At each child
\texttt{node}\in\mathcal{T}_{\frac{1}{2}}
, the invocation of
\texttt{AutoLearn}_{\frac{1}{2}}
incurs a burn-in computational cost of
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(dTC_{\texttt{ans}}^{(B)}+dBC_{\texttt{seq}}^{(B)})=n_{\texttt{burn-in}}
by
Lemma

D.4
. Summing over the
k_{1}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(1)
children gives

n_{\texttt{comp}}\leqslant\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{dT}{\varepsilon}\right)+n_{\texttt{burn-in}}.

Note that, as in the analysis of the query complexity, this bound is stated for when the target error probability is
k_{1}\delta
. Replacing
\delta\leftarrow\delta/k_{1}
results in a bound which is larger by a factor of
\log(k_1)=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(1)
. Combining the accuracy, sample, query, and computational bounds completes the proof of
Theorem

4.2
.

Appendix E
Proofs of Lemmas

E.1
Upper Bounding Prediction Error via Potentials: Proof of
Lemma

B.2

In this section, we will produce an upper bound which decomposes the error of the model returned by the depth-
1
construction into the regression accumulated across iterations which abort. In order to establish this lemma, we will first introduce a potential function which will serve as a proxy to bounding the accuracy of the plurality model. For
0\leqslant j\leqslant k
, define the potential function,

\Phi_{j}=\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}\mathbb{E}\big[\beta^{j,k}_{\mathrm{rank}_{j}(X_{i})}\cdot\mathbb{I}((\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\in\mathcal{K}_{j-1})\mid\mathcal{H}_{k-1}\big]

Here,
\underline{X}\sim\sigma
and
\bm{\eta}=(\bm{\eta}_{j})_{j=0}^{k-1}
where
\bm{\eta}_{j}=(\eta_{j,i})_{i=0}^{L-1}
. The proof of
Lemma

B.2
follows by first showing how the potential function
\Phi_{k}
relates to the test loss of the final model returned by
\texttt{AutoLearn}.\texttt{warmup}
,
\widehat{\pi}
. Then, we show how
\Phi_{k}
can be analyzed by writing it down as
\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\Phi_{j+1}-\Phi_{j}
and bounding the successive differences.

Lemma E.1

(Potential function decomposition)
.

Let
\widehat{\pi}
denote the model returned by
\texttt{AutoLearn}.\texttt{warmup}
. Then,

\mathrm{Pr}_{{\bm{x}}\sim\rho}\big[\widehat{\pi}({\bm{x}})\neq\pi^{\star}_{T}({\bm{x}})\big]\leqslant\Phi_{k}+\mathrm{Pr}((\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\not\in\mathcal{K}_{k-1}\mid\mathcal{H}_{k-1}).

Proof.
The proof of this result is deferred to
Section

F.2
.
∎

Next we show how the potential difference
\Phi_{j+1}-\Phi_{j}
evolves, by relating it to the
\texttt{err}_{j}
error terms. The following lemma is adapted from
[
18
, Lemma 3.7]
, with a subtle modification to account for the nested
\mathcal{K}_{j}
events.

Lemma E.2

(Recursive bound on potentials)
.

Let
\texttt{err}_{j}
be as defined in the statement of
Lemma

B.6
. The potential function
\Phi_{j}
satisfies the following recurrence relation,

\displaystyle\Phi_{j+1}-\Phi_{j}\leqslant(\texttt{err}_{j}-\texttt{err}_{\star})\cdot\mathbb{E}\big[R_{j}\cdot\mathbb{I}((\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\in\mathcal{K}_{j})\mid\mathcal{H}_{k-1}\big]

Proof.
The proof of this result is deferred to
Section

F.3
.
∎

E.1.1
Proof of
Lemma

B.2

By cascading
Lemma

E.2
across
j
and combining with
Lemma

E.1
, we get,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}_{{\bm{x}}\sim\rho}\big[\widehat{\pi}({\bm{x}})\neq\pi^{\star}_{T}({\bm{x}})\big]

\displaystyle\leqslant\Phi_{k}+\mathrm{Pr}((\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\not\in\mathcal{K}_{k-1}\mid\mathcal{H}_{k-1})

\displaystyle\leqslant\Phi_{0}+\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}(\texttt{err}_{j}-\texttt{err}_{\star})\cdot\mathbb{E}\big[R_{j}\cdot\mathbb{I}((\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\in\mathcal{K}_{j})\mid\mathcal{H}_{k-1}\big]+\mathrm{Pr}((\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\not\in\mathcal{K}_{k-1}\mid\mathcal{H}_{k-1}).

Noting that
\Phi_{0}=\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}\mathbb{E}\big[\beta^{0,k}_{\mathrm{rank}_{0}(X_{i})}\mid\mathcal{H}_{k-1}\big]=L\cdot\beta^{0,k}_{0}
completes the proof.

E.2
Sampling Approximately from
\lambda_{j}
: Proof of
Lemma

B.3

In this section, we will prove
Lemma

B.3
by studying the underlying problem of sampling from a reweighted mixture of marginals (
Definition

5.1
) which captures its more general structure.

The problem of sampling from the reweighted mixture of marginals posits that the learner has sampling access to a distribution
\mu
over some product space
\bm{X}^{L}
, with the objective of approximately generating samples from the distribution
\nu(\cdot)\propto\frac{1}{L}\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}\mu(X_{i}=\cdot\,)w(\cdot)
for some weight function
w:\bm{X}\to[0,1]
. For appropriately chosen
w
,
\nu
captures the idealized distribution to train weak learners under when boosting is used to train a model with small error under
\overline{\mu}
. When connected back to
Lemma

B.3
,
\mu
corresponds to the distribution
\sigma
over tuples (of size
L
) of instances of length
\tau
, while
\nu(\cdot)
corresponds to a reweighted version of the distribution
\overline{\rho}(\cdot)=\frac{1}{L}\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}\rho(X_{i}=\cdot\,)
. We use
\underline{X}=(X_{0},\cdots,X_{L-1})
to denote samples from
\mu
. The first part of this section will focus on the case where there is a single weight function and the goal is to sample from its reweighted mixture of marginals,
\nu
.

What does it mean to sample from
\nu

approximately
?

It is worth stopping to ask what kind notion of approximation we are willing to tolerate to sample from
\nu
. In the context of boosting, the learner aims to train a model which achieves constant (say,
\frac{1}{4}
) error under
\nu
. To achieve such a guarantee, it suffices for the learner to generate a sample from some other distribution
\widehat{\nu}
which satisfies the following
approximate coverage
guarantee:

\mathrm{Pr}_{X\sim\nu}\left(\frac{\nu(X)}{\widehat{\nu}(X)}\geqslant C_{\texttt{cov}}\right)\leqslant\delta_{\text{cov}}

(54)

In particular, under the above assumption, as long as
\delta_{\text{cov}}<\frac{1}{4}
, a model which achieves error
\varepsilon=C_{\texttt{cov}}^{-1}(\frac{1}{4}-\delta_{\text{cov}})
under the distribution over inputs
\widehat{\nu}
also achieves error
\frac{1}{4}
under the distribution
\nu
. The requirement in
Eq.

54
is natural, and in fact (via an application of Markov’s inequality) implied by closeness of
\nu
and
\widehat{\nu}
in any
f
-divergence as long as
f
grows faster than a linear function. In particular, a bound on
D_{\mathrm{KL}}\big(\nu\,\big\|\,\widehat{\nu}\big)
implies
Eq.

54
with appropriately chosen
C
and
\delta_{C}
.

Sampling from a distribution with approximate coverage to
\nu
is only a sufficient condition for transferring error guarantees under
\widehat{\nu}
to under
\nu
. Since we aim to train models via boosting, it may suffice to use a weaker notion of approximation, and we discuss a candidate in the next paragraph. The main reason for doing so is not merely convenience: in
Lemma

E.6
we establish a
strong lower bound
to achieve the guarantee in
Eq.

54
unless
C_{\texttt{cov}}
scales as
\mathrm{poly}(L)
or the sampling algorithm uses
\mathrm{poly}(L)
samples from
\mu
.

E.2.1
A weakening of approximate coverage

Rather than requiring the learner to satisfy
Eq.

54
, we will consider the following weakened version. Let
\widetilde{\mu}
denote any distribution on
\bm{X}^{L}
such that
\mathrm{TV}\left(\mu,\widetilde{\mu}\right)
is at most
c
. Define
\widetilde{\nu}\propto\overline{\widetilde{\mu}}(\cdot)w(\cdot)
where
\overline{\widetilde{\mu}}
is the mixture of marginals of
\widetilde{\mu}
. Define the following weakened version of approximate coverage,

\text{There exists }\widetilde{\mu}\text{ such that }\mathrm{TV}\left(\mu,\widetilde{\mu}\right)\leqslant c\text{ and }\mathrm{Pr}_{X\sim\widetilde{\nu}}\left(\frac{\widetilde{\nu}(X)}{\widehat{\nu}(X)}\geqslant C_{\texttt{cov}}\right)\leqslant\delta_{\text{cov}}.

(55)

In particular, this relaxation only requires a sampling algorithm to achieve approximate coverage with respect to the reweighted mixture of marginals of
any distribution
\widetilde{\mu}
which is close to
\mu
. Note that the operation of taking the mixture of marginals and tilting is highly “non-Lipschitz”, so even though
\mu
and
\widetilde{\mu}
are close to one another,
\nu
and
\widetilde{\nu}
may be far apart.

In order to realize the above style of guarantee, we will construct
\widetilde{\mu}
by considering restrictions/conditionals of
\mu
unto some high probability set. In particular consider some events
\mathcal{K}\subseteq\bm{X}^{L}\times[0,1]^{L}
and define,

\mu_{\mathcal{K}}(\cdot)=\mathrm{Pr}(\underline{X}=\cdot\,|\,(\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\in\mathcal{K})\text{ and }\overline{\mu_{\mathcal{K}}}(\cdot)=\frac{1}{L}\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}\mathrm{Pr}(X_{i}=\cdot\,|\,(\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\in\mathcal{K})

where
\bm{\eta}\sim\operatorname{Unif}([0,1])^{\otimes L}
is a sequence of i.i.d. uniform random variables. If
\mathcal{K}=\bm{X}^{L}\times[0,1]^{L}
is total,
\mu_{\mathcal{K}}=\mu
. The first result we establish in this section shows that when
\mathcal{K}
is chosen carefully, the guarantee in
Eq.

55
can be achieved with
\widetilde{\nu}\leftarrow\nu_{\mathcal{K}}\propto\overline{\mu_{\mathcal{K}}}(\cdot)w(\cdot)
, with
\delta_{\text{cov}}=0
and
C_{\texttt{cov}}=\mathcal{O}(c^{-1}\log(L))
, for any
c>0
bounding the TV distance between
\mu
and
\mu_{\mathcal{K}}
.

Lemma E.3

(Approximate sampling from
\nu_{\mathcal{K}}
)
.

Fix any distribution
\mu
over
\bm{X}^{L}
and non-negative weight function
w:\bm{X}\to[0,1]
. Let
\widehat{\nu}
be the distribution over
\bm{X}
induced by the procedure:

Procedure E.1

Draw
\underline{X}\sim\mu
until one is accepted by the following filter: Sample random variables
\bm{\eta}=(\eta_{i})_{i=0}^{L-1}\sim\operatorname{Unif}([0,1])^{\otimes L}
, and define
\mathcal{I}=\big\{\eta_{i}\leqslant\frac{w(X_{i})}{\|w\|_{\infty}}:0\leqslant i\leqslant L-1\big\}
; accept
\underline{X}
and
|\mathcal{I}|\geqslant 1
and return
X_{I}
for
I\sim\operatorname{Unif}(\mathcal{I})
.

Fix any
c\in(0,1)
. There exists
\mathcal{K}\subseteq\bm{X}^{L}\times[0,1]^{L}
such that,
\mathrm{Pr}((\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\not\in\mathcal{K})\leqslant c
. Furthermore,

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{\nu_{\mathcal{K}}}{\widehat{\nu}}\right\|_{\infty}\leqslant C_{\texttt{cov}},\text{ where }C_{\texttt{cov}}=\mathcal{O}\left(c^{-1}\cdot\log(L)\right)

(56)

Finally, the probability that
\underline{X}\sim\mu
is accepted within
Lemma

E.3
to generate a sample from
\widehat{\nu}
is,

p_{\mu,w}=\mathbb{E}\left[1-\prod_{i=0}^{L-1}\left(1-\frac{w(X_{i})}{\|w\|_{\infty}}\right)\right]

(57)

Proof.
This result is a corollary of
Lemma

E.5
setting
\mathcal{U}=\bm{X}^{L}
.
∎

Remark E.4
.

Note that applying
\texttt{InvSampling}(D_{{\bm{x}}}\|\Pi_{j},k)
where
D_{\bm{x}}\leftarrow\texttt{Split}({\bm{x}}|L)
(
Algorithms

4
and
3
) for
{\bm{x}}=(s_{0},\bm{w}_{1:T})\sim\rho
is identical to applying
Lemma

E.3
to
\underline{X}
constructed from
{\bm{x}}
by splitting and labeling the intermediate states using
f^{\star}
: i.e.,
\underline{X}=(X_{i})_{i=0}^{L-1}
where
X_{i}=(s_{i\tau},\bm{w}_{i\tau+1:(i+1)\tau})
where
s_{i\tau}=f^{\star}_{i\tau}({\bm{x}})
.

Note that
Lemma

E.3
achieves the guarantee in
Lemma

E.3
using just a single draw from
\mu
if
p_{\mu,w}=1
. Even in this special case, any sampling algorithm which is forced to achieve approximate coverage
Eq.

54
with respect to
\mu
(i.e.,
Eq.

55
with
c=0
) achieves compete with the best guarantee achievable if
\mu
is allowed to be slightly perturbed to
\widetilde{\mu}
. We will discuss this further in
Section

E.2.2
.

Lemma

E.3
itself is a special case of
Lemma

E.5
below, which extends this result to the setting where the learner aims to approximately sample from the reweighted mixture of marginals of
\mu
.

Lemma E.5

(Approximate sampling from
\nu_{\mathcal{K}}
)
.

Fix any distribution
\mu
over
\bm{X}^{L}
and non-negative weight function
w:\bm{X}\to[0,1]
. Let
\widehat{\nu}
be the distribution over
\bm{X}
induced by
Lemma

E.3
. Consider any event
\mathcal{U}\subseteq\bm{X}^{L}
and fix
c\in(0,1)
. There exists
\mathcal{K}\subseteq\bm{X}^{L}\times[0,1]^{L}
such that,

(\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\in\mathcal{K}\implies\underline{X}\in\mathcal{U}\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathrm{Pr}((\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\not\in\mathcal{K}\mid\underline{X}\in\mathcal{U})\leqslant c.

Furthermore,

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{\nu_{\mathcal{K}}}{\widehat{\nu}}\right\|_{\infty}\leqslant C_{\texttt{cov}},\text{ where }C_{\texttt{cov}}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{c^{-1}\cdot\log(L)}{\mathrm{Pr}(\underline{X}\in\mathcal{U}\mid|\mathcal{I}|\geqslant 1)}\right)

(58)

Finally, the probability that
\underline{X}\sim\mu
is accepted within
Lemma

E.3
to generate a sample from
\widehat{\nu}
is
p_{\mu,w}
(cf.
Eq.

57
).

Proof.
This result is proved formally in
Section

F.4
, and a consequence of the two bounds we establish in
Lemma

F.3
and
Lemma

F.4
. The former is a pointwise upper bound on
\nu_{\mathcal{K}}
, and the latter, a pointwise lower bound on
\widehat{\nu}
. The bound on the acceptance probability of
\underline{X}\sim\mu
by
Lemma

E.3
is a direct calculation using the fact that for
\underline{X}
to be accepted,
|\mathcal{I}|\neq 0
, equivalent to the existence of an index
0\leqslant i\leqslant L-1
such that
\eta_{i}\leqslant\frac{w(X_{i})}{\|w\|_{\infty}}
.
∎

The extension of this result to the case where there are multiple weight functions, is relatively straightforward and discussed further in
Section

F.5
where we also prove
Lemma

B.3
. In particular
Lemma

B.3
considers the case we have a sequence of
k
(potentially random) weight functions
(w_{j})_{j=0}^{k-1}
and events
(\mathcal{U}_{j})_{j=0}^{k-1}
, and constructs a sequence of events
(\mathcal{K}_{j})_{j=0}^{k-1}
which enable density ratio bounds akin to
Eq.

58
.

E.2.2
A sampling lower bound for achieving approximate coverage

In order to further interpret the bound in
Lemma

E.3
, it will be helpful to mention a worst case bound which pertains to the guarantee achievable in the absence of the conditioning event
\mathcal{K}
. We show that sampling from a distribution satisfying approximate coverage (cf.
Eq.

54
) to the target distribution
\nu\propto\overline{\mu}(\cdot)w(\cdot)
, even with a modest
\delta_{\text{cov}}=\frac{1}{4}
, either requires
C_{\texttt{cov}}=\mathrm{poly}(L)
or requires the sampling algorithm to draw
\mathrm{poly}(L)
samples form
\mu
, even when considering the ideal scenario where
p_{\mu,w}=1
.

Lemma E.6

(Worst-case bound when
\mathcal{K}^{c}=\emptyset
)
.

Let
\bm{X}=\{0,1,\cdots,L+1\}
. There exists a class of distributions
\bm{\mu}=\{\mu_{p,z}:(p,z)\in[0,1]\times[L]\}
, parameterized by
p\in(0,1)
supported on
\bm{X}^{L}
, as well as a fixed weight function
w:\bm{X}\to[0,1]
such that:

1.

p_{\mu,w}=1
for all
\mu\in\bm{\mu}
.

2.

For a sufficiently small absolute constant
c>0
, consider any sampling algorithm
Alg
which draws at most
N=c\sqrt{L}
samples drawn from the distribution
\mu\in\bm{\mu}
to generate a sample from some distribution
\widehat{\nu}\in\Delta_{\{0,1,\cdots,L+1\}}
, and such that
\widehat{\nu}
is well defined for every base distribution
\mu\in\bm{\mu}
. Then the distribution
\widehat{\nu}
realized by
Alg
must incur,

\max_{\mu\in\bm{\mu}}\mathrm{TV}\left(\widehat{\nu},\nu\right)\geqslant\frac{1}{2}

where
\nu
is the distribution
\propto\overline{\mu}(\cdot)w(\cdot)
.

3.

Furthermore, if
Alg
draws at most
N=c^{\prime}L^{1/3}
samples from
\mu\in\bm{\mu}
for a sufficiently small constant
c^{\prime}>0
, then the distribution
\widehat{\nu}
realized by
Alg
must incur,

\max_{\mu\in\bm{\mu}}\mathrm{Pr}_{X\sim\nu}\left(\frac{\nu(X)}{\widehat{\nu}(X)}\geqslant c^{\prime}L^{1/3}\right)\geqslant\frac{1}{2}

Proof.
The proof of this result is discussed in
Section

F.6
.
∎

E.3
\mathcal{K}_{j}
is a High Probability Event: Proof of
Lemma

B.4

We first introduce an auxiliary lemma which will be helpful to prove
Lemma

B.4
. The following result establishes that the
\mathcal{U}_{j}
events occur simultaneously with moderately high probability.

Lemma E.7
.

Recall that Across all iterations
0\leqslant j\leqslant k-1
, we have
\mathrm{Pr}(\mathcal{U}_{j}\mid\mathcal{K}_{j-1},\mathcal{H}_{j-1})\geqslant 1-\frac{1}{32k^{2}}
.

Proof.
The proof of this result is in
Section

E.3.1
.
∎

Having established this result, we next show how to prove
Lemma

B.4
. As a consequence of the recursive definition of
\mathcal{K}_{j}
which is constructed from
\mathcal{U}_{j}
and
\mathcal{K}_{j-1}
, and by
Eq.

23
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}(\mathcal{K}_{j}\mid\mathcal{H}_{j-1})

\displaystyle\geqslant(1-c)\cdot\mathrm{Pr}(\mathcal{U}_{j}\cap\mathcal{K}_{j-1}\mid\mathcal{H}_{j-1})

\displaystyle=(1-c)\cdot\mathrm{Pr}(\mathcal{U}_{j}\mid\mathcal{K}_{j-1},\mathcal{H}_{j-1})\cdot\mathrm{Pr}(\mathcal{K}_{j-1}\mid\mathcal{H}_{j-1})

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{\geqslant}(1-c)\cdot\left(1-\frac{1}{32k^{2}}\right)\cdot\mathrm{Pr}\big(\mathcal{K}_{j-1}\mid\mathcal{H}_{j-2}\big).

where in
(a)
we use the fact that
\mathcal{K}_{j-1}
is a measurable function of
(\underline{X},\bm{\eta})
and
\mathcal{H}_{j-2}
, and
Lemma

E.7
. Plugging in the value of
c=\frac{1}{32k}
, and solving the recursion over
k
steps gives us: for all
0\leqslant j\leqslant k-1
,

\mathrm{Pr}(\mathcal{K}_{j}\mid\mathcal{H}_{j-1})\geqslant\frac{15}{16}.

E.3.1
\mathcal{U}_{\leqslant j}
is a High Probability Event: Proof of
Lemma

E.7

By
Lemma

B.7
,

\displaystyle\widetilde{p}_{j}\leqslant p_{j}^{\star}\implies

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}\big(R_{j}>\mathfrak{r}_{j}\mid\mathcal{K}_{j-1},\mathcal{H}_{j-1}\big)\leqslant\frac{1}{32k^{2}}

(59)

Simplifying further, this implies for all
j
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}(\mathcal{U}_{j}^{c}\mid\mathcal{K}_{j-1},\mathcal{H}_{j-1})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Pr}\big(\widetilde{p}_{j}\leqslant p_{j}^{\star}\text{ and }R_{j}>\mathfrak{r}_{j}\mid\mathcal{K}_{j-1},\mathcal{H}_{j-1}\big)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{I}(\widetilde{p}_{j}\leqslant p_{j}^{\star})\cdot\mathrm{Pr}\big(R_{j}>\mathfrak{r}_{j}\mid\mathcal{K}_{j-1},\mathcal{H}_{j-1}\big)\leqslant\frac{1}{32k^{2}}

(60)

This completes the proof of the lemma.

E.4
Truthful Aborts: Proof of
Lemma

B.5

By definition of
p_{j}
, and by the structure of the split-and-inverted-sampling pipeline (
Algorithms

4
and
3
), the size of the dataset
|D_{\mathrm{out}}^{j}|
which the model
\widehat{\pi}^{j}
is trained on can be expressed as the sum of
n^{\prime}=|D_{\mathrm{input}}^{j}|=|D_{\mathrm{input}}|/k
i.i.d. Bernoulli random variables, each with mean
p_{j}
. By an application of the multiplicative Chernoff bound,

\mathrm{Pr}\left(|D_{\mathrm{out}}^{j}|<\frac{n^{\prime}p_{j}}{2}\ \middle|\ \mathcal{H}_{j-1}\right)\leqslant\exp\left(-\frac{n^{\prime}p_{j}}{8}\right)

In a non-aborted iteration,
p_{j}\geqslant\frac{15}{16}\cdot\widetilde{p}_{j}>\frac{15}{16}\cdot p_{j}^{\star}
by
Eq.

28
.
By the sufficiently large choice of
|D_{\mathrm{input}}|=n^{\prime}k
, we have that,
(a)

n^{\prime}p_{j}\geqslant 8\log(4k/\delta)
, and
(b)

n^{\prime}p_{j}\gtrsim k^{7/2}\log(L)\cdot(d\log(|S|)+\log(\delta/k))
. By its definition in
Eq.

19
, this implies,

\mathrm{Pr}\left(|D_{\mathrm{out}}^{j}|<C_{2}k^{7/2}\log(L)\cdot(d\log(|S|)+\log(k/\delta))\ \middle|\ \mathcal{H}_{j-1}\right)\leqslant\frac{\delta}{2k}

Plugging in
n^{\prime}
and
p_{j}^{\star}
completes the proof.

E.5
Training Weak Models: Proof of
Lemma

B.6

By
Lemma

B.3
, in order to prove an upper bound on the prediction error under
\lambda_{j}
, it suffices to establish learning guarantees under
\widehat{\lambda}_{j}
and use a change-of-measure argument. Noting that
\texttt{AutoLearn}.\texttt{warmup}
instantiates the base learner as
\texttt{NTP}_{\texttt{Full-CoT}}
(
Algorithm

9
), we next provide a learner guarantee for this next-token prediction based rule. As a corollary of
[
29
, Theorem B.5]
, we have the following result.

Proposition E.8
.

Given a dataset
D=\{{\bm{x}}^{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
of length
\tau
instances drawn i.i.d. from
\widehat{\lambda}_{j}
,
\texttt{NTP}_{\texttt{Full-CoT}}(\cdot)
queries the
\texttt{iCoT}(\cdot)
oracle
\tau n
times in total to return a model
\widehat{\pi}^{\texttt{NTP}}
such that, with probability
1-\frac{\delta}{2k}
for any constant
C_{0}>0
,

\mathrm{Pr}_{X\sim\widehat{\lambda}_{j}}\big[\widehat{\pi}^{\texttt{NTP}}_{\tau}(X)\neq\pi^{\star}_{\tau}(X)\big]\leqslant\frac{1}{C_{0}k^{7/2}\log(L)},

as long as for a sufficiently large absolute constant
C_{1}>0
depending on
C_{0}
,

\displaystyle n\geqslant C_{1}k^{7/2}\log(L)\cdot(d\log(|S|)+\log(k/\delta)).

E.5.1
Proof of
Lemma

B.6

Consider any non-aborted iteration
j
. Note that the model
\widehat{\pi}^{j}
is learned via
\texttt{NTP}_{\texttt{Full-CoT}}
on the dataset
D_{\mathrm{out}}^{j}
. By the bound on the density ratio in
Eq.

27
, and a change-of-measure argument, with probability at least
1-\frac{\delta}{k}
,

\mathrm{Pr}_{X\sim\lambda_{j}}\big[\widehat{\pi}^{j}(X)\neq\pi^{\star}_{\tau}(X)\big]\leqslant\left\|\frac{\lambda_{j}}{\widehat{\lambda}_{j}}\right\|_{\infty}\cdot\mathrm{Pr}_{X\sim\widehat{\lambda}_{j}}\big[\widehat{\pi}^{j}_{\tau}(X)\neq\pi^{\star}_{\tau}(X)\big]\leqslant\frac{1}{4}.

The last inequality uses
Proposition

E.8
with sufficiently large
C_{0}
, and
Lemma

B.5
.

E.6
Aborted Iterations Have Low Regression: Proof of
Lemma

B.7

Prior to proving
Lemma

B.7
,
Next, we move onto establishing the moderate probability upper bound on
R_{j}
via Markov’s inequality. From its definition in
Eq.

18
,

\displaystyle\widetilde{p}_{j}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Bigg[1-\prod_{i=0}^{L-1}\Bigg(1-\frac{\alpha^{j,k}_{\mathrm{rank}_{j}(X_{i})}}{\alpha^{j,k}_{\max}}\Bigg)\ \Bigg|\ \mathcal{H}_{j-1},\mathcal{K}_{j-1}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\geqslant\mathbb{E}\left[1-\exp\left(-\frac{R_{j}}{\alpha^{j,k}_{\max}}\right)\ \middle|\ \mathcal{H}_{j-1},\mathcal{K}_{j-1}\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{\geqslant}(1-e^{-\theta})\cdot\mathrm{Pr}\left(R_{j}\geqslant\theta\alpha^{j,k}_{\max}\ \middle|\ \mathcal{H}_{j-1},\mathcal{K}_{j-1}\right)

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{\theta}{2}\cdot\mathrm{Pr}\left(R_{j}\geqslant\theta\alpha^{j,k}_{\max}\ \middle|\ \mathcal{H}_{j-1},\mathcal{K}_{j-1}\right)

where
(a)
is by an application of Markov’s inequality for any
\theta>0
. The proof concludes by choosing
\theta=\frac{1}{16\sqrt{k}}
and using the upper bound on
\widetilde{p}_{j}
assumed in the statement of the lemma.

E.6.1
Explicit formula for
p_{j}

Below we prove the explicit formula for
p_{j}
we described in
Eq.

17
. Let
\mathcal{I}_{j}
denote the (random) set of indices in
[L]
accepted by
InvSampling
, so that
\mathcal{I}_{j}\neq\emptyset\iff\texttt{InvSampling}(D_{\bm{x}}\ \|\ \Pi_{j},k)\neq\perp
. This implies that,

\displaystyle p_{j}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Pr}\big(\texttt{InvSampling}(D_{\bm{x}}\ \|\ \Pi_{j},k)\neq\perp\ \big|\ \mathcal{H}_{j-1}\big)

(61)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Pr}(|\mathcal{I}_{j}|\geqslant 1\mid\mathcal{H}_{j-1})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[1-\mathrm{Pr}_{(\eta_{i})_{i=0}^{L-1}\sim\operatorname{Unif}([0,1])^{\otimes L}}\left(\forall i\in\{0,\cdots,L-1\},\ \eta_{i}\geqslant\frac{w_{j}(X_{i})}{\|w_{j}\|_{\infty}}\right)\ \middle|\ \mathcal{H}_{j-1}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[1-\prod_{i=0}^{L-1}\left(1-\frac{w_{j}(X_{i})}{\|w_{j}\|_{\infty}}\right)\ \middle|\ \mathcal{H}_{j-1}\right].

(62)

Appendix F
Proofs of Auxiliary Results

F.1
Lower Bounds Against Full-CoT Learners: Proof of
Proposition

3.7

Consider any family of models
\mathcal{G}:S\to S
with Natarajan dimension
d
. Construct the semiautomaton class
\Pi
on the state space
S
and
\Sigma=\{-1,+1\}
. Each
\pi\in\Pi
corresponds to a
g\in\mathcal{G}
which we will indicate through the mapping
\pi_{g}
and the transition of
\pi_{g}
is defined as,

\displaystyle\forall s\in S,\

\displaystyle\pi_{g}((s,-1))=g(s)

\displaystyle\forall s\in S,\

\displaystyle\pi_{g}((s,+1))=s

In other words,
\pi
transitions according to
g(\cdot)
when the input letter is
-1
, and behaves like the identity mapping when the input letter is
+1
. Suppose the distribution over instances,
\rho
, is constructed by taking the direct product of any distribution over states
\sigma^{\star}_{0}
, and the (deterministic) distribution over symbols
\delta_{-1}\times(\delta_{+1})^{T-1}
. Under this structure, note that almost surely for
s_{0}\sim\sigma^{\star}_{0}
,
\pi_{g,T}(s_{0})=g(s_{0})
. Consider any PAC learning algorithm
\texttt{Alg}(\cdot)
which consumes a dataset of CoTs,
D=\{((s_{0},\bm{w})\mapsto\pi_{g^{\star},1:T}((s_{0},\bm{w})))\}
for some unknown
\pi_{g^{\star}}\in\Pi
and
(s_{0},\bm{w})\sim\rho
, to return a model
\widehat{\pi}
satisfying with probability at least
\frac{1}{2}
,

\mathrm{Pr}_{(s_{0},\bm{w})\sim\rho}\big(\widehat{\pi}((s_{0},\bm{w}))\neq\pi_{g^{\star},T}((s_{0},\bm{w}))\big)\leqslant\frac{1}{4}.

This can be rewritten as,

\mathrm{Pr}_{s_{0}\sim\sigma^{\star}_{0}}\big(\widehat{\pi}((s_{0},\{-1,+1\cdots,+1\}))\neq g^{\star}(s_{0})\big)\leqslant\frac{1}{4}.

This implies that the induced model
\widehat{\pi}\big((\cdot,\{-1,+1,\cdots,+1\})\big)
is a PAC learner for
\mathcal{G}
under any distribution over states
\sigma^{\star}_{0}
. Furthermore, note that any fully labeled state sequence
(s_{0},\bm{w})\mapsto\pi_{g^{\star},1:T}((s_{0},\bm{w}))
can be constructed from the singular labeling
s_{0}\mapsto g^{\star}(s_{0})
and vice versa.

Now suppose
\sigma_{0}^{\star}
is supported on a set of
\mathrm{Ndim}(\mathcal{G})
points which are Natarajan-shattered by
\mathcal{G}
. This implies that
Alg
must query at least
c\mathrm{Ndim}(\mathcal{G})
instances to be able to achieve error
\frac{1}{4}
with probability at least
\frac{1}{2}
for some absolute constant
c>0
. Finally, noting that
\mathrm{Ndim}(\mathcal{G})=\mathcal{O}(\mathrm{Ndim}(\Pi))
(this follows immediately from the definition of Natarajan dimension), and the fact that
n_{\texttt{query}}\geqslant T\cdot n_{\texttt{sample}}
for any full CoT learner, we get the desired result.

F.2
Bounding Prediction Error by Potential
\Phi_{k}
: Proof of
Lemma

E.1

Recall that the output of
\texttt{AutoLearn}.\texttt{warmup}
is the model
\widehat{\pi}^{\circ L}
where
\widehat{\pi}=\texttt{Plu}\big(\big\{\widehat{\pi}^{j}_{\tau}:j\in\{0,\cdots,k-1\}\big\}\big)
. By definition of
\beta^{k,k}_{r}
, we have that,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}_{{\bm{x}}\sim\rho}\big[\widehat{\pi}^{\circ L}({\bm{x}})\neq\pi^{\star}_{T}({\bm{x}})\mid\mathcal{H}_{k-1}\big]

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}\mathrm{Pr}\big(\widehat{\pi}^{\circ L}(\texttt{concat}(\underline{X}))\neq\pi^{\star}_{T}(\texttt{concat}(\underline{X}))\mid\mathcal{H}_{k-1}\big)

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathrm{Pr}\big(\exists i\in\{0,\cdots,L-1\}:\widehat{\pi}(X_{i})\neq\pi^{\star}_{\tau}(X_{i})\text{ and }(\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\in\mathcal{K}_{k-1}\mid\mathcal{H}_{k-1}\big)+\mathrm{Pr}((\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\not\in\mathcal{K}_{k-1}\mid\mathcal{H}_{k-1})

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}\mathrm{Pr}\big(\widehat{\pi}(X_{i})\neq\pi^{\star}_{\tau}(X_{i})\text{ and }(\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\in\mathcal{K}_{k-1}\mid\mathcal{H}_{k-1}\big)+\mathrm{Pr}((\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\not\in\mathcal{K}_{k-1}\mid\mathcal{H}_{k-1})

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{\leqslant}\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}\sum_{r=0}^{\lceil k/2\rceil}\mathrm{Pr}\big(\mathrm{rank}_{k}(X_{i})=r\text{ and }(\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\in\mathcal{K}_{k-1}\mid\mathcal{H}_{k-1}\big)+\mathrm{Pr}((\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\not\in\mathcal{K}_{k-1}\mid\mathcal{H}_{k-1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}\mathbb{E}\big[\beta^{k,k}_{\mathrm{rank}_{k}(X_{i})}\cdot\mathbb{I}((\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\in\mathcal{K}_{k-1})\mid\mathcal{H}_{k-1}\big]+\mathrm{Pr}((\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\not\in\mathcal{K}_{k-1}\mid\mathcal{H}_{k-1})

\displaystyle=\Phi_{k}+\mathrm{Pr}\big((\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\not\in\mathcal{K}_{k-1}\mid\mathcal{H}_{k-1}\big)

where in
(a)
,
\texttt{concat}(X)
is defined as taking
X=(X_{0},\cdots,X_{L-1})\in(S\times\Sigma^{\tau})^{L}
where
X_{i}=(s^{i},\bm{w}^{i})
and mapping it to
(s^{0},\bm{w}^{0},\cdots,\bm{w}^{L-1})
, which is in
S\times\Sigma^{T}
, and
(b)
uses the fact that if
\mathrm{rank}_{k}(X_{i})>\lceil k/2\rceil
, then the plurality vote satisfies
\widehat{\pi}(X_{i})=\pi^{\star}_{\tau}(X_{i})
.

F.3
Recursion for
\Phi_{j}
: Proof of
Lemma

E.2

Recall by definition,

\displaystyle\Phi_{j+1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}\sum_{r=0}^{j+1}\beta^{j+1,k}_{r}\cdot\mathrm{Pr}\big(\mathrm{rank}_{j+1}(X_{i})=r\text{ and }(\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\in\mathcal{K}_{j}\mid\mathcal{H}_{k-1}\big)

Note that
\mathrm{rank}_{j+1}(X_{i})=r
is only possible if
\mathrm{rank}_{j}(X_{i})=r
or
\mathrm{rank}_{j}(X_{i})=r-1
. Likewise, under the condition
\mathrm{rank}_{j}(X_{i})=r
, then
\mathrm{rank}_{j+1}(X_{i})\neq r\implies\mathrm{rank}_{j+1}(X_{i})=r+1
. With this, we decompose the above expression as,

\displaystyle\Phi_{j+1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}\sum_{r=0}^{j}\beta^{j+1,k}_{r}\cdot\mathrm{Pr}\big((\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\in\mathcal{K}_{j}\text{ and }\mathrm{rank}_{j}(X_{i})=r\mid\mathcal{H}_{k-1}\big)

\displaystyle\qquad-\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}\sum_{r=0}^{j}\beta^{j+1,k}_{r}\cdot\mathrm{Pr}\big((\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\in\mathcal{K}_{j}\text{ and }\mathrm{rank}_{j+1}(X_{i})=r+1\text{ and }\mathrm{rank}_{j}(X_{i})=r\mid\mathcal{H}_{k-1}\big)

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}\sum_{r=1}^{j+1}\beta^{j+1,k}_{r}\cdot\mathrm{Pr}\big((\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\in\mathcal{K}_{j}\text{ and }\mathrm{rank}_{j+1}(X_{i})=r\text{ and }\mathrm{rank}_{j}(X_{i})=r-1\mid\mathcal{H}_{k-1}\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}\sum_{r=0}^{j}\beta^{j+1,k}_{r}\cdot\mathrm{Pr}\big((\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\in\mathcal{K}_{j}\text{ and }\mathrm{rank}_{j}(X_{i})=r\mid\mathcal{H}_{k-1}\big)

\displaystyle\qquad-\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}\sum_{r=0}^{j}\big(\underbrace{\beta^{j+1,k}_{r}-\beta^{j+1,k}_{r+1}}_{\alpha^{j,k}_{r}}\big)\cdot\mathrm{Pr}\big((\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\in\mathcal{K}_{j}\text{ and }\underbrace{\mathrm{rank}_{j+1}(X_{i})=r+1\text{ and }\mathrm{rank}_{j}(X_{i})=r}_{\equiv\big\{\mathrm{rank}_{j}(X_{i})\,=\,r\text{ and }\widehat{\pi}^{j}_{\tau}(X_{i})\,=\,\pi^{\star}_{\tau}(X_{i})\big\}}\mid\mathcal{H}_{k-1}\big)

(63)

Let
\sigma_{\mathcal{K}_{j}}
denote the distribution
\mathrm{Pr}(\underline{X}=\cdot\,|\,(\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\in\mathcal{K}_{j},\mathcal{H}_{j-1})
. Then, the last expression in
Eq.

63
can be further decomposed as,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}\sum_{r=0}^{j}\alpha^{j,k}_{r}\cdot\mathrm{Pr}\big((\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\in\mathcal{K}_{j}\text{ and }\mathrm{rank}_{j}(X_{i})=r\text{ and }\widehat{\pi}^{j}_{\tau}(X_{i})=\pi^{\star}_{\tau}(X_{i})\mid\mathcal{H}_{k-1}\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}\mathbb{E}_{\underline{X}\sim\sigma_{\mathcal{K}_{j}}}\big[\alpha^{j,k}_{\mathrm{rank}_{j}(X_{i})}\cdot\mathbb{I}\big(\widehat{\pi}^{j}_{\tau}(X_{i})=\pi^{\star}_{\tau}(X_{i})\big)\mid\mathcal{H}_{k-1}\big]\cdot\mathrm{Pr}\big((\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\in\mathcal{K}_{j}\mid\mathcal{H}_{k-1}\big)

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}L\cdot\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\overline{\sigma_{\mathcal{K}_{j}}}}\big[w_{j}(X)\cdot\mathbb{I}\big(\widehat{\pi}^{j}_{\tau}(X)=\pi^{\star}_{\tau}(X)\big)\mid\mathcal{H}_{j-1}\big]\cdot\mathrm{Pr}\big((\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\in\mathcal{K}_{j}\mid\mathcal{H}_{k-1}\big)

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{=}L\cdot\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\overline{\sigma_{\mathcal{K}_{j}}}}\big[w_{j}(X)\big]\cdot\mathrm{Pr}_{X\sim\lambda_{j}}\big(\widehat{\pi}^{j}_{\tau}(X)=\pi^{\star}_{\tau}(X)\big)\cdot\mathrm{Pr}\big((\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\in\mathcal{K}_{j}\mid\mathcal{H}_{k-1}\big)

\displaystyle\overset{(c)}{=}L\cdot\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\overline{\sigma_{\mathcal{K}_{j}}}}\big[\alpha^{j,k}_{\mathrm{rank}_{j}(X)}\big]\cdot(1-\texttt{err}_{j})\cdot\mathrm{Pr}\big((\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\in\mathcal{K}_{j}\mid\mathcal{H}_{k-1}\big)

\displaystyle=(1-\texttt{err}_{j})\cdot\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}\sum_{r=0}^{j}\mathbb{E}\big[\alpha^{j,k}_{r}\cdot\mathbb{I}((\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\in\mathcal{K}_{j}\text{ and }\mathrm{rank}_{j}(X_{i})=r)\mid\mathcal{H}_{k-1}\big]

where in
(a)
, recall that
w_{j}(X)=\alpha^{j,k}_{\mathrm{rank}_{j}(X)}
which is measurable with respect to
\mathcal{H}_{j-1}
. In
(b)
,
\lambda_{j}(\cdot)
is the distribution proportional to
\overline{\sigma_{\mathcal{K}_{j}}}(\cdot)w_{j}(\cdot)
. In equation
(c)
, we use the definition of
\texttt{err}_{j}
from
Lemma

B.6
. We may combine this equation back with
Eq.

63
, noting that
\alpha^{j,k}_{r}=\beta^{j+1,k}_{r}-\beta^{j+1,k}_{r+1}
,
\texttt{err}_{\star}=\frac{1}{4}
and
\texttt{err}_{\star}\cdot\beta^{j+1,k}_{r}+\left(1-\texttt{err}_{\star}\right)\cdot\beta^{j+1,k}_{r+1}=\beta^{j,k}_{r}
,

\displaystyle\Phi_{j+1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}\sum_{r=0}^{j}\Big(\texttt{err}_{\star}\cdot\beta^{j+1,k}_{r}+\left(1-\texttt{err}_{\star}\right)\cdot\beta^{j+1,k}_{r+1}\Big)\cdot\mathrm{Pr}\big((\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\in\mathcal{K}_{j}\text{ and }\mathrm{rank}_{j}(X_{i})=r\mid\mathcal{H}_{k-1}\big)

\displaystyle\qquad+(\texttt{err}_{j}-\texttt{err}_{\star})\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}\sum_{r=0}^{j}\alpha^{j,k}_{r}\cdot\mathrm{Pr}\big((\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\in\mathcal{K}_{j}\text{ and }\mathrm{rank}_{j}(X_{i})=r\mid\mathcal{H}_{k-1}\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}\sum_{r=0}^{j}\beta^{j,k}_{r}\cdot\mathrm{Pr}\big((\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\in\mathcal{K}_{j}\text{ and }\mathrm{rank}_{j}(X_{i})=r\mid\mathcal{H}_{k-1}\big)+(\texttt{err}_{j}-\texttt{err}_{\star})\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}\mathbb{E}\big[\alpha^{j,k}_{\mathrm{rank}_{j}(X_{i})}\cdot\mathbb{I}((\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\in\mathcal{K}_{j})\mid\mathcal{H}_{k-1}\big]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}\sum_{r=0}^{j}\beta^{j,k}_{r}\cdot\mathrm{Pr}\big((\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\in\mathcal{K}_{j}\text{ and }\mathrm{rank}_{j}(X_{i})=r\mid\mathcal{H}_{k-1}\big)+(\texttt{err}_{j}-\texttt{err}_{\star})\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}\mathbb{E}\big[\alpha^{j,k}_{\mathrm{rank}_{j}(X_{i})}\cdot\mathbb{I}((\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\in\mathcal{K}_{j})\mid\mathcal{H}_{k-1}\big]

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{\leqslant}\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}\sum_{r=0}^{j}\beta^{j,k}_{r}\cdot\mathrm{Pr}\big((\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\in\mathcal{K}_{j-1}\text{ and }\mathrm{rank}_{j}(X_{i})=r\mid\mathcal{H}_{k-1}\big)+(\texttt{err}_{j}-\texttt{err}_{\star})\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}\mathbb{E}\big[\alpha^{j,k}_{\mathrm{rank}_{j}(X_{i})}\cdot\mathbb{I}((\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\in\mathcal{K}_{j})\mid\mathcal{H}_{k-1}\big]

\displaystyle=\Phi_{j}+(\texttt{err}_{j}-\texttt{err}_{\star})\cdot\mathbb{E}\big[R_{j}\cdot\mathbb{I}((\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\in\mathcal{K}_{j})\mid\mathcal{H}_{k-1}\big].

where
(a)
uses the fact that
\mathcal{K}_{j}\subseteq\mathcal{K}_{j-1}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq\mathcal{K}_{0}
.

F.4
Density Ratio Bound between
\nu_{\mathcal{K}}
and
\widehat{\nu}
: Proof of
Lemma

E.5

Prior to proving the main bounds on the densities of
\widehat{\nu}
and
\nu_{\mathcal{K}}
, we prove a lemma which will help understand the structure of the sampling from the mixture of marginal problem (
Definition

5.1
) for a single weight function
w
. Recall the notation:
\underline{X}=(X_{i})_{i=0}^{L-1}\sim\mu
is a distribution over
\bm{X}^{L}
, and
w
is a
[0,1]
-bounded weight function. We also let
m\triangleq\lceil\log_{2}(L+1)\rceil
.

Lemma F.1

(Sampling from
\nu
)
.

One sample from
\nu\propto\overline{\mu}(\cdot)w(\cdot)
can be generated by the following procedure.

Procedure F.1

Keep drawing samples
\underline{X}\sim\mu
until one is accepted by the following filter: Draw
L
random variables
(\eta_{i})_{i=0}^{L-1}\overset{\text{i.i.d.}}{\sim}\operatorname{Unif}([0,1])
and define
\mathcal{I}=\left\{\eta_{i}\leqslant\frac{w(X_{i})}{\|w\|_{\infty}}:i\in\{0,\ldots,L-1\}\right\}
;
\underline{X}
is accepted with probability,

p_{\underline{X}}=\frac{\left|\mathcal{I}\right|}{L}.

For the accepted
\underline{X}
, return
X_{I}\in\underline{X}
for
I\sim\operatorname{Unif}(\mathcal{I})
.

Proof.
The probability that an
\underline{X}\sim\mu
in
Lemma

F.1
is accepted and
I=i
is returned,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}(\text{accept }\underline{X}\text{ and }I=i\mid\underline{X})=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{|\mathcal{I}|}{L}\cdot\frac{\mathbb{I}(i\in\mathcal{I})}{|\mathcal{I}|}\right]=\frac{1}{L}\,\mathrm{Pr}\left(\eta_{i}\leqslant\frac{w(X_{i})}{\|w\|_{\infty}}\right)=\frac{1}{L}\cdot\frac{w(X_{i})}{\|w\|_{\infty}}.

Therefore, for any measurable set
S\subseteq\bm{X}
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}(\text{output}\in S\text{ and accept }\underline{X}\mid\underline{X})=\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}\frac{1}{L}\cdot\frac{w(X_{i})}{\|w\|_{\infty}}\,\mathbb{I}(X_{i}\in S).

Taking expectation over
\underline{X}\sim\mu
and using the definition of
\overline{\mu}
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}(\text{output}\in S\text{ and accept }\underline{X})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}\frac{1}{L}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{w(X_{i})}{\|w\|_{\infty}}\,\mathbb{I}(X_{i}\in S)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{w(X)}{\|w\|_{\infty}}\,\mathbb{I}(X\in S)\right]

With
S=\bm{X}
, we see that the total acceptance probability is
\mathrm{Pr}(\text{accept})=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{w(X)}{\|w\|_{\infty}}\right]
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}(\text{output}\in S\mid\text{accept }\underline{X})=\frac{\mathrm{Pr}(\text{output}\in S\text{ and accept }\underline{X})}{\mathrm{Pr}(\text{accept }\underline{X})}=\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[w(X)\,\mathbb{I}(X\in S)\right]}{\mathbb{E}\left[w(X)\right]}=\nu(S).

This completes the proof.
∎

In proving
Lemma

E.5
, we will first introduce some relevant notation and auxiliary results. For
\ell\in[m]
, define
\nu_{\ell}
as the distribution over
\bm{X}
induced by the following procedure.

Procedure F.2

Keep drawing samples
\underline{X}\sim\mu
until one is accepted by the following filter: Draw
L
random variables
(\eta_{i})_{i=0}^{L-1}\overset{\text{i.i.d.}}{\sim}\operatorname{Unif}([0,1])
and define
\mathcal{I}=\left\{\eta_{i}\leqslant\frac{w(X_{i})}{\|w\|_{\infty}}:i\in\{0,\cdots,L-1\}\right\}
; accept
\underline{X}
with probability,

p_{\underline{X}}=\frac{|\mathcal{I}|}{L}\cdot\mathbb{I}(|\mathcal{I}|\in[2^{\ell-1},2^{\ell})\text{ and }\underline{X}\in\mathcal{U}).

For the accepted
\underline{X}
, return
X_{I}
for
I\sim\operatorname{Unif}(\mathcal{I})
if
\ell\geqslant 1
and
I\sim\operatorname{Unif}(\{0,\cdots,L-1\})
otherwise.

Following the proof of
Lemma

F.1
, we arrive at the following lemma.

Lemma F.2

(Explicit formula for
\nu_{\ell}
)
.

The distribution
\nu_{\ell}
can be written as: for any
S\subseteq\bm{X}
,

\displaystyle\nu_{\ell}(S)=\frac{\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{Pr}\left(i\in\mathcal{I}\text{ and }|\mathcal{I}|\in[2^{\ell-1},2^{\ell})\text{ and }\underline{X}\in\mathcal{U}\ \middle|\ \underline{X}\right)\mathbb{I}(X_{i}\in S)\big]}{\mathbb{E}\left[|\mathcal{I}|\cdot\mathbb{I}(|\mathcal{I}|\in[2^{\ell-1},2^{\ell})\text{ and }\underline{X}\in\mathcal{U})\right]}.

Proof.
Following the proof of
Lemma

F.1
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}(\text{accept }\underline{X}\text{ and }I=i\mid\underline{X})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{|\mathcal{I}|}{L}\cdot\frac{\mathbb{I}(i\in\mathcal{I})}{|\mathcal{I}|}\cdot\mathbb{I}\big(|\mathcal{I}|\in[2^{\ell-1},2^{\ell})\text{ and }\underline{X}\in\mathcal{U}\big)\ \middle|\ \underline{X}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{L}\mathrm{Pr}\left(i\in\mathcal{I}\text{ and }|\mathcal{I}|\in[2^{\ell-1},2^{\ell})\text{ and }\underline{X}\in\mathcal{U}\ \middle|\ \underline{X}\right)

Therefore, for any measurable set
S\subseteq\bm{X}
, taking expectation over
\underline{X}\sim\mu
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}(\text{output}\in S\text{ and accept }\underline{X})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{Pr}(\text{accept }\underline{X}\text{ and }I=i\mid\underline{X})\cdot\mathbb{I}(X_{i}\in S)\big]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{L}\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{Pr}\left(i\in\mathcal{I}\text{ and }|\mathcal{I}|\in[2^{\ell-1},2^{\ell})\text{ and }\underline{X}\in\mathcal{U}\ \middle|\ \underline{X}\right)\cdot\mathbb{I}(X_{i}\in S)\big]

(64)

With
S=\bm{X}
, we get,
\mathrm{Pr}(\text{accept }\underline{X})=\frac{1}{L}\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}\mathrm{Pr}\left(i\in\mathcal{I}\text{ and }|\mathcal{I}|\in[2^{\ell-1},2^{\ell})\right)
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}(\text{output}\in S\mid\text{accept }\underline{X})

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{Pr}(\text{output}\in S\text{ and accept }\underline{X})}{\mathrm{Pr}(\text{accept }\underline{X})}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{Pr}\left(i\in\mathcal{I}\text{ and }|\mathcal{I}|\in[2^{\ell-1},2^{\ell})\text{ and }\underline{X}\in\mathcal{U}\ \middle|\ \underline{X}\right)\mathbb{I}(X_{i}\in S)\big]}{\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}\mathrm{Pr}\left(i\in\mathcal{I}\text{ and }|\mathcal{I}|\in[2^{\ell-1},2^{\ell})\text{ and }\underline{X}\in\mathcal{U}\right)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{Pr}\left(i\in\mathcal{I}\text{ and }|\mathcal{I}|\in[2^{\ell-1},2^{\ell})\text{ and }\underline{X}\in\mathcal{U}\ \middle|\ \underline{X}\right)\mathbb{I}(X_{i}\in S)\big]}{\mathbb{E}\left[|\mathcal{I}|\cdot\mathbb{I}(|\mathcal{I}|\in[2^{\ell-1},2^{\ell})\text{ and }\underline{X}\in\mathcal{U})\right]}

This completes the proof.
∎

Next, we will introduce some notation to help define the event
\mathcal{K}
in the statement of
Lemma

E.5
. Firstly, define,

L_{\text{heavy}}=\left\{\ell\in[m]:\mathrm{Pr}\big(|\mathcal{I}|\in[2^{\ell-1},2^{\ell})\ \big|\ |\mathcal{I}|\geqslant 1,\ \underline{X}\in\mathcal{U}\big)\geqslant\frac{c}{m}\right\}

(65)

Note that
L_{\text{heavy}}
is non-empty by the pigeonhole principle, and the fact that
c<1
.
L_{\text{heavy}}
captures the set of dyadic intervals which witness a non-trivial mass of the random variable
|\mathcal{I}|
, conditioned on
\underline{X}\in\mathcal{U}
. Indeed, defining the event,

\mathcal{K}=\big\{|\mathcal{I}|\in\cup_{\ell\in L_{\text{heavy}}}[2^{\ell-1},2^{\ell})\text{ or }|\mathcal{I}|=0\big\}\cap\big\{\underline{X}\in\mathcal{U}\big\}

(66)

Note that
\{(\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\in\mathcal{K}\}\implies\{\underline{X}\in\mathcal{U}\}
, and by a union bound, we have the inequality,

\mathrm{Pr}\big((\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\not\in\mathcal{K}\mid\underline{X}\in\mathcal{U}\big)\leqslant c

With this definition of
\mathcal{K}
, we will prove a pointwise upper bound on the density of the induced
\nu_{\mathcal{K}}
.

Lemma F.3

(Sampling from
\nu_{\mathcal{K}}
)
.

The distribution
\nu_{\mathcal{K}}
satisfies: for any
S\subseteq\bm{X}
,

\displaystyle\nu_{\mathcal{K}}(S)\leqslant\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{c^{-1}\log(L)}{\mathrm{Pr}(\underline{X}\in\mathcal{U}\mid|\mathcal{I}|\geqslant 1)}\right)\cdot\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}\mathrm{Pr}\left(|\mathcal{I}|\in[2^{\ell-1},2^{\ell})\text{ and }\underline{X}\in\mathcal{U}\ \middle|\ |\mathcal{I}|\geqslant 1\right)\cdot\nu_{\ell}(S).

Proof.
Following the proof of
Lemma

F.1
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}(\text{accept }\underline{X}\text{ and }I=i\mid\underline{X})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{|\mathcal{I}|}{L}\cdot\frac{\mathbb{I}(i\in\mathcal{I})}{|\mathcal{I}|}\cdot\mathbb{I}\big(|\mathcal{I}|=0\text{ or }\exists\ell\in L_{\text{heavy}}:|\mathcal{I}|\in[2^{\ell-1},2^{\ell})\big)\cdot\mathbb{I}\big(\underline{X}\in\mathcal{U}\big)\ \middle|\ \underline{X}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell\in L_{\text{heavy}}}\frac{1}{L}\mathrm{Pr}\left(i\in\mathcal{I}\text{ and }|\mathcal{I}|\in[2^{\ell-1},2^{\ell})\text{ and }\underline{X}\in\mathcal{U}\big)\ \middle|\ \underline{X}\right)

Note that this is identical to the bound in
Eq.

64
, with an additional summation over
\ell\in L_{\text{heavy}}
. Following through the same steps as in the proof of
Lemma

F.2
, we arrive at the equation,

\displaystyle\nu_{\mathcal{K}}(S)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Pr}(\text{output}\in S\mid\text{accept }\underline{X})

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{\ell\in L_{\text{heavy}}}\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{Pr}\left(i\in\mathcal{I}\text{ and }|\mathcal{I}|\in[2^{\ell-1},2^{\ell})\text{ and }\underline{X}\in\mathcal{U}\ \middle|\ \underline{X}\right)\mathbb{I}(X_{i}\in S)\big]}{\sum_{\ell\in L_{\text{heavy}}}\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}\mathrm{Pr}\left(i\in\mathcal{I}\text{ and }|\mathcal{I}|\in[2^{\ell-1},2^{\ell})\text{ and }\underline{X}\in\mathcal{U}\right)}

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}\frac{\sum_{\ell\in L_{\text{heavy}}}\mathbb{E}\left[|\mathcal{I}|\cdot\mathbb{I}(|\mathcal{I}|\in[2^{\ell-1},2^{\ell})\text{ and }\underline{X}\in\mathcal{U})\right]\cdot\nu_{\ell}(S)}{\sum_{\ell\in L_{\text{heavy}}}\mathbb{E}\left[|\mathcal{I}|\cdot\mathbb{I}(|\mathcal{I}|\in[2^{\ell-1},2^{\ell})\text{ and }\underline{X}\in\mathcal{U})\right]}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{\sum_{\ell\in L_{\text{heavy}}}2^{\ell}\mathrm{Pr}\left(|\mathcal{I}|\in[2^{\ell-1},2^{\ell})\text{ and }\underline{X}\in\mathcal{U}\ \middle|\ |\mathcal{I}|\geqslant 1\right)\cdot\nu_{\ell}(S)}{\sum_{\ell\in L_{\text{heavy}}}2^{\ell-1}\mathrm{Pr}\left(|\mathcal{I}|\in[2^{\ell-1},2^{\ell})\text{ and }\underline{X}\in\mathcal{U}\ \middle|\ |\mathcal{I}|\geqslant 1\right)}.

where
(a)
relies on
Lemma

F.2
. Defining
\ell_{\max}=\max\{\ell\in L_{\text{heavy}}\}
, this can be bounded as,

\displaystyle\nu_{\mathcal{K}}(S)

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{\sum_{\ell\in L_{\text{heavy}}}2^{\ell_{\max}}\mathrm{Pr}\left(|\mathcal{I}|\in[2^{\ell-1},2^{\ell})\text{ and }\underline{X}\in\mathcal{U}\ \middle|\ |\mathcal{I}|\geqslant 1\right)\cdot\nu_{\ell}(S)}{2^{\ell_{\max}-1}\mathrm{Pr}\left(|\mathcal{I}|\in[2^{\ell_{\max}-1},2^{\ell_{\max}})\text{ and }\underline{X}\in\mathcal{U}\ \middle|\ |\mathcal{I}|\geqslant 1\right)}

\displaystyle=2\frac{\sum_{\ell\in L_{\text{heavy}}}\mathrm{Pr}\left(|\mathcal{I}|\in[2^{\ell-1},2^{\ell})\mid\underline{X}\in\mathcal{U}\text{ and }|\mathcal{I}|\geqslant 1\right)\cdot\nu_{\ell}(S)}{\mathrm{Pr}\left(|\mathcal{I}|\in[2^{\ell_{\max}-1},2^{\ell_{\max}})\mid\underline{X}\in\mathcal{U}\text{ and }|\mathcal{I}|\geqslant 1\right)}

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{\leqslant}\frac{2m}{c}\sum_{\ell\in L_{\text{heavy}}}\mathrm{Pr}\left(|\mathcal{I}|\in[2^{\ell-1},2^{\ell})\ \middle|\ \underline{X}\in\mathcal{U}\text{ and }|\mathcal{I}|\geqslant 1\right)\cdot\nu_{\ell}(S)

\displaystyle=\frac{2c^{-1}m}{\mathrm{Pr}(\underline{X}\in\mathcal{U}\mid|\mathcal{I}|\geqslant 1)}\sum_{\ell\in L_{\text{heavy}}}\mathrm{Pr}\left(|\mathcal{I}|\in[2^{\ell-1},2^{\ell})\text{ and }\underline{X}\in\mathcal{U}\mid|\mathcal{I}|\geqslant 1\right)\cdot\nu_{\ell}(S)

where
(a)
uses
\ell_{\max}\in L_{\text{heavy}}
and the definition of this set. Since
L_{\text{heavy}}\subseteq[m]
, the proof concludes.
∎

Finally, we will prove a pointwise lower bound on the density
\widehat{\nu}
.

Lemma F.4

(Pointwise lower bound on
\widehat{\nu}
)
.

The distribution
\widehat{\nu}
satisfies: for any
S\subseteq\bm{X}
,

\displaystyle\widehat{\nu}(S)\geqslant\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}\mathrm{Pr}\left(|\mathcal{I}|\in[2^{\ell-1},2^{\ell})\text{ and }\underline{X}\in\mathcal{U}\ \middle|\ |\mathcal{I}|\geqslant 1\right)\cdot\nu_{\ell}(S)

Proof.
Observe that for the distribution
\widehat{\nu}
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}(\text{accept }\underline{X}\text{ and }I=i\mid\underline{X})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\mathbb{I}(i\in\mathcal{I})}{|\mathcal{I}|}\cdot\mathbb{I}\big(|\mathcal{I}|\geqslant 1\big)\ \middle|\ \underline{X}\right]

Following through the steps in the proof of
Lemma

F.2
, we arrive at the equation,

\displaystyle\widehat{\nu}(S)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Pr}(\text{output}\in S\mid\text{accept }\underline{X})

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\mathbb{I}(i\in\mathcal{I}\text{ and }|\mathcal{I}|\geqslant 1)}{|\mathcal{I}|}\ \middle|\ \underline{X}\right]\mathbb{I}(X_{i}\in S)\right]}{\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\mathbb{I}(i\in\mathcal{I}\text{ and }|\mathcal{I}|\geqslant 1)}{|\mathcal{I}|}\right]}

(67)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\mathbb{I}(i\in\mathcal{I})}{|\mathcal{I}|}\cdot\mathbb{I}(|\mathcal{I}|\in[2^{\ell-1},2^{\ell}))\ \middle|\ \underline{X}\right]\mathbb{I}(X_{i}\in S)\right]}{\mathrm{Pr}(|\mathcal{I}|\geqslant 1)}

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}2^{-\ell}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[\mathrm{Pr}\left(i\in\mathcal{I}\text{ and }|\mathcal{I}|\in[2^{\ell-1},2^{\ell})\ \middle|\ \underline{X}\right)\mathbb{I}(X_{i}\in S)\right]}{\mathrm{Pr}(|\mathcal{I}|\geqslant 1)}
