\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}2^{-\ell}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[\mathrm{Pr}\left(i\in\mathcal{I}\text{ and }|\mathcal{I}|\in[2^{\ell-1},2^{\ell})\text{ and }\underline{X}\in\mathcal{U}\ \middle|\ \underline{X}\right)\mathbb{I}(X_{i}\in S)\right]}{\mathrm{Pr}(|\mathcal{I}|\geqslant 1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}2^{-\ell}\mathbb{E}\left[|\mathcal{I}|\cdot\mathbb{I}(|\mathcal{I}|\in[2^{\ell-1},2^{\ell})\text{ and }\underline{X}\in\mathcal{U})\right]\cdot\nu_{\ell}(S)}{\mathrm{Pr}(|\mathcal{I}|\geqslant 1)}

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{1}{2}\frac{\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}\mathrm{Pr}\left(|\mathcal{I}|\in[2^{\ell-1},2^{\ell})\text{ and }\underline{X}\in\mathcal{U}\right)\cdot\nu_{\ell}(S)}{\mathrm{Pr}(|\mathcal{I}|\geqslant 1)}

∎

F.5
Density Ratio Bound between
\nu_{\mathcal{K}_{j}}
and
\widehat{\nu}_{j}
: Proof of
Lemma

B.3

Prior to discussing the proof of
Lemma

B.3
, recall that the sequence of random events
(\mathcal{U}_{j})_{j=0}^{k-1}
are such that each
\mathcal{U}_{j}\subseteq\bm{X}^{L}
and is measurable with respect to
\mathcal{H}_{j-1}
. We view
\bm{\eta}=(\bm{\eta}_{j})_{j=0}^{k-1}\sim\operatorname{Unif}([0,1])^{\otimes kL}
as a tuple of tuples of i.i.d. random variables; the
L
coins in
\bm{\eta}_{j}
are used to determine the accepted indices
\mathcal{I}_{j}
in
Lemma

E.3
for
\widehat{\nu}_{j}
. We will define the heavy sets and the events
\mathcal{K}_{j}
in an iterative fashion. In particular,

L_{\text{heavy}}[j]=\left\{\ell\in[m]:\mathrm{Pr}\big(|\mathcal{I}_{j}|\in[2^{\ell-1},2^{\ell})\ \big|\ |\mathcal{I}_{j}|\geqslant 1,\ (\underline{X},\bm{\eta}_{0:j-1})\in\mathcal{U}_{j}\cap\mathcal{K}_{j-1},\mathcal{H}_{j-1}\big)\geqslant\frac{c}{m}\right\}

(68)

where
\mathcal{K}_{-1}^{c}=\emptyset
. And using this, we define the event: for
j\geqslant 0
,

\mathcal{K}_{j}=\big\{|\mathcal{I}_{j}|\in\cup_{\ell\in L_{\text{heavy}}[j]}[2^{\ell-1},2^{\ell})\text{ or }|\mathcal{I}_{j}|=0\big\}\cap\big\{(\underline{X},\bm{\eta}_{0:j-1})\in\mathcal{U}_{j}\cap\mathcal{K}_{j-1}\big\}

(69)

This statement precisely realizes the function
\mathcal{K}_{j}\leftarrow\texttt{ConstructEvent}(\mathcal{U}_{j}\cap\mathcal{K}_{j-1}\mid c,\sigma,\mathcal{H}_{j-1})
in the statement of
Lemma

B.3
. Finally, similar to how the event
\mathcal{K}
was defined with respect to
\mathcal{U}
earlier in
Eq.

66
, here the event
\mathcal{K}_{j}
is defined with respect to the event
\mathcal{U}_{j}\cap\mathcal{K}_{j-1}
. Finally, note that the following formula can be derived for the distribution
\widehat{\nu}_{j}
from
Eq.

67
,

\widehat{\nu}_{j}(S)=\frac{\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\mathbb{I}(i\in\mathcal{I}_{j}\text{ and }|\mathcal{I}_{j}|\geqslant 1)}{|\mathcal{I}_{j}|}\cdot\mathbb{I}(X_{i}\in S)\,\middle|\,\mathcal{H}_{j-1}\right]}{\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\mathbb{I}(i\in\mathcal{I}_{j}\text{ and }|\mathcal{I}_{j}|\geqslant 1)}{|\mathcal{I}_{j}|}\,\middle|\,\mathcal{H}_{j-1}\right]}

This definition can be used to derive a lower bound on
\widehat{\nu}_{j}
in a similar manner as we did for
\widehat{\nu}
earlier in
Lemma

F.4
. We invoke this lemma with the event
\mathcal{U}\leftarrow\mathcal{U}_{j}\cap\mathcal{K}_{j-1}
in an iterative fashion.

Lemma F.5
.

Consider any
S\subseteq\bm{X}
. Then,

\widehat{\nu}_{j}(S)\geqslant\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}\mathrm{Pr}\big(|\mathcal{I}_{j}|\in[2^{\ell-1},2^{\ell})\text{ and }(\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\in\mathcal{U}_{j}\cap\mathcal{K}_{j-1}\mid|\mathcal{I}_{j}|\geqslant 1,\mathcal{H}_{j-1}\big)\cdot\nu_{j,\ell}(S)

Where,

\nu_{j,\ell}(S)=\frac{\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}\mathrm{Pr}\left(i\in\mathcal{I}_{j}\text{ and }|\mathcal{I}_{j}|\in[2^{\ell-1},2^{\ell})\text{ and }(\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\in\mathcal{U}_{j}\cap\mathcal{K}_{j-1}\text{ and }X_{i}\in S)\mid\mathcal{H}_{j-1}\right)}{\mathbb{E}\left[|\mathcal{I}_{j}|\cdot\mathbb{I}(|\mathcal{I}_{j}|\in[2^{\ell-1},2^{\ell})\text{ and }(\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\in\mathcal{U}_{j}\cap\mathcal{K}_{j-1})\mid\mathcal{H}_{j-1}\right]}

Proof.
The lemma is a direct consequence of the approach we use to prove
Lemma

F.4
, only additionally conditioning on
\mathcal{H}_{j-1}
within the definitions of
\widehat{\nu}_{j}(S)
and
\nu_{j,\ell}(S)
, which determines the (potentially random) weight function
w_{j}
, events
\mathcal{U}_{j}
and
\mathcal{K}_{j-1}
.
∎

On the other hand, we can derive an upper bound on
\nu_{\mathcal{K}_{j}}
similar to how we did earlier for
\nu_{\mathcal{K}}
in
Lemma

F.3
.

Lemma F.6
.

Consider any
S\subseteq\bm{X}
. Then,

\nu_{\mathcal{K}_{j}}(S)\leqslant\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{c^{-1}\log(L)}{q_{j}}\right)\cdot\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}\mathrm{Pr}\left(|\mathcal{I}_{j}|\in[2^{\ell-1},2^{\ell})\text{ and }(\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\in\mathcal{U}_{j}\cap\mathcal{K}_{j-1}\ \middle|\ |\mathcal{I}_{j}|\geqslant 1,\mathcal{H}_{j-1}\right)\cdot\nu_{j,\ell}(S).

where
q_{j}=\mathrm{Pr}\big((\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\in\mathcal{U}_{j}\cap\mathcal{K}_{j-1}\mid|\mathcal{I}_{j}|\geqslant 1,\mathcal{H}_{j-1}\big)
.

Proof.
The lemma is a direct consequence of the approach we use to show
Lemma

F.4
, with the additional conditioning on
\mathcal{H}_{j-1}
within the definitions of
\widehat{\nu}_{j}(S)
and
\nu_{j,\ell}(S)
, as well as the definition of
q_{j}
, which depends on the event
\mathcal{U}_{j}\cap\mathcal{K}_{j-1}
, which is what we choose
\mathcal{U}
as in the
j^{\text{th}}
iteration.
∎

Combining the bounds in
Lemmas

F.5
and
F.6
, we arrive at a bound on the density ratio between
\nu_{\mathcal{K}_{j}}
and
\widehat{\nu}_{j}
. In particular, we get the upper bound,

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{\nu_{\mathcal{K}_{j}}}{\widehat{\nu}_{j}}\right\|_{\infty}\leqslant\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{c^{-1}\log(L)}{\mathrm{Pr}\big((\underline{X},\bm{\eta})\in\mathcal{U}_{j}\cap\mathcal{K}_{j-1}\mid|\mathcal{I}_{j}|\geqslant 1,\mathcal{H}_{j-1}\big)}\right).

(70)

F.6
Sampling Lower Bound: Proof of
Lemma

E.6

Let
\bm{1}
denote the all
1
’s vector and
e_{i}
’s denote the standard basis vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{L}
. For parameters
p\in[0,1]
and
z\in[L]
, and consider the base distribution,

\displaystyle\mu_{p,z}(z\bm{1})=p\text{ and }\forall i\in\{0,\cdots,L-1\},\ \mu_{p,z}((L+1)e_{i})=\frac{1-p}{L}

All other sequences have probability
0
under
\mu_{p,z}
. Define
w(x)=\mathbb{I}(x\in[L+1])
. Note firstly that
p_{\mu_{p,z},w}=1
, since almost surely, any sequence drawn from this distribution contains some
j\in[L+1]
. Next observe that, by Bayes rule,
\nu_{p,z}\propto\overline{\mu}_{p,z}(\cdot)w(\cdot)
can be calculated to be the distribution,

\displaystyle\nu_{p,z}(z)=1-\nu_{p}(L+1)=\frac{p}{p+\frac{1-p}{L}}=\frac{Lp}{(L-1)p+1}\triangleq\theta_{L}(p)

Thus, returning a sample from a distribution close to
\nu
in TV distance, is equivalent to finding an unbiased estimator for the functional
\theta_{L}(p)
. Indeed, note that, consider a sampling algorithm
\texttt{Alg}(\cdot)
which draws a dataset
D
of samples from
\mu
, and uses this dataset to generate a single sample distributed according to
\widehat{\nu}
. Then, overload notation to let
\widehat{\nu}(\cdot|D)
denote the distribution over samples generated by
Alg
conditioned on
D
, we have that,

\mathrm{TV}\left(\widehat{\nu},\nu\right)=\mathrm{TV}\left(\mathbb{E}[\widehat{\nu}(\cdot|D)],\nu\right)\geqslant\left|\mathbb{E}[\widehat{\nu}(\cdot|D)](z)-\theta_{L}(p)\right|

(71)

Next, observe that
\bm{N}=(N_{j})_{j\in[L]}
, where
N_{j}
is frequency of sequences in
D
containing a
j
, is a sufficient statistic for the dataset
D
. Indeed, given
\bm{N}
, we can reconstruct a dataset
D^{\prime}
which is statistically indistinguishable from
D
, as follows. Pick a uniformly random partition of
[N]
with
2L
(potentially empty) parts, under the constraint that the number of elements in the
j^{\text{th}}
part equals
N_{j}
for each
j\in[L]
, and the number of elements in the remaining parts are uniform subject to containing
N-\sum_{j\in[L}N_{j}
in total. With such a partition, define the dataset
D^{\prime}
as allocating the indices in
[N]
belonging to the
j^{\text{th}}
part as copies of the datapoint
j\bm{1}
for
j\in[L]
, and of the remaining
L
parts, allocating the
j^{\text{th}}
one as copies of the datapoint
(L+1)e_{j}
.

With this observation, we argue that for each
j\in[L]
,
\mathbb{E}[\widehat{\nu}(\cdot|D)](j)
for any estimator can be written down as a polynomial of degree
N
in
(p_{j})_{j\in[L]}
, for any
Alg
which processes a dataset of size
N
drawn from
\mu
. This follows by Rao-blackwellization: for any sampling algorithm
Alg
, we have that
\mathbb{E}[\widehat{\nu}(\cdot|D)](j)=\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{E}[\widehat{\nu}(\cdot|D^{\prime})|\bm{N}]](j)=\mathbb{E}[\widehat{\theta}_{j}(\bm{N})]
, where we let
\widehat{\theta}_{j}(\bm{N})
denote
\mathbb{E}[\widehat{\nu}(\cdot|D^{\prime})|\bm{N}](j)
. Finally, observe
\widehat{\theta}_{j}(\bm{N})
can be written down as a polynomial:

\mathbb{E}[\widehat{\theta}_{j}(\bm{N})]=\sum_{\bm{N}^{\prime}}\widehat{\theta}_{j}(\bm{N}^{\prime})\mathrm{Pr}(\bm{N}=\bm{N}^{\prime})

(72)

since for any fixed
\bm{N}^{\prime}
,
\mathrm{Pr}(\bm{N}=\bm{N}^{\prime})
itself is a polynomial of degree
N
in
p
. Let
P_{N,j}(p,z)=\mathbb{E}[\widehat{\theta}_{j}(\bm{N})]
denote the degree-
N
polynomial corresponding to the
j^{\text{th}}
symbol, when the underlying base distribution is
\mu_{p,z}
. What remains to show is that degree
N
polynomials cannot uniformly approximate the functional
\theta_{L}(p)
. In the subsequent
Lemma

F.7
, we show that uniform error of a constant is not achievable unless
N>c\sqrt{L}
for some absolute constant
c>0
.

Lemma F.7
.

Fix
L\geqslant 2
and define for
p\in[0,1]
,
\theta_{L}(p)=\frac{Lp}{(L-1)p+1}
. Then there exist absolute constants
c,c^{\prime}>0
such that for all polynomials
P_{N}
of degree at most
N
with
N\leqslant c\sqrt{L}
, satisfying
\|P_{N}\|_{\infty}\leqslant 1
,

\|P_{N,z}(z,\cdot)-\theta_{L}(\cdot)\|_{\infty}\geqslant c^{\prime}.

Proof.
This lemma is proved in
Section

F.6.1
.
∎

Remark F.8
.

Note that the proofs of
Lemma

F.7
uses an elementary approach in polynomial approximation to argue an
\Omega(\sqrt{L})
lower bound on the number of sampling oracle queries necessary to achieve constant TV distance. Using more involved techniques, via bounding the Ditzian-Totik moduli of smoothness
[
12
,
31
]
of certain functionals involved in the analysis, we expect that to achieve constant TV error, this lower bound on the number of sampling oracle queries can be lifted to
\Omega(L/\omega(L))
for any arbitrarily slow growing function
\omega(\cdot)
. Therefore in the worst case, in order to generate a sample from
\nu
, it is essentially impossible to beat the trivial sampling strategy of drawing
X\sim\mu
, sampling
I\sim\operatorname{Unif}(\{0,\cdots,L-1\})
and returning
X_{I}
if
I\in\mathcal{I}
(repeating until termination).

The proof of the lower bound on the TV error in
Lemma

E.6
follows by combining the guarantee in
Lemma

F.7
with the bound on the TV error in
Eq.

71
and the polynomial structure of
\widehat{\nu}
from
Eq.

72
. The lower bound on the density ratio in
Lemma

E.6
is shown next. Below, we show that there always exists an index
j^{\star}\in[L]
such that
\nu(j^{\star})
must be polynomially small in
L
as long as
N
is not too large, when
\nu
is induced by the base distribution
\mu_{p_{0},j^{\star}}
for
p_{0}=\frac{1}{L}
.

Lemma F.9
.

There exists
c>0
such that if
N\leqslant cL^{1/3}
, then there exists a
j^{\star}\in[L]
such that
P_{N,j^{\star}}(p_{0},j^{\star})\leqslant L^{-1/3}
.

Proof.
This lemma is proved in
Section

F.7
.
∎

With this, we prove the lower bound on the density ratio in
Lemma

E.6
. For the index
j^{\star}\in[L]
from
Lemma

F.9
, let
\nu^{\star}=\nu_{p_{0},j^{\star}}
for
p_{0}=\frac{1}{L}
. Then, note that
\nu^{\star}(j^{\star})\geqslant\frac{1}{2}
and furthermore,
\frac{\nu^{\star}(j^{\star})}{\widehat{\nu}(j^{\star})}=\frac{\nu^{\star}(j^{\star})}{P_{N,j^{\star}}(p_{0},j^{\star})}\geqslant\frac{L^{1/4}}{2}
. This implies

\displaystyle\max_{\mu\in\bm{\mu}}\mathrm{Pr}_{X\sim\nu}\left(\frac{\nu(X)}{\widehat{\nu}(X)}\geqslant\frac{L^{1/4}}{4}\right)=\mathrm{Pr}_{X\sim\nu^{\star}}\left(\frac{\nu^{\star}(X)}{P_{N,X}(p_{0},j^{\star})}\geqslant\frac{L^{1/4}}{4}\right)\geqslant\nu^{\star}(j^{\star})\geqslant\frac{1}{2}.

F.6.1
Proof of
Lemma

F.7

Let
P_{N}
be any real polynomial of degree at most
N
. Our objective is to lower bound the uniform approximation error,
\inf_{P_{N}\in\mathcal{P}_{N}}\|P_{N}-\theta_{L}\|_{\infty}
, where
\mathcal{P}_{N}
is the class of algebraic polynomials of degree at most
N
which are bounded in the range
[0,1]
. Applying Markov brothers’ inequality for the first derivative: there exists an absolute constant
C_{\mathrm{Markov}}>0
such that for every real polynomial
Q
of degree at most
N
,

\|Q^{\prime}(x)\|_{\infty}\leqslant C_{\mathrm{Markov}}N^{2}\|Q(x)\|_{\infty}.

Applying this to
P_{N}
and using
\|P_{N}\|_{\infty}\leqslant 1
gives,

\|P_{N}^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\leqslant C_{\mathrm{Markov}}N^{2}

(73)

Next, we bound the curvature of the target function
\theta_{L}(p)
around
p_{0}=1/L
. Let
p_{0}\triangleq 1/L
and define intervals
I_{1}\triangleq\big[p_{0}-\frac{1}{10L},\ p_{0}+\frac{1}{10L}\big]
and
I_{2}\triangleq\big[p_{0}-\frac{1}{20L},\ p_{0}+\frac{1}{20L}\big]
. By explicit calculation, observe that,
\theta_{L}^{\prime}(p)=\frac{L}{(1+(L-1)p)^{2}}
. This implies that for all
p\in I_{2}
, there exists an absolute constant
c_{1}>0
such that
|\theta_{L}^{\prime}(p)|\geqslant c_{1}L
.

Finally we integrate the magnitude of
\theta_{L}^{\prime\prime}(\cdot)
to argue that
\theta_{L}
and
P_{N}
cannot uniformly be close to each other. By the mean value theorem, for each fixed
x
and step
\delta>0
, there exists a
\xi\in[x,x+\delta]
such that,

\displaystyle\Delta_{\delta}g(x)=g(x+\delta)-g(x)=g^{\prime}(\xi)\delta

for any function
g
such that
g^{\prime}
is differentiable and continuous. With the choice of
\delta=\frac{1}{10L}
, observe that for any
x\in I_{1}
, the points
x
and
x+\delta
belong to
I_{2}
. Thus, applying the above presentation to
g=\theta_{L}
, we obtain for each
x\in I_{1}
,

\displaystyle|\Delta_{\delta}\theta_{L}(x)|=|\theta_{L}^{\prime}(\xi(x))\delta|\geqslant c_{1}L\delta=c_{1}L\cdot\frac{1}{10L}\triangleq c_{2}>0,

(74)

Similarly, applying the mean value theorem to
g=P_{N}
and using
Eq.

73
, for each
x\in I_{1}
,

|\Delta_{\delta}P_{N}(x)|\leqslant\|P^{\prime}_{N}\|_{\infty}\cdot\delta\leqslant C_{\mathrm{Markov}}N^{2}\cdot\frac{1}{10L}=\frac{C_{\mathrm{Markov}}}{10}\cdot\frac{N^{2}}{L}\leqslant\frac{c_{2}}{2}.

(75)

where the last inequality assumes that
N\leqslant c\sqrt{L}
for sufficiently small
c>0
.
Eqs.

74
and
75
in conjunction establish a gap in the first-order differences between
P_{N}
and
\theta_{L}
. Together these can be used to bound the uniform approximation error between
P_{N}
and
\theta_{L}
. Indeed, observe that,

\displaystyle\frac{c_{2}}{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant|\Delta_{\delta}P_{N}(x)-\Delta_{\delta}\theta_{L}(x)|

\displaystyle=\bigl|(P_{N}(x+\delta)-\theta_{L}(x+\delta))-(P_{N}(x)-\theta_{L}(x))\bigr|

\displaystyle\leqslant|P_{N}(x+\delta)-\theta_{L}(x+\delta)|+|P_{N}(x)-\theta_{L}(x)|

\displaystyle\leqslant 2\|P_{N}-\theta_{L}\|_{\infty},

This implies that we can lower bound the uniform approximation error by
c^{\prime}=\frac{c_{2}}{4}
.

F.7
Proof of
Lemma

F.9

Recall that
P_{N,j}(p,z)=\mathbb{E}[\widehat{\theta}_{j}(\bm{N})]
is the polynomial in
p
capturing the probability that
\widehat{\nu}
places on
j
under the base distribution
\mu_{p,z}
. First, observe that when
p=0
,
\mu_{p,z}
is the same across all values of
z\in[L]
, which implies that
P_{N,j}(p,z)
, which is a measurable function of a dataset drawn from
\mu_{p,z}
must also satisfy the invariance of
P_{N,j}(0,z)
across
z\in[L]
. This implies,

\min_{j\in[L]}\max_{z\in[L]}p_{N,j}(0,z)\leqslant\frac{1}{L}

(76)

Let
j^{\star}\in[L]
denote the index of any minimizer of
\max_{z\in[L]}p_{N,j}(0,z)
. Next, we show that when
N\leqslant cL^{1/4}
, with
p_{0}=\frac{1}{L}
,
\max\big\{P_{N}(z,x):x\in[0,p_{0}]\big\}\leqslant\frac{c_{3}}{L}
for some constant
c_{3}>0
. Formally, by an application of Markov brothers’ inequality,

\max_{z\in[L]}|P_{N,j^{\star}}(p_{0},z)-P_{N,j^{\star}}(0,z)|\leqslant p_{0}\cdot\|P_{N,j^{\star}}^{\prime}(z,\cdot)\|_{\infty}\leqslant\frac{C_{\mathrm{Markov}}}{10}\cdot\frac{N^{2}}{L}\leqslant\frac{1}{2L^{1/3}}

if
N\leqslant c_{3}L^{1/3}
for some constant
c_{3}>0
. Combining with
Eq.

76
gives the bound,
\max_{z\in[L]}P_{N,j^{\star}}(p_{0},z)\leqslant L^{-1/3}
. Replacing the maximum over
z\in[L]
by
z\leftarrow j^{\star}
completes the proof.

F.8
Improved
T
Dependency when Online Learning is Possible: Proof of
Theorem

6.4

For each instance
{\bm{x}}=(s_{0},\bm{w}_{1:T})
in the dataset,
\texttt{Alg}_{\mathrm{on}}
predicts states
s_{1},\cdots,s_{T}
. The binary search procedure described in
11
to
21
of
Algorithm

10
satisfies the property that if the while loop is broken out of, the learner must have found a time
t
such that
s_{t}\neq s_{t}^{\star}
, but
s_{t-1}=s^{\star}_{t-1}
. If the while loop is never broken out of, then by
Lemma

F.10
, the prediction of
\texttt{Alg}_{\mathrm{on}}
(which is a function of the prior instances and label observations) satisfies
s_{T}=s_{T}^{\star}
. On the other hand, each instance where the while loop is broken out of gives an
(s,w)
tuple such that the prediction
s_{+}
of
\texttt{Alg}_{\mathrm{on}}
satisfies
\pi^{\star}(s,w)\neq s_{+}
but where
s
itself was predicted correctly. This is a “true” mistake, where the prediction was not incorrect because the prior state itself was predicted incorrectly. Since
\texttt{Alg}_{\mathrm{on}}
makes at most
M^{\star}(\texttt{Alg}_{\mathrm{on}})
(true) mistakes, we have that,

\displaystyle\sum_{m=1}^{n}\mathbb{I}(y_{T}^{m}\neq\pi^{\star}_{T}({\bm{x}}^{m}))\leqslant M^{\star}(\texttt{Alg}_{\mathrm{on}}).

where
y_{T}^{m}\sim\widehat{\pi}^{m}({\bm{x}}^{m})
. Taking an expectation on both sides and noting the definition of
\widehat{\pi}
returned by
Algorithm

10
, by an online-to-batch argument we get,

\mathbb{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim\rho,y_{T}\sim\widehat{\pi}(\cdot|{\bm{x}})}\big[\mathbb{I}\big(y_{T}\neq\pi^{\star}_{T}({\bm{x}})\big)\big]=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{m=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{I}(\widehat{\pi}^{m}({\bm{x}}^{m})\neq\pi^{\star}_{T}({\bm{x}}^{m})\big]\leqslant\frac{M^{\star}(\texttt{Alg}_{\mathrm{on}})}{n}.

This shows that
n=\mathcal{O}(M^{\star}(\texttt{Alg}_{\mathrm{on}})/\varepsilon)
samples suffices to get expected error
\varepsilon
. For each of the
n
instances in the input dataset,
\texttt{iCoT}(\cdot)
is queried at at most
2\log_{2}(T)
timepoints. This implies the relation between the query complexity and sample complexity.

Lemma F.10
.

If no
t\in[T]
exists such that
s_{t}\neq s_{t}^{\star}
, but
s_{t-1}=s^{\star}_{t-1}
, then
s_{T}=s_{T}^{\star}
.

Proof.
Start from
t=1
. Since
s_{0}=s_{0}^{\star}
by definition, the condition in the statement of the lemma implies that
s_{1}=s_{1}^{\star}
. Repeating this argument inductively, this implies that for all
t\leqslant T
,
s_{t}=s_{t}^{\star}
, which proves the claim.
∎

Lemma F.11
.

For a class of next-state/token predictors
\Pi
from
S\times\Sigma\to S
,
\mathrm{Ldim}(\Pi)\leqslant\mathrm{Ndim}(\Pi)\log(|S| |\Sigma|)
.

Proof.
By a counting argument
\mathrm{Ldim}(\Pi)\leqslant\log_{2}|\Pi|
, since a complete mistake tree of depth
m
requires
2^{m}
distinct hypotheses, one for each root-to-leaf path. On the other hand, viewing
\Pi
as a multiclass prediction class from
X=S\times\Sigma
to
Y=S
, the multiclass Sauer–Shelah lemma for Natarajan dimension implies
[
37
,
11
]

|\Pi|\leqslant\sum_{i=0}^{d}\binom{|X|}{i}|Y|^{2i}\leqslant\left(\frac{e|X||Y|^{2}}{d}\right)^{d}.

Simplifying completes the proof.
∎

Algorithm 10

\texttt{AutoLearn}_{\mathrm{on}}(D_{\mathrm{input}}\|T)

1:

# Learning semiautomata via curriculum: reduction to online learning

2:

Input:
Class of semiautomaton transitions
\Pi
over state space
S
and alphabet
\Sigma
,

3:

Number of steps of semiautomaton simulation,
T
,

4:

Dataset of instances
D_{\mathrm{input}}=\{{\bm{x}}^{i}=(s^{i}_{0},\bm{w}^{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
.

5:

Online learning algorithm
\texttt{Alg}_{\mathrm{on}}
for
\Pi
(
Definition

6.2
)

6:

Instantiate:
Let
\texttt{Alg}_{\mathrm{on}}
’s initial model be
\widehat{\pi}^{1}:S\times\Sigma\to S
and let
m\leftarrow 1
.

7:

for
instance
{\bm{x}}^{m}=(s_{0},\bm{w}_{1:T})\in D_{\mathrm{input}}

do

8:

for

t=1,\cdots,T

do

9:

Let
s_{t}\leftarrow\widehat{\pi}^{m}(s_{t-1},w_{t})
.

10:

Instantiate
T_{\mathrm{min}}\leftarrow 1
,
T_{\mathrm{max}}\leftarrow T
.

11:

while

T_{\mathrm{max}}\geqslant T_{\mathrm{min}}

do

12:

t\leftarrow T_{\mathrm{min}}+\big\lfloor\frac{T_{\mathrm{max}}-T_{\mathrm{min}}}{2}\big\rfloor

13:

Query
s_{t}^{\star}\leftarrow\pi^{\star}_{t}({\bm{x}})
and
s_{t-1}^{\star}\leftarrow\pi^{\star}_{t-1}({\bm{x}})
.

14:

if

s_{t}\neq s_{t}^{\star}
but
s_{t-1}=s_{t-1}^{\star}

then

15:

Submit input
(s_{t-1},w_{t})
to
\texttt{Alg}_{\mathrm{on}}
, observe its prediction (
s_{t}
) and

16:

reveal the correct label
s_{t}^{\star}

\blacktriangleright

\texttt{Alg}_{\mathrm{on}}
updates itself on the mistake

17:

break

18:

else

if

s_{t}=s_{t}^{\star}
and
s_{t-1}=s_{t-1}^{\star}

then

19:

T_{\mathrm{min}}=t+1

20:

else

if

s_{t-1}\neq s_{t-1}^{\star}

then

21:

T_{\mathrm{max}}=t-1

22:

Denote the (potentially updated) model of
\texttt{Alg}_{\mathrm{on}}
as
\widehat{\pi}^{m+1}:S\times\Sigma\to S

23:

Update
m\leftarrow m+1
.

24:

Return:

\operatorname{Unif}\big(\{\widehat{\pi}^{m}\}_{m=1}^{n}\big)
.

F.9
Composition for Stochastic Models: Proof of
Proposition

6.1

Let
S^{\star}_{t}\sim\pi^{\star}_{t}(\cdot|{\bm{x}})
for
{\bm{x}}\sim\rho
. For
i=0,\cdots,L-1
, let
\widehat{S}_{\tau(i+1)}\sim\widehat{\pi}(\cdot|{\bm{x}})
for
{\bm{x}}=(S_{\tau i}^{\star},\bm{w}_{\tau i+1:\tau(i+1)})\sim\rho_{\tau i+1:\tau(i+1)}
. Let
S^{\star}_{0}=\widehat{S}_{0}=S_{0}
(distributed according to the initial state distribution within
\rho
). Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim\overline{\rho}}\big[D_{\mathrm{KL}}\big(\pi^{\star}_{\tau}(\cdot|{\bm{x}})\,\big\|\,\widehat{\pi}(\cdot|{\bm{x}})\big)\big]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{L}\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}\mathbb{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim\rho_{\tau i+1:\tau(i+1)}}\big[D_{\mathrm{KL}}\big(S^{\star}_{\tau(i+1)}\,\big|\,{\bm{x}}\,\big\|\,\widehat{S}_{\tau(i+1)}\,\big|\,{\bm{x}}\big)\big]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{L}\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}\mathbb{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim\rho}\big[D_{\mathrm{KL}}\big(S^{\star}_{\tau(i+1)}\,\big|\,{\bm{x}},S^{\star}_{0},\cdots,S^{\star}_{\tau(i-1)},S^{\star}_{\tau i}\,\big\|\,\widehat{S}_{\tau(i+1)}\,\big|\,{\bm{x}},S^{\star}_{0},\cdots,S^{\star}_{\tau(i-1)},S^{\star}_{\tau i}\big)\big]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{L}\mathbb{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim\rho}\big[D_{\mathrm{KL}}\big(S^{\star}_{0},S^{\star}_{\tau},\cdots,S^{\star}_{T}\,\big|\,{\bm{x}}\,\big\|\,\widehat{S}_{0},\widehat{S}_{\tau},\cdots,\widehat{S}_{T}\,\big|\,{\bm{x}}\big)\big]

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{1}{L}\mathbb{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim\rho}\big[D_{\mathrm{KL}}\big(S^{\star}_{T}\,\big|\,{\bm{x}}\,\big\|\,\widehat{S}_{T}\,\big|\,{\bm{x}}\big)\big]

The proof concludes by noting that
\widehat{S}_{T}|{\bm{x}}
is distributed according to
\widehat{\pi}^{\circ L}(\cdot|{\bm{x}})
.
